B. Purpose and Organization of Filing 
17 
Q. 
What is the purpose of the Panel’s direct testimony in this 
18 
proceeding? 
19 
A. 
The purpose of the Panel’s direct testimony is to present 
20 
and support the Company’s electric T&D capital budget and 
21 
major plant additions that were identified through the 
22 
Company’s planning process and that will be placed into 
23 
plant in service during the period January 1, 2022 through 
24 


--- Page 12 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
 
Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
 
 
- 12 - 
December 31, 2022 (“Rate Year” or “RY1”). The Panel also 
1 
will address the Company’s operation and maintenance 
2 
(“O&M”) requirements during the Rate Year. As explained 
3 
more fully in the direct testimony of the Company’s 
4 
Accounting Panel, the Company is not proposing a multiyear 
5 
rate plan in this electric base rate filing. However, in 
6 
addition to providing projections for the Rate Year, the 
7 
Company has included forecasted financial information for 
8 
the two annual calendar years beyond the Rate Year (i.e., 
9 
2023 and 2024), which we will refer to as “RY2” and “RY3”, 
10 
respectively, for ease of reference.  
11 
 
The Panel also discusses the Company’s activities and 
12 
initiatives contributing to the realization of the State’s 
13 
vision to develop a cleaner, more resilient, and more 
14 
affordable energy system. This includes support for the 
15 
goals identified in the CLCPA, the adoption of 
16 
electrification technologies such as electric vehicles 
17 
(“EVs”) and heat pumps, the integration of DERs, and 
18 
continued progress toward becoming the DSP provider. 
19

C. Major Objectives 
1 
Q. 
Please identify the major objectives that the Company seeks 
2 
to accomplish by this electric rate increase request. 
3 
A. 
The Company selected the electric projects and programs for 
4 
which it seeks funding based on three major objectives: 
5 
supporting the State’s climate goals and the CLCPA; 
6 
increasing storm hardening and resilience; and maintaining 
7 
design standards and upgrading equipment. As discussed 
8 
below, the Company is pursuing these objectives in a cost-
9 
conscious manner. Finally, an ancillary benefit of these 
10 
programs and projects is that they will result in increased 
11 
employment.  
12 
i. 
State Climate Goals and CLCPA 
13 
Q. 
Please elaborate on the Company’s objective of supporting 
14 
the State’s climate goals and the CLCPA. 
15 
A. 
O&R is fully committed to supporting the State of New York 
16 
in realizing its vision to develop a cleaner, more 
17 
resilient energy system. This includes ongoing 
18 
participation in the Commission’s REV Proceeding and 
19 
efforts to assist the State in reaching decarbonization 
20 
targets and supporting policies designed to realize the 
21 
State’s goal of net zero greenhouse gas (“GHG”) emissions 
22 
over the next decade as laid out in the CLCPA. 
23 
Q.  
Please describe the CLCPA. 
24 


--- Page 16 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
 
Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
 
 
- 16 - 
A.  
The CLCPA advances decarbonization through large-scale 
1 
resources, such as offshore wind and utility-scale solar. 
2 
The CLCPA’s targets include the following: 
3 
 
40 percent emissions reductions in absolute terms from 
4 
1990 levels by 2030, 85 percent emissions reductions by 
5 
2050; 
6 
 
70 percent renewable energy by 2030, 100 percent of the 
7 
State’s electric supply GHG emissions free by 2040; 
8 
 
6,000 MW of solar energy by 2025; 
9 
 
3,000 MW of energy storage capacity by 2030; 
10 
 
9,000 MW of offshore wind by 2035; and 
11 
 
Reduced energy consumption by 185 trillion British 
12 
thermal units (“BTUs”) from the State’s 2025 forecast. 
13 
In 2020, the State passed the Accelerated Renewable Energy 
14 
Growth and Community Benefit Act3 (“Benefit Act”) expanding 
15 
on the CLCPA by developing State organizations and 
16 
processes to expedite the development of renewable energy 
17 
in New York, particularly through increased transmission 
18 
buildout. In 2020, the Commission issued its Order on 
19 
Transmission Planning Pursuant to the Accelerated Renewable 
20 
 
3 Accelerated Renewable Energy Growth and Community Benefit Act. Full 
text of the legislation is available online. See 
https://www.budget.ny.gov/pubs/archive/fy21/exec/30day/ted-artvii-
newpart-jjj.pdf. 


--- Page 17 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
 
Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
 
 
- 17 - 
Energy Growth and Community Benefit Act4 accelerating the 
1 
timeline for key T&D upgrades to accommodate large-scale 
2 
renewables. 
3 
Q. 
In what ways does O&R support the CLCPA and its associated 
4 
efforts through its forecasting and planning process? 
5 
A. 
In its system planning process, the Company plans for 
6 
distribution and local transmission upgrades necessary or 
7 
appropriate for the timely achievement of the CLCPA’s 
8 
climate goals. In support of the Order on Transmission 
9 
Planning, the Company recently completed a Utility Study 
10 
evaluating transmission capabilities. This Utility Study is 
11 
needed to support the CLCPA’s goal of deriving 70 percent 
12 
of the State’s electric energy needs from renewable sources 
13 
by 2030. In the Utility Study, O&R indicated that available 
14 
headroom on the Company’s transmission system will decrease 
15 
in 2030 due to the addition of photovoltaic (“PV”) and 
16 
energy storage (“ES”) projects in the New York Independent 
17 
System Operator (“NYISO”) queue. If left unaddressed, 
18 
reliability concerns would require the Company to curtail 
19 
renewable generation connected to these lines under peak 
20 
 
4 Case 20-E-0197, Proceeding on Motion of the Commission to Implement 
Transmission Planning Pursuant to the Accelerated Renewable Energy 
Growth and Community Benefit Act, Order on Transmission Planning 
Pursuant to the Accelerated Renewable Energy Growth and Community 
Benefit Act (issued May 14, 2020) (“Order on Transmission Planning”). 


--- Page 18 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
 
Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
 
 
- 18 - 
load conditions. In the Utility Study, O&R identified 
1 
multi-value T&D projects that can help increase the 
2 
available headroom on the system, thereby unbottling 
3 
renewable generation. In this direct testimony, the Panel 
4 
describes those projects that the Company will commence 
5 
and/or complete during 2022 - 2024. These include three 
6 
such projects that will be completed during 2022 - 2024 
7 
with total expenditures of $34.6 million. The Company will 
8 
also initiate three additional multi-value T&D projects 
9 
identified in the Utility Study to be completed after 2024.  
10 
The Company currently forecasts that it will expend $20.1 
11 
million on these three additional projects during 2022 – 
12 
2024.  
13 
O&R also continues to look for opportunities to integrate 
14 
DER where suitable and appropriate through NWA projects. In 
15 
addition to the typical NWA projects, O&R has identified 
16 
other opportunities to deploy ES (and DER more broadly) 
17 
through a hybrid-NWA concept, and in conjunction with 
18 
traditional utility infrastructure investment. If an NWA 
19 
does not pass NWA Suitability Criteria, O&R will explore 
20 
opportunities to combine DER with the traditional utility 
21 
infrastructure investment solution.  
22 
Q. 
What technology investments is O&R planning to support 
23 
achieving the CLCPA’s goals? 
24 


--- Page 19 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
 
Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
 
 
- 19 - 
A. 
The Company is planning investments in a number of grid 
1 
modernization technologies necessary to balance reliability 
2 
with optimal dispatch of DER, increased electrification, 
3 
and the integration of large-scale renewables with the 
4 
transmission system required to meet the goals defined in 
5 
the CLCPA. These projects entail the continued 
6 
implementation of an Advanced Distribution Management 
7 
System (“ADMS”), to include advanced applications and a 
8

fault isolation. These autoloop schemes reduce customer

II. 
Electric Delivery System Planning Process 
19 
Q. 
Please describe the purpose of the Company’s Electric 
20 
Delivery System Planning Process. 
21 
A. 
The purpose of the Company’s electric delivery system 
22 
planning process is to maintain and enhance the safety and 
23 


--- Page 28 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
 
Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
 
 
- 28 - 
reliability of the Company’s T&D system while maintaining 
1 
system performance within defined and acceptable design and 
2 
operating risk tolerances.   
3 
Q. 
What are the primary deliverables and high-level steps of 
4 
the Company’s planning process? 
5 
A. 
The Company’s planning process evaluates the electric 
6 
delivery system over a specified future forecast period and 
7 
identifies system needs and solutions. Each year, the 
8 
Company produces a ten-year detailed weather-adjusted 
9 
forecast at the system, substation bank, and circuit 
10 
levels. This forecast includes the impact of base load 
11 
growth, new business projects, and several load modifiers 
12 
(such as EVs, Demand Response (“DR”), Energy Efficiency 
13 
(“EE”), PV, Batteries, NWAs). The Company then conducts 
14 
operating reviews of its assets through that forecast 
15 
period and applies its design standards and risk-assessment 
16 
methodology to the results to identify current and future 
17 
operating risks and potential corrective solutions. The 
18 
Company also investigates if major capital infrastructure 
19 
investments can be substantially deferred, reprioritized, 
20 
or even eliminated by alternative and less costly 
21 
traditional infrastructure investments, targeted non-
22 
traditional measures and alternative solutions, such as 
23 
DER, Distributed Generation (“DG”), DR, EE, or a 
24 


--- Page 29 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
 
Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
 
 
- 29 - 
combination thereof. Alternative traditional solutions 
1 
could include: (1) constructing lower cost distribution 
2 
projects to defer major upgrades or new construction, (2) 
3 
using new technologies and/or DA/smart grid asset 
4 
deployment for improved asset utilization, and (3) 
5 
reprioritizing and accelerating the construction of lower 
6 
cost distribution and substation investments. As further 
7 
described later in this testimony, the Company is 
8 
implementing an integrated planning approach to facilitate 
9 
consideration of alternatives as part of its planning and 
10 
capital budgeting process. The Company also reassesses 
11 
previously identified needs and project solutions that have 
12 
not yet been initiated to confirm the need(s) and timing of 
13 
the solution(s). As part of this reassessment, the Company 
14 
reviews available data such as: updated load forecasts, 
15 
load modifier forecasts (which include DERs), asset 
16 
condition, system reliability, and the system’s load 
17 
serving capability under normal and specific contingency 
18 
conditions.  
19 
Q. 
When did the Company most recently complete its planning 
20 
process? 
21 
A. 
In the spring of 2020. 
22 


--- Page 30 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
 
Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
 
 
- 30 - 
A. Integrated Planning 
1 
Q. 
Please describe the role the Company’s design standards 
2 
play in the T&D system planning process. 
3 
A. 
The Company’s electric system planning design standards 
4 
provide guidance in assessing operating risk, identifying 
5 
system needs, and prioritizing electrical infrastructure 
6 
projects. The design standards balance the costs of 
7 
infrastructure investment against the benefits of 
8 
mitigating the risk of significant outage events as defined 
9 
by the magnitude of the outage and duration of the event. 
10 
The electric design standards provide criteria to evaluate 
11 
whether electric facilities are, or will be, operating 
12 
outside of acceptable tolerances for equipment loading, 
13 
operating parameters, and customer outage exposure. For the 
14 
Company, acceptability is measured by meeting Company 
15 
criteria for both the amount of load or number of customers 
16 
impacted, and the reliability impact based on anticipated 
17 
customer hours of outage duration. These standards are 
18 
foundational to the capital planning process, and critical 
19 
for both short-term and long-term planning, as they provide 
20 
a process by which future risk mitigation investments are 
21 
identified and prioritized. 
22 
Q. 
In what ways is the Company’s planning process evolving to 
23 
incorporate an integrated planning approach? 
24 


--- Page 31 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
 
Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
 
 
- 31 - 
A. 
The Company has been expanding the capabilities of the 
1 
current distribution modeling software to support hosting 
2 
capacity and DER system impact studies. The next step is to 
3 
incorporate field data from DSCADA devices and AMI into the 
4 
distribution system model. Robust data is available down to 
5 
the circuit level for the majority of the circuits in the 
6 
O&R service territory. This data along with customer meter 
7 
data has been used effectively to model the operation of 
8 
the electric delivery system. Incorporating more granular 
9 
data down to the field device and interval customer meter 
10 
data will allow system planners to improve and refine 
11 
system operating scenarios significantly to perform more 
12 
accurate planning studies. The Company continues to work 
13 
with industry experts, developers, and the other New York 
14 
electric distribution companies (“EDCs”) to determine best 
15 
practices and refine the approach to advanced forecasting 
16 
and integrated planning processes. 
17 
The Company is continuing to develop more detailed and 
18 
location-based information about how load and load 
19 
modifiers will evolve and impact local system reliability 
20 
and system investment requirements. The Company’s 
21 
commitment to continued enhancement of this information 
22 
will improve O&R’s ability to perform its DSP 
23 
responsibilities and safely and reliably integrate more 
24 


--- Page 32 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC.

- 32 - 
utility-scale renewables onto its electric delivery system 
1 
in alignment with the CLCPA’s goals.

3

The CLCPA directs the utilities to implement a transparent 
5

6

7

8

14

16 
Study is based upon projected system conditions for year 
17 
2030. Undertaken by the Technical Working Group Sub-group 
18 
of the Utility Investment T&D Working Group, the Utility 
19 
Study evaluates transmission capabilities that will be 
20 
needed to support the CLCPA’s goal of deriving 70 percent 
21 
of the State’s electric energy needs from renewable sources 
22 
by 2030. In accordance with the Order on Transmission 
23 


--- Page 33 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC.

Planning, the Utility Study took into account the following 
1 
considerations: 
2 
1. Evaluate the local T&D system of the individual New 
3 
York EDCs service territories, to understand where 
4 
capacity “headroom” exists on the existing system; 
5 
2. Identify existing constraints or bottlenecks that 
6 
limit energy deliverability; 
7 
3. Consider synergies with traditional capital 
8 
expenditure projects – including upgrading 
9 
infrastructure, reliability, resilience, market 
10 
efficiency, and operational flexibility; 
11 
4. Identify least cost upgrade projects to increase the 
12 
capacity of the existing system; 
13

14 
accompany or complement traditional upgrades; 
15 
6. Identify potential new projects which would increase 
16 
capacity on the local T&D system to allow for 
17 
interconnection of new renewable generation resources; 
18 
and 
19 
7. Identify the possibility of fossil generation 
20 
retirements and the impacts and potential availability 
21 
of those interconnection points.

Q. 
Does Orange and Rockland have a formalized process to 
13 
prioritize its projects? 
14 
A. 
Yes. The Company employs a two-step process for 
15 
prioritizing major projects in its overall electric capital 
16 
investment plan. The first step is a prioritization 
17 
conducted by the Electrical Engineering organization within 
18 
the planning process. The second step is a prioritization 
19 
against other Company projects through a corporate-wide 
20 
optimization process and methodology. 
21 
In the first step, Electrical Engineering prioritizes 
22 
projects based on key drivers that include load, existing 
23 
condition towards satisfying design standards, condition of 
24 


--- Page 36 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
 
Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
 
 
- 36 - 
equipment, relationship with respect to sequential project 
1 
needs, reliability, customer needs, and construction window 
2 
availability. Electrical Engineering also considers other 
3 
factors, such as operating conditions, safety, system 
4 
losses, improving DER hosting capacity, and voltage 
5 
improvements. Projects that provide the most value and risk 
6 
reduction are prioritized relative to other projects for 
7 
the entire forecasted planning period. These results are 
8 
used in the development of the Company’s five–year budget. 
9 
In the second step, the Company considers and prioritizes 
10 
the overall capital budget for a one-year future-looking 
11 
forecast period. The Company then analyzes its corporate 
12 
portfolio using its strategic alignment optimization 
13 
methodology and process. During this optimization process 
14 
capital projects seeking funds for the upcoming budget year 
15 
are ranked after they are reviewed using a series of 
16 
Corporate key drivers. Projects are ranked relative to each 
17 
other based on their attributes with consideration towards 
18 
the following objectives (in no particular order): 
19 
 
Improve Public and Employee Safety; 
20 
 
Reduce Cost to Customers; 
21 
 
Provide Reliable Service; 
22 
 
Improve Customer Experience; 
23 


--- Page 37 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
 
Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
 
 
- 37 - 
 
Enhance External Relationships; 
1 
 
Reduce and Manage Risk; 
2 
 
Strengthen and Develop Employees; 
3 
 
Strengthen Company Processes; and 
4 
 
Sustain Environmental Excellence. 
5 
The initial portfolio prioritization is selected by a team 
6 
comprised of department managers and directors from all 
7 
areas of the Company. The overall capital portfolio is then 
8

9 
portfolio is then approved by the O&R Capital Governance 
10 
Committee.

- 40 - 
Q. 
What are the benefits of using an NWA suitability matrix? 
1 
A. 
The NWA suitability matrix provides greater clarity, 
2 
certainty, and long-term visibility to the market. It 
3 
promotes an efficient allocation of time and resources for 
4 
both developers and utilities. The NWA suitability matrix 
5 
focuses on three criteria: project type, timeline, and 
6 
cost. These criteria identify projects that are best suited 
7 
for competitive procurement of an NWA, giving developers 
8 
the greatest opportunity to compete, and providing the 
9 
greatest opportunity for the success of the process.  
10 
Q. 
What type of projects are best suited for replacement or 
11 
deferral by an NWA? 
12 
A. 
The nature and characteristics of electric delivery system 
13 
needs are a primary influence on whether a given project is 
14 
viable and suitable for NWA consideration. As part of the 
15 
project evaluation with respect to the suitability matrix 
16 
criteria, the Company considers numerous factors when 
17 
determining whether a proposed solution, or portfolio of 
18 
solutions, has the characteristics that would effectively 
19 
satisfy the system need. These factors include the lead 
20 
time with respect to the system need date, the economics of 
21 
the project, and any additional positive reliability 
22 
impacts of the traditional project beyond the identified 
23 
planning need. Based on an assessment of these three 
24 


--- Page 41 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
 
Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
 
 
- 41 - 
criteria, load relief or capacity projects, as well as some 
1 
types of reliability projects, are expected to be the best 
2 
candidates for NWAs in the near term.  
3 
Q. 
What do you mean by a load relief or capacity project? 
4 
A. 
Load relief or capacity projects are projects that address 
5 
T&D capacity needs at some forecasted future period in 
6 
order to meet Orange and Rockland’s planning design 
7 
standards resulting from projected increases in load; 
8 
typically, during hours of peak demand and/or 
9 
contingencies. 
10 
Q. 
Why are such projects best suited for replacement or 
11 
deferral by an NWA? 
12 
A. 
There are several reasons. First, the grid services 
13 
provided by installed DER are more likely to align with 
14 
traditional load relief and reliability solutions. Second, 
15 
these types of projects will be required to be identified 
16 
far enough in advance to provide sufficient lead time for 
17 
an NWA solicitation. Finally, the scale of investment for 
18 
the project can influence the likelihood of an NWA being 
19 
cost-effective.7  
20 
 
7 See Case 16-M-0411, In the Matter of Distributed System Implementation 
Plans, Joint Utilities’ Supplemental Information on the Non-Wires 
Alternatives Identification and Sourcing Process and Notification 
Practices, filed May 8, 2017. 


--- Page 42 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
 
Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
 
 
- 42 - 
Q. 
Please describe the Timeline Suitability Criterion. 
1 
A. 
Timeline Suitability addresses whether there is sufficient 
2 
time to conduct an NWA solicitation and successfully 
3 
implement the chosen solution. The Company must implement 
4 
the chosen NWA before the required trigger date to commit 
5 
significant funds and resources toward meeting the required 
6 
traditional T&D project in-service date. Timelines vary 
7 
depending on factors such as project size, complexity, and 
8 
customer demographics. Similarly, the traditional utility 
9 
project required in-service date greatly influences whether 
10 
there is sufficient time to conduct and implement an NWA 
11 
solicitation.  
12 
Q. 
Please describe the Cost Suitability criterion. 
13 
A. 
Cost Suitability assesses the potential for an NWA solution 
14 
to be more cost-effective at meeting customers’ needs than 
15 
a traditional infrastructure solution. The cost suitability 
16 
criterion sets a threshold above which NWA solutions are 
17 
more likely to be cost-competitive with traditional 
18 
solutions. Orange and Rockland established a cost floor for 
19 
small projects (under $1 million) at $450K based on 
20 
historical averages of previously completed capital 
21 
projects. For large projects ($1 million and over), no cost 
22 
floor is assigned. 
23 


--- Page 43 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
 
Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
 
 
- 43 - 
Q. 
What type of projects are generally not suitable candidates 
1 
for replacement or deferral by an NWA?  
2 
A. 
Typically, projects that (1) are often driven by new or 
3 
expanding customer load, (2) involve high-risk 
4 
circumstances, (3) address regulatory compliance 
5 
requirements (such as those imposed by the NERC, Federal 
6 
Energy Regulatory Commission (“FERC”), or NYISO), (5) 
7 
address safety or operational issues, or (6) are required 
8 
to upgrade equipment, are not good candidates for 
9 
replacement or deferral by an NWA.

19 
solutions.

4 
B. Advanced Forecasting 
5 
Q. 
Please describe Orange and Rockland’s current load 
6 
forecasting and risk assessment processes. 
7 
A. 
Each year, the Company forecasts overall system load and 
8 
the projected summer peak loads for each transmission 
9 
facility, individual substation and station transformer 
10 
bank, and distribution circuit as part of its ten-year 
11 
forecast. This process begins immediately after summer as 
12 
substation and circuit data is collected, analyzed, and 
13 
cleaned (from the impact of switching, outages, or weather-
14 
related impacts). After outliers are removed, mathematical 
15 
regression models consider all summer weekday/non-holiday 
16 
substation transformer load data along with the 
17 
corresponding daily temperature variable to determine 
18 
weather-normalized peak loads for each bank.  
19 
The Company considers the impact of load modifiers, which 
20 
include PV, EVs, DER, DG, and other demand-side management 
21 
(“DSM”) measures, such as EE and demand management (“EEDM”) 
22 
programs and voluntary or Company-administered load 
23 
reduction programs. The Company then uses the forecasted 
24 


--- Page 50 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
 
Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
 
 
- 50 - 
loads to perform operating reviews on each of its major 
1 
assets. These reviews cover transmission lines and banks 
2 
down through their distribution circuits, for both normal 
3 
and contingency operating conditions. The Company then 
4 
evaluates the results of the contingency analysis against 
5 
the Company’s design standards to assess if the electric 
6 
facilities are, or will be, operating outside of acceptable 
7 
design and/or risk tolerances. If any of the assets do not 
8 
operate within their respective design standards either 
9 
currently, or at some point during the future forecast 
10 
period, the Company identifies a need, determines a 
11 
solution, and develops a schedule to implement the solution 
12 
consistent with its priority, as part of its capital budget 
13 
development process. This process includes evaluating 
14 
traditional solutions and NWAs. The Company employs 
15 
additional screening tests to determine if or where 
16 
targeted load reduction may be used as a potential 
17 
solution. 
18 
Q. 
Please describe the importance of the forecasting process 
19 
for integrated planning and helping to meet the CLCPA’s 
20 
goals. 
21 
A. 
Forecasting is an integral part of O&R’s planning process 
22 
and is one of the most important and influential components 
23 
of the integrated planning effort which drives overall 
24 


--- Page 51 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
 
Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
 
 
- 51 - 
results, as described in this testimony. The Company has 
1 
focused on incorporating greater granularity in its 
2 
forecasting models to understand better how specific load 
3 
modifiers (i.e., the quantity, the location, and the 
4 
temporal characteristics) impact the system. This enhanced 
5 
forecasting process is another tool the Company uses to 
6 
send correct signals to the market, the DER developer 
7 
community, and other third parties to deploy solutions in 
8 
the most appropriate locations of the O&R service territory 
9 
in order to support the safe and reliable operation of the 
10 
grid. O&R will continue to focus on developing and 
11 
improving its forecasting methodology to provide data at 
12 
appropriate system levels, particularly down to granular 
13 
circuit-level detail. This increased granularity will allow 
14 
the Company to project system needs and improve its 
15 
understanding of how both traditional and potential non-
16 
traditional solutions will address reliability and capacity 
17 
requirements to meet design standards within specific 
18 
geographic/operating regions.  
19 
 
Furthermore, as CLCPA related initiatives expand to meet 
20 
the State’s clean energy goals (e.g., beneficial 
21 
electrification, more focused and impactful DR and EE 
22 
programs and measures, and larger renewable generation 
23 
interconnecting with the transmission system), the ability 
24 


--- Page 52 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
 
Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
 
 
- 52 - 
to understand the temporal and locational impacts across 
1 
the electric delivery system is critical to projecting the 
2 
right type of investments and solutions at the appropriate 
3 
times. Developing a more granular understanding of the 
4 
forecasted impacts from load modifiers will support efforts 
5 
to reach the State’s clean energy targets.  
6 
Q. 
In what ways has the Company recently enhanced its 
7 
forecasting process? 
8 
A. 
Beginning in 2019, the Company improved its granular level 
9 
Weather Adjusted Peak (“WAP”) process by implementing a new 
10 
algorithm that employs a regression tool. Using Python 
11 
programming language, the new regression tool was developed 
12 
to improve the accuracy of weather adjusted peak forecasts. 
13 
The new tool provides graphical representation of the data 
14 
prior to regression to familiarize the user with the data 
15 
set. The enhanced algorithm cleans the load data as it 
16 
iterates and removes outliers to improve the accuracy of 
17 
the regression model. This was the foundation for a more 
18 
granular look at the current O&R WAP process, as well as 
19 
the addition of load modifiers in the system. The Company 
20 
tested the methodology for planning and contingency studies 
21 
in 2019 and has transitioned fully to this forecasting 
22 
methodology as part of the 2020 planning cycle. 
23 


--- Page 53 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
 
Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
 
 
- 53 - 
In addition, O&R had previously used single data point 
1 
historical peaks to calculate the anticipated demand 
2 
forecast. Through work with CECONY and the other New York 
3 
EDCs, the Company developed an improved methodology to 
4 
develop forecasts based on more extensive peak load data 
5 
obtained throughout the summer season. This provides more 
6 
accurate and statistically valid forecasting results that 
7 
are incorporated into the Company’s overall planning 
8 
process.  
9 
 
The Company has spent extensive time revising internal 
10 
databases to improve reporting and automate data transfer 
11 
to realize process efficiencies and improve the quality of 
12 
data used in peak forecast scenario development and 
13 
contingency analysis. The ability to track and report the 
14 
contribution of each load modifier provides engineers and 
15 
operators with a better understanding of the impact of DER, 
16 
beneficial electrification, and other modifiers 
17 
individually. Such ability is the starting point for future 
18 
sensitivity analysis for various modifier effects and DER 
19 
adoption. It also sets the stage for future calculations 
20 
for native load and to determine how the system will behave 
21 
during contingencies. 
22 
Q. 
In what ways has the Company specifically enhanced its 
23 
forecasting methodology to include load modifiers? 
24 


--- Page 54 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
 
Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
 
 
- 54 - 
A. 
The Company has expanded the forecast methodology to 
1 
include ten-year DER modifier forecasts for each 
2 
substation, bank, and circuit. This helped to capture the 
3 
impact of DERs on those particular elements of the system. 
4 
This level of granular data will refine inputs in forecasts 
5 
that traditionally were estimated based on historical 
6 
system bank level data. It also will add detail for the 
7 
electric delivery system within specific 
8 
geographic/operating regions to provide improved study and 
9 
solution development for projected system needs. The 
10 
Company established this methodology with the expectation 
11 
that data sources and assumptions will continue to evolve. 
12 
As such, the Company will continue to enhance and refine 
13 
its processes for projecting load growth and for modifying 
14 
the net load to account for all load modifiers 
15 
appropriately. 
16 
 
As additional and more granular data becomes available, the 
17 
Company will continue to refine its forecasting methodology 
18 
and will be better positioned to adjust its plans/forecasts 
19 
as required. Below are a few examples of how the Company is 
20 
using information on specific load modifiers to inform 
21 
forecasts. 
22 
 
EVs: The Company used light duty EV zip code data to 
23 
develop granular level forecasts. O&R introduced a 
24 


--- Page 55 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
 
Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
 
 
- 55 - 
Direct Current Fast Charger (“DCFC”) charging forecast 
1 
in the 2019 forecasting process. This will evolve to 
2 
include mid-size and heavy-duty EV as soon as more 
3 
data is available. 
4 
 
Distributed PV: The Company employed improved queue 
5 
management system at the circuit level to capture the 
6 
current status and potential pending jobs. The Company 
7 
analyzed and applied the average solar output curve 
8 
during the summer months to the forecasts every year, 
9 
as provided by metered data. The forecasts now include 
10 
two different types of average solar output curves 
11 
(residential/small commercial and large-
12 
scale/community solar) using both system and bank 
13 
peaking hours to develop granular level forecasts. 
14 
 
Battery Storage: As solar paired battery jobs grow 
15 
every year, the 2019 granular level forecasts 
16 
considered different coincident factors to 
17 
differentiate small and large solar paired batteries. 
18 
 
NWAs: The Company treated NWAs as another modifier to 
19 
represent their effects on local facilities in the 
20 
forecasting and planning process, and for reporting 
21 
purposes for granular level forecasts. 
22 


--- Page 56 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
 
Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
 
 
- 56 - 
This advanced understanding of the impact of load modifiers 
1 
combined with the ability to develop bottom-up forecasts 
2 
from circuit-level data will enhance O&R’s ability to plan 
3 
for and address the requirements of the CLCPA. Increased 
4 
understanding of how different load modifiers impact the 
5 
grid will support O&R’s ability to produce more granular, 
6 
long-term forecasts. Such forecasts will provide planners 
7 
with greater insights to determine how load modifiers can 
8 
be incorporated to determine appropriate system needs to 
9 
meet design standards, address the clean energy goals of 
10 
the CLCPA, and enhance the safety and reliable operation of 
11 
the electric delivery system. 
12 
Q. 
In what ways does O&R plan to modify the forecasting 
13 
process going forward? 
14 
A. 
The Company continues to examine and modify the forecasting 
15 
process to improve accuracy and increase granularity. Part 
16 
of this effort moving forward is leveraging field data 
17 
collected through ADMS, power quality (“PQ”) meters, and 
18 
AMI to validate DER assumptions and support forecasting 
19 
methodologies. In addition, to enable the system to be 
20 
designed to support the CLCPA’s goals, the Company has also 
21 
started to consider the potential growth associated with 
22 
beneficial electrification. This includes long-term 
23 
sensitivity analysis regarding electrification of heating. 
24 


--- Page 57 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
 
Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
 
 
- 57 - 
The Company also plans to extend the forecast period from a 
1 
ten- to a 20-year outlook, so that the system is prepared 
2 
to meet CLCPA goals beyond 2030. In addition, to plan for 
3 
potential winter peaking loads beyond 2030, O&R plans to 
4 
produce winter peak forecasts that will be used for 
5 
planning analysis to identify future projects that may be 
6 
needed to support the anticipated load from beneficial 
7 
electrification.  
8 
Q. 
In what ways does the Company plan to refine its 
9 
forecasting related to load modifiers? 
10 
A. 
The Company is planning to work with McKinsey & Company to 
11 
develop a REV/DER/EEDM Forecasting Tool (“Forecasting 
12 
Tool”) to assist in the forecast of specific load 
13 
modifiers. 
14 
Q. 
Please provide an overview of the Forecasting Tool. 
15 
A. 
The specifics of the Forecasting Tool are described in the 
16 
O&R REV/DER/EEDM Forecasting Tool white paper included in 
17 
Exhibit __ (EIOP-1). The Forecasting Tool to be developed 
18 
will be an application software that will organize data and 
19 
sources of information, and contain algorithms, in order to 
20 
forecast the quantities of specific load modifiers and 
21 
their impacts to electric volumetric energy at the system 
22 
level and electric summer and winter peak demand at the 
23 
system level, at the substation level, and at the circuit 
24 


--- Page 58 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
 
Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
 
 
- 58 - 
level, all for a 20-year period. The Forecasting Tool will 
1 
include the following six modules: 
2 
 
Electrification of Heating; 
3 
 
Battery Storage; 
4 
 
PV,  
5 
 
DG and Combined Heat and Power (“CHP”); 
6 
 
EVs; and 
7 
 
EEDM.  
8 
In addition, the EEDM module will forecast programmatic 
9 
measures associated with New Energy Efficiency New York 
10 
(“NENY”) and New York State Research and Development 
11 
Authority (“NYSERDA”), as well as organic EE through new 
12 
technologies, natural conservation, and the CLCPA. McKinsey 
13 
& Company developed similar tools for EEDM and the 
14 
aforementioned DERs and load modifiers for CECONY in 2017 
15 
and 2020, respectively. This project will build on these 
16 
tested and proven models to develop a combined tool for O&R 
17 
with multiple modules, as described above. This work will 
18 
be completed in two phases. The first phase will involve 
19 
adapting the existing CECONY model for O&R’s needs, 
20 
including gathering and integration of the required data; 
21 
adjusting the model as needed; customizing necessary 
22 
algorithms for each module; and testing the methodologies 
23 


--- Page 59 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
 
Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
 
 
- 59 - 
and Forecasting Tool with O&R subject matter 
1 
experts(“SMEs”). The second phase will include the 
2 
integration of all modules into a single application, the 
3 
deployment of that application, technical documentation, 
4 
user testing/acceptance, and employee training. 
5 
Q. 
How long will it take for the Forecasting Tool to be 
6 
developed and operational? 
7 
A. 
Approximately 10-14 months. The Company currently projects 
8 
that the Forecasting Model will be operational by May 2023. 
9 
Q. 
What is the justification for this project? 
10 
A. 
The increased accuracy and granularity of load modifier 
11 
forecasts is critical to projecting the right type and 
12 
timing of investments and customer and Company solutions. 
13 
By providing such forecasts, the Forecasting Tool will help 
14 
manage supply costs and maintain reliability, as well as 
15 
support the Company’s effort to achieve the CLCPA’s goals. 
16 
The Forecasting Tool will reduce the risk of both over- and 
17 
under-forecasting of load modifiers, thus reducing untimely 
18 
and premium supply costs and unnecessary load relief or 
19 
load shedding. This tool will allow O&R’s forecasting to 
20 
keep pace with the ever-evolving energy landscape in New 
21 
York, facilitating more prudent planning, more efficient 
22 
operations, and supporting the achievement of the CLCPA’s 
23 
goals.  
24

22

--- Page 63 ---

1

2

3

4 
typically include new substations, feeder cable, and 
5



7

9

10

17 
the durability and stability of the Company’s 
18 
infrastructure allowing the system to withstand the 
19 
impacts of severe weather events with less damage 
20 
and/or provide for the recovery of the system and

i. 
Electric Blankets and Regular Projects Under $1 Million 
3 
Q. 
What is included in the category of Electric Blankets set 
4 
forth in Exhibit AP-E5, Schedule 2? 
5 
A. 
Electric Blankets include a variety of work, including all 
6 
materials and labor, which must be performed regularly so 
7 
that the Company can continue to provide safe and reliable 
8 
electric service. Blankets are an accounting convention, 
9 
long accepted by the Commission and DPS Staff, whereby, for 
10 
the sake of convenience, the costs of certain recurring 
11 
labor and equipment are grouped together. Included in the 
12 
overall blankets category on Exhibit AP-E5, Schedule 2, are 
13 
the Electric Overhead and Underground Distribution 
14 
Blankets. 
15 
These blankets cover routine expenditures on the Company’s 
16 
Electric Distribution Overhead and Underground systems to 
17 
connect new customers, address municipal requirements, and 
18 
provide necessary funds for daily requirements and upkeep 
19 
of the distribution system. More details on these blanket 
20 
categories are as follows: 
21 
• New Business - This blanket covers overhead or 
22 
underground system improvement electrical projects 
23 


--- Page 66 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
 
Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
 
 
- 66 - 
required for the connection of new customers to the 
1 
electric distribution system. 
2 
• Streetlights - This blanket covers the installation of 
3 
new streetlights on the Company’s system associated 
4 
with new business projects and new customer 
5 
requirements. 
6 
• Road Widening - This blanket covers the relocation of 
7 
existing Company facilities that interfere with 
8 
municipal, state or federal road widening projects. 
9 
• Telephone Interference Work - This blanket covers 
10 
expenditures required when spacing for 
11 
telecommunications facilities is not available on a 
12 
pole and the electric facilities have to be relocated 
13 
to order to accommodate other utilities on the pole, 
14 
pursuant to the Company’s joint use agreements with 
15 
telecommunications companies (e.g., Verizon). 
16 
• Voltage Complaints - This blanket covers installations 
17 
or upgrades to existing facilities to address customer 
18 
voltage complaints. This type of work may include 
19 
adding new transformers, upgrading existing 
20 
transformer capacity and/or upgrading secondary 
21 
systems to improve operating conditions. 
22 


--- Page 67 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
 
Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
 
 
- 67 - 
• System Integrity - This blanket covers small system 
1 
improvement projects on the distribution system to 
2 
enhance service reliability. 
3 
• Customer Complaint Investigations - This blanket 
4 
covers all types of projects that are the result of 
5 
complaints and issues that are raised by customers. 
6 
They may include relocation of guy wires, damage to 
7 
customer property, and all other complaints that come 
8 
through the Company’s blue card system (i.e., system 
9 
for handling non-emergency customer trouble calls). 
10 
• Resiliency – This blanket covers projects that make 
11 
physical changes to reduce susceptibility to storm 
12 
damage, including high winds, flooding, or flying 
13 
debris. Resiliency projects improve the durability and 
14 
stability of the Company’s infrastructure allowing the 
15 
system to withstand the impacts of severe weather 
16 
events with minimal damage and/or provide for the 
17 
recovery of the system and customer load more quickly.    
18 
• Reinforcement/Replacement – This blanket includes the 
19 
replacement or reinforcement of transmission 
20 
structures and possibly the replacement of shield 
21 
wires and/or conductors determined to be structurally 
22 
deficient.

1

2

3

What is the justification for this project? 
5 
A.

6

7 
investments to improve system reliability and resiliency. 
8 
O&R has previously identified this need as having the 
9

10

11

12

13

14 
44-2-13 in the Monsey area. Assuming no BESS is installed, 
15 
the Monsey banks will fail design criteria in approximately 
16 
2026. This project will provide needed load relief to the 
17 
Monsey area, specifically circuit 44-6-13 which currently 
18 
fails Distribution Planning criteria. This project will 
19 
also increase DER hosting capacity in the area, helping the 
20 
Company support the CLCPA’s goals.

24

existing Lovett 138kV Substation. If the new Lovett 345 kV 
1

4

What is the justification for this project? 
8 
A. 
The Silver Lake Substation is a two-bank (Bank 1113 and 
9 
2113) substation with a 3,000 amp, 13.2kV bus tie between 
10 
the banks. For a loss of either bank, the bus tie breaker 
11

12 
loss of Bank 1113, Bank 2113 will exceed its normal rating 
13 
and be at/over its LTE rating. Loading Bank 2113 above its 
14 
LTE rating will require System Operators to shed load to 
15 
avoid damage to the bank, resulting in interrupted service 
16 
for several large shopping centers, grocery stores, nursing 
17 
homes, and large industrial customers along the Route 211 
18 
corridor. When the Company upgraded this station from a 
19 
single-bank to a two-bank station in 2006, the existing 25 
20 
MVA Bank 2113 was not replaced as it was not necessary at 
21 
that time and to reduce overall project costs. By 2023, 
22 
Bank 2113 will have been in service 50 years and will be 
23 
approaching the end of its expected service life. The 
24 


--- Page 80 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
 
Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
 
 
- 80 - 
replacement of Bank 2113 will reduce the current, and 
1 
increasing, risk of extended outages during peak loading 
2 
conditions if there is a long-term outage of Bank 1113.

8

9

10 
that were previously in the Town of Monroe. Driven by 
11

13

14

15

16 
buildings. Distribution Planning has determined that an 
17 
additional 18-25MW of power at peak will be required to 
18 
serve the new load, and the existing infrastructure in the 
19 
area will not be able to support the proposed growth. By 
20 
2021, several circuits will be over relief rating and will 
21 
not pass the Company’s Distribution Design Standard. Until 
22 
the substation project is completed, mobile batteries at 
23 
two locations are required to meet the peak load needs in 
24

this area and enable further economic development. The

Q. 
Please provide an overview of the Line 51 Upgrade project. 
19 
A. 
This project will replace a portion of 795 circular mils 
20 
(“MCM”) aluminum conductor steel-reinforced (“ACSR”) 
21 
conductor on Line 51 with larger conductor. It also will 
22 
relocate a portion of Line 51 to increase the line’s 
23 
overall thermal rating by approximately 20 percent. It will 
24 


--- Page 83 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
 
Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
 
 
- 83 - 
also eliminate the two crossings of Line 51 over 
1 
Transmission Lines 52 and 60 in the area of the Ramapo 
2 
Substation. Line 51 is a 138kV line that originates at the 
3 
Ramapo Substation (New York) and terminates at the South 
4 
Mahwah Substation (New Jersey). 
5 
Q. 
What is the justification for the project? 
6 
A. 
Recent summer studies have indicated that a contingency on 
7 
South Mahwah 345/138kV Bank 258 will load Line 51 slightly 
8 
above its Long-Term Emergency (“LTE”) rating. Without the 
9 
Line 51 Upgrade project, and if the forecasted line 
10 
overloads persist, load shedding would need to commence 
11 
during system peak or emergency conditions, in order to 
12 
prevent damage to the existing overhead conductor. Even 
13 
with load shedding, the exposure to a triple circuit 
14 
transmission outage remains with the crossing of Line 51 
15 
over Lines 52 and 60. This will worsen with time as load in 
16 
the area grows. The increase in thermal rating will make 
17 
the operation of Line 51 more reliable at system peak and 
18 
during emergency conditions for at least the next 20 years. 
19 
The elimination of these two transmission line crossings 
20 
will also result in greater reliability of the transmission 
21 
system in the Ramapo area as well as the Eastern Division 
22 
in general.  
23

ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC.

Please provide a description of the Wurtsboro Substation 
16 
Upgrade – 34.5 kV project. 
17 
A. 
This project will install two (2) 10MVA, 34.5/13.2kV pad 
18 
mount transformers at the existing Wurtsboro Substation 
19 
site and begin the conversion of the Wurtsboro area 
20 
distribution circuits from 4.8kV to 13.2kV. For 
21 
distribution protection and control, the Company will 
22 
install a DSCADA controlled recloser on the 13.2kV side of 
23 
each transformer. This design will allow for two (2) 
24 


--- Page 85 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
 
Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
 
 
- 85 - 
separate 13.2 kV circuits to supply the Village of 
1

2 
capability from the Company’s DCC. 
3 
Q. 
What is the justification for this project? 
4 
A. 
The Wurtsboro Substation is a single-bank station (Bank 
5 
29), which does not have an LTC.  Without an LTC, it is 
6 
very difficult to maintain adequate voltage during normal 
7 
or contingency conditions. Also, as one of three remaining 
8 
substations without communication, there is no ability to 
9 
monitor breaker status or voltage without sending a crew to 
10 
the site to obtain this information. There are also 
11 
electrical Minimum Approach Distance (“MAD”) concerns 
12 
inside the station that require both distribution circuits 
13 
to be offloaded and de-energized to work on the circuit 
14 
breakers. Installing the two pad mount transformers will 
15 
allow the Company to convert the distribution circuits in 
16 
the area to 13.2kV, improving area reliability by 
17 
establishing a 13.2kV tie with the Summitville Substation. 
18 
The new devices will have DSCADA communication with 
19 
capability of remote monitoring and control and will 
20 
eliminate the MAD clearance concerns associated with the 
21 
old station.  
22

brooks, streams, rivers and wetlands. This work may involve 
13 
re-grading, fill placement, or armoring of the shoreline, 
14 
as well as structure and foundation reinforcement in some 
15 
cases. 
16 
Q. 
What is the justification for this project? 
17 
A. 
Shoreline erosion in the immediate vicinity of transmission 
18 
facilities will eventually compromise the structural 
19 
integrity of the towers or poles and could ultimately lead 
20 
to significant customer outages. Restoring the shoreline 
21 
will re-establish the structural integrity of the 
22 
transmission towers or poles and reduce the probability of 
23 
failure of the structures due to erosion. 
24

Q. 
Please provide an overview of the Glen Spey – Mohican Lake 
20 
Road project. 
21 
A. 
This storm hardening overhead distribution project is the 
22 
final part of a four-part project to construct a new three-
23 
phase mainline distribution circuit on the Rio 3-1N-34 
24 


--- Page 92 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
 
Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
 
 
- 92 - 
circuit. This four-part project will upgrade the Rio 3-1N-
1 
34 mainline circuit to 13.2kv and provide for alternate 
2 
sources for backup in the Mohican Lake area to improve 
3 
reliability. This project will replace the existing three-
4 
phase primary along Mohican Lake Road and High Road with 
5 
approximately 13,300 feet of three-phase 13.2 kV Hendrix 
6 
reinforced spacer cable along with one 052 Alumoweld-
7 
Aluminum (“AWA”) messenger cable. This project will also 
8 
include the replacement of approximate eighty (80) poles 
9 
and the addition of three (3) switching devices.   
10 
Q. 
What is the justification for this project? 
11 
A. 
The 3-1N-34 circuit is consistently among the top-ten worst 
12 
performing circuits in the Western Division due to the 
13 
absence of any alternate source ties. This new reinforced 
14 
spacer cable system three-phase mainline distribution 
15 
circuit will provide backup, in the event of a contingency 
16 
on Rio circuit 3-1N-34, to approximately 550 customers 
17 
currently fed from a radial in the Mohican Lake area in 
18 
Lumberland, New York. During storm conditions, the 
19 
probability currently exists that this radial circuit would 
20 
be lost for any tree related outage or a major vehicle 
21 
accident on any single pole leaving these customers out of 
22 
service until repairs are completed. The Hendrix Spacer 
23 
Cable is a pre-engineered electrical distribution system 
24 


--- Page 93 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
 
Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
 
 
- 93 - 
designed for high reliability, tree contact resistant, and 
1 
compact design to reduce tree trimming clearances. The 
2 
addition of multiple DA devices will also assist with 
3 
restoration and fault isolation. This new overhead circuit 
4 
will provide a back feed, maintaining system reliability 
5 
and providing additional storm hardening benefits to the 
6 
surrounding area. Resiliency will be further increased in 
7 
the area resulting from the upgrade of poles, and the added 
8 
switching devices will reduce the number of customers 
9 
impacted by outages.

- 95 - 
station underground and rise on a double circuit pole line 
1

8

10 
customers interrupted with extended restoration times. This

What is the justification for this project? 
11 
A.

12 
of Haverstraw with limited distribution ties. As a result, 
13 
this circuit currently fails the Company’s contingency 
14 
design criteria during peak summer periods. During peak 
15 
summer loading periods, the alternate circuit (27-8-13) 
16 
cannot support the entire load of the two additional 
17 
circuits that serve the Village of Haverstraw (27-4-13 and 
18 
27-3-13) due to cascading of load. As a result, circuit 
19 
(27-3-13) may be without power until repairs are completed; 
20 
this can be significant (6-8 hours) if a pole replacement 
21 
is required. This exact scenario has occurred during a peak 
22 
summer period. The underground system will provide an 
23 
alternate path in the event of a contingency issue, 
24 


--- Page 109 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
 
Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
 
 
- 109 - 
improving reliability for approximately 2,800 customers. 
1

2 
of the circuit that is most prone to impacts as a result of 
3 
vegetation contacts, motor vehicle accidents, or equipment 
4 
failure due to the double circuit configuration, further 
5 
improving reliability.

1

2

area. The new transmission system and substations are 
15

16

for the USMA and surrounding areas in the future. This 
18

4

5

6

7

Q. 
Please provide an overview of the Underground Transmission 
13 
Line 705 project. 
14 
A. 
This project involves the installation of a new 138 kV 
15 
underground transmission line that will span approximately 
16 
5.5 miles entirely within the Town of Clarkstown, in 
17 
Rockland County. Line 705 will originate at the Burns 
18 
Substation and terminate at the West Nyack Substation. This 
19 
project will require alterations at the Burns and West 
20 
Nyack Substations to accommodate Line 705, including a 
21 
termination structure at each substation to transition the 
22 
underground cable to an overhead connection.  The overhead 
23 
connection will then interconnect with newly installed 138 
24 


--- Page 116 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
 
Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
 
 
- 116 - 
kV circuit breakers, disconnect switches, and a 138 kV 
1 
aluminum bus. The West Nyack terminal will also feed a new 
2 
196 MVA 138/69 kV autotransformer and interconnect with the 
3 
existing 69kV copper bus. This project was originally 
4 
proposed and approved (as Line 702A Underground) in the 
5 
2018 O&R Electric Rate Case, with a projected in-service 
6 
date of December 2022. However, the Company’s current 
7 
analysis has allowed for the deferral of the Line 705 
8 
project to a projected in-service date of December 2026. 
9 
The Company plans to prepare and submit an Article VII 
10 
application for this project and begin engineering for the 
11 
project during the period 2022 - 2024. 
12 
Q. 
What is the justification for the project? 
13 
A. 
The O&R Transmission System in the southeastern Rockland 
14 
County area is being stressed by load growth, including the 
15 
addition of large data centers in the Orangetown area. This 
16 
load pocket is presently served from the end of the 138 kV 
17 
system from the north, and the overall southeastern load 
18 
pocket electric delivery system is failing O&R’s design 
19 
standards. Continued load growth without a near-term 
20 
solution increases the risk and likelihood of unplanned 
21 
outages due to capacity overload, causing widespread 
22 
customer outages. As data center loads increase, a double 
23 
contingency condition will result in load shedding to 
24 


--- Page 117 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
 
Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
 
 
- 117 - 
relieve overloads and to prevent further line outages. 
1 
Further increases in the loading of neighboring stations 
2 
and/or the anticipated addition of block loads in the 
3 
southern Rockland County and northern Bergen County areas 
4 
will only worsen the situation. The Line 705 underground 
5 
project offers the best solution as the new line provides 
6 
an independent, diverse source to feed the area. The 
7 
installation of Line 705 will bolster system reliability by 
8 
mitigating the contingency concerns and operating risks 
9 
highlighted above. It will provide path and construction 
10 
diversity to improve system operation for normal and 
11 
contingency operating conditions that meet the Company’s 
12 
design standards and enable further economic development. 
13

What is the justification for this project? 
17 
A.

18 
of the lattice towers that support these facilities that 
19 
are direct embedded grillages, which are prone to 
20 
deterioration over time. Without upgrade, costs associated 
21 
with maintenance of these transmission structures will 
22 
increase. O&R is prepared to develop and implement this 
23 
multi-value project to enable utility-scale renewable 
24 


--- Page 119 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
 
Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
 
 
- 119 - 
generation and storage interconnections, unbottle capacity 
1 
limited facilities, and facilitate the upgrade of 
2 
infrastructure. The Company has been informed by the NYISO 
3 
queue of targeted development areas that align very well 
4 
with “no regrets” investments to the O&R transmission 
5 
system.  The capacity increase in Line 12 & Line 13/131 
6 
will significantly improve their available headroom to 
7 
unbottle current renewable projects, as well as future 
8 
utility-scale renewable generation projects in the 
9 
Company’s Western Division that are planning to 
10 
interconnect to these lines, providing support to meet the 
11 
CLCPA’s goals.

Q.  
Please provide an overview and justification for the 
12 
Transmission System LiDAR Survey program. 
13 
A. 
This program involves complete LiDAR surveys of the lines 
14 
designed as Bulk Electric System (“BES”) transmission 
15 
facilities on the O&R transmission system. The Company 
16 
plans to use this survey information to develop as-built 
17 
plan and profile drawings for the safe and reliable 
18 
operation of these transmission facilities. Many of the 
19 
Company’s transmission facilities were constructed in the 
20 
1920s and 1930s, and the records that O&R has related to 
21 
these facilities are usually limited in nature. 
22 
Construction records are typically not available. An as-
23 
built LiDAR survey will enable these transmission 
24 


--- Page 129 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
 
Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
 
 
- 129 - 
facilities to be modeled in Power Line Systems - Computer 
1 
Aided Design and Drafting (“PLS-CADD”) software to review 
2 
the clearances under all operating temperatures and weather 
3 
conditions. These PLS-CADD models could then be used to 
4 
determine if conductor temperature operating limits should 
5 
be imposed upon any transmission facilities due to 
6 
conductor sag limitations. This program should be 
7 
undertaken to investigate, and improve upon if necessary, 
8 
the safety and reliability of these transmission facilities 
9 
under all operating conditions.

Q.  
Why is the EPM group needed?  
22 
A.  
New York State has ambitious decarbonization goals which 
23 
include adding 850,000 EVs by 2025 and reducing 1990 
24 


--- Page 138 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
 
Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
 
 
- 138 - 
emissions levels by 85 percent by 2050. Electrification of 
1 
the transportation and heating sectors is critical to 
2 
achieving these goals, and the Company’s role as a trusted 
3 
advisor and system operator positions the Company well to 
4 
encourage adoption of clean energy technologies (including 
5 
EVs and heat pumps). The clean energy technologies that 
6 
make up the electrification industry are in their early 
7 
stages of maturity and require dedicated attention to (i) 
8 
help accelerate the growth of the industry, (ii) coordinate 
9 
with customers and third parties to explore innovative 
10 
business models, and (iii) develop internal capabilities 
11 
which will allow for sustainable operation as 
12 
electrification adoption rates increase. Currently, 
13 
customer awareness of electrification technologies is low 
14 
and progress to date has been associated with proactive, 
15 
first-mover customers. The Company must increase customer 
16 
awareness of these technologies, educate customers about 
17 
the benefits of the technologies, and incentivize customers 
18 
to take action when evaluating a purchase – especially when 
19 
existing end-use solutions (e.g., internal combustion 
20 
vehicles, fossil-fueled boilers and stoves) are at the end 
21 
of their useful life. To do so, the Company must increase 
22 
customer engagement through a series of outreach and 
23 
education programs, demonstration projects, innovative 
24 


--- Page 139 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
 
Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
 
 
- 139 - 
business models, and incentives. In facilitating these 
1 
projects and programs, the EPM group will be supporting 
2 
local economic development through increased EV sales, 
3 
employment of contractors for project construction and 
4 
implementation, and (when available) engaging local 
5 
developers and vendors. Finally, the methods by which 
6 
customers adopt and implement electrification will continue 
7 
to increase in complexity. Customers now typically only 
8 
implement electrification technologies in isolation. 
9 
However, in the future, the Company expects customers to 
10 
implement a portfolio of electrification solutions 
11 
simultaneously. Under this scenario, electrification 
12 
programs will have overlap with compounding costs and 
13 
benefits, and the Company will need to be prepared to 
14 
design programs that are understandable by the customer and 
15 
easy to implement and track. Having a strategic vision 
16 
early in the process will be critical to future program 
17 
success. A dedicated team, well-versed in electrification 
18 
technologies, will be most capable of handling the 
19 
increased workload and complexity, and successfully 
20 
executing new programs.

Monsey 
21 
Q. 
Please provide a brief description of the Monsey NWA 
22 
project. 
23 


--- Page 141 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
 
Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
 
 
- 141 - 
A. 
The Monsey area, in the Town of Ramapo, in Rockland County, 
1 
is experiencing significant residential and business growth 
2 
that has led to highly loaded circuits and substation 
3 
transformer banks. As a result, the Company expects that 
4 
loads will exceed distribution design standards under 
5 
normal and contingency conditions during the summer of 
6 
2022. In the 2018 O&R Electric Rate Case, the Commission 
7 
authorized the Company to recover the program costs for an 
8 
NWA solution to address this need. The original NWA 
9 
solution proposed installing a battery energy storage 
10 
system (“battery”) to defer the upgrade of two transformer 
11 
banks and the addition of new circuits. Estimated costs for 
12 
the original NWA solution were approximately $16 million. 
13 
The Company was unable to gain approval from authorities 
14 
having jurisdiction (“AHJs”) for an installation location 
15 
for the battery and was not able to continue implementation 
16 
of the NWA solution. The Company then identified a new need 
17 
and re-designed a new NWA solution in the Monsey area. The 
18 
new NWA solution employs a hybrid approach that combines an 
19 
NWA with a traditional capital investment. Specifically, 
20 
the Company plans to install three batteries and complete a 
21 
transformer installation in the Burns substation. Compared 
22 
to the original NWA solution, the new NWA solution defers a 
23 
much larger capital investment, i.e., construction of the 
24 


--- Page 142 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
 
Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
 
 
- 142 - 
new Viola substation. Since it defers such a large capital

12

13

14

22 
Q. 
Please describe the Optimal Export project.  
23 


--- Page 150 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
 
Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
 
 
- 150 - 
A. 
The Optimal Export project sought to increase hosting 
1 
capacity and incentivize and accommodate greater deployment 
2 
of DERs in the Company’s service territory. By using 
3 
advanced interconnection technology, the Company could 
4 
optimize the export of PV generation to the distribution 
5 
system and positively influence a developer’s decision to 
6 
install DER. The Company partnered with Smarter Grid 
7 
Solution, Inc., a third-party software provider, to execute 
8 
this project. The Optimal Export project divided DERs into 
9 
two classes based upon the DER’s size. The Company 
10 
identified candidates from the interconnection queue that, 
11 
based upon key metrics from their projects, would benefit 
12 
from enrolling into the Optimal Export project. The list of 
13 
candidates was shorter than expected and project 
14 
participation declined for a variety of reasons including: 
15 
not all equipment could integrate with the distribution 
16 
system, the fast evolution of smart inverter functionality 
17 
had increased the risk that technology used in the project 
18 
may become obsolete, and limitations driven by the physical 
19 
space available at potential sites caused projects to be 
20 
cancelled. At the end of Q2 2020, the Company documented 
21 
lessons learned and closed out the project.  
22 
Q. 
Please describe the ISBM project. 
23 


--- Page 151 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
 
Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
 
 
- 151 - 
A.  
The ISBM project focuses on residential, behind the meter 
1 
storage technology. The Company, in collaboration with 
2 
partner Sunrun, Inc., will use an innovative solar plus 
3 
storage Virtual Power Plant (“VPP”) to optimize the 
4 
delivery of clean energy and reduce costs for customers. 
5 
This project will serve as a test-case for the Company to 
6 
evaluate the ability of an aggregated collection of behind-
7 
the-meter (“BTM”) solar plus storage systems to provide 
8 
value to all stakeholders. The Company will use Sunrun’s 
9 
BTM solar plus a storage product, Brightbox, to implement 
10 
this project. This project is targeting 300 residential 
11 
customers across 15 high-impact locations within the 
12 
Company’s service territory. In full, the project will 
13 
deploy 2.9 MW of distributed rooftop solar and 2.1 MW/4.7 
14 
MWh of distributed energy storage over a ten-year 
15 
demonstration period. Sunrun will develop, design, install, 
16 
own, operate and maintain all Brightbox products used 
17 
throughout this project. Sunrun will enter into an 
18 
incentivized leasing agreement with individual project 
19 
participants in order to encourage installation of the 
20 
Brightbox. Twenty percent of the Brightbox’s battery 
21 
capacity will be reserved for backup power, and the 
22 
remaining capacity will be provided to the Company. This 
23 
will provide an aggregated, dispatchable portfolio of 
24 


--- Page 152 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
 
Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
 
 
- 152 - 
energy resources that the Company will use to provide value 
1 
through a multitude of services (e.g., distribution 
2 
benefits, backup power for resiliency, and wholesale 
3 
revenues). The market for this product is not yet mature 
4 
enough to be financially viable for all parties. The 
5 
Company will provide an incentive to Sunrun that ultimately 
6 
decreases costs for project participants who install a 
7 
Brightbox. When critical mass is reached and the market 
8 
matures, the Company hypothesizes that VPPs like the one 
9 
produced through this ISBM project will be allowed to 
10 
participate in wholesale markets. This will shift benefit-
11 
cost analyses for prospective developers and customers and 
12 
reduce the Company’s need to provide an incentive. The BTM 
13 
solar plus energy storage market is dynamic and subject to 
14 
advances in technology, changes in regulatory policy, and 
15 
changes in customer behavior. The Company expects to make 
16 
adjustments throughout the implementation of this project 
17 
to optimize its value.

Q. 
Please describe the Smart Home Rate project.  
6 
A. 
The Smart Home Rate project is designed to demonstrate how 
7 
alternative rate structures can provide price signals to 
8 
customers to optimize value for the customer and the 
9 
distribution system. The Company submitted a project 
10 
implementation plan with the DPS Staff on August 24, 2018. 
11 
The Commission approved the Smart Home Rate Tariff via an 
12 
Order issued on February 7, 2019. The project is a joint 
13 
effort between the Company and CECONY. The project seeks to 
14 
recruit eligible residential customers who opt-in to new 
15 
electric rates (“Smart Home Rates”) that will optimize 
16 
their consumption by means of a price responsive home 
17 
energy management platform. The new Smart Home Rates are 
18 
designed to incentivize customers to use their electricity 
19 
in a way that reduces peak load. Customers must have 
20 
Advanced Metering Infrastructure meters to participate and 
21 
employ the home energy management technologies that will 
22 
accept and react to price signals to optimize their energy 
23 
usage and maximize savings. The Company engaged with 
24 


--- Page 154 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
 
Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
 
 
- 154 - 
Uplight to deploy their Orchestrated Energy platform 
1 
technology to automate central air conditioning loads in 
2 
participants’ homes, which requires installation of an in-
3 
home smart thermostat. Future technologies may include 
4 
automated home battery systems, rooftop PV systems, or 
5 
paired systems. As of October 27, 2020, the Company has 
6 
enrolled approximately 550 customers in the Smart Home Rate 
7 
project.  
8 
Due to social distancing measures enacted in response to 
9 
the COVID-19 pandemic, the Company put the recruitment 
10 
campaign on hold in March 2020 and re-evaluated the 
11 
complete project plan after pausing to align with state and 
12 
federal government guidance. The Company resumed the 
13 
recruitment campaign in September 2020. Following 
14 
successful technology installation, the Company will 
15 
monitor bill impacts, customer response to the technology, 
16 
and technology performance throughout the project’s 
17 
duration. This Smart Home Rate project is currently 
18 
scheduled to end in December 2023. The Company will 
19 
continue to monitor program outcomes, as well as any 
20 
continued impacts of the COVID-19 pandemic and revise the 
21 
project plan as needed.

22

2

3

10

11

12 
program implementation. The Company has the following 
13 
program design principle: all programs will focus on 
14 
customer engagement, maintain flexibility, and establish an 
15 
environment where customers have choice, control, and 
16 
convenience in their decision-making process. The Company 
17 
is currently considering the following program types, 
18 
although others may be identified before or during the 
19 
initiative’s launch and implementation: (i) Outreach and 
20

21

22

23 
a focus on LMI markets, (iii) Ride and Drive events that 
24 


--- Page 159 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC.

- 159 - 
provide prospective EV owners the opportunity to test drive 
1

2 
that meet the customer at the point-of-sale with a hands on 
3 
experience, and (v) Tools, such as online calculators, that 
4 
allow customers to evaluate the total-cost of ownership 
5 
(“TCO”) for a clean energy technology or portfolio of clean 
6 
energy technologies. All of these programs can be enhanced 
7 
by targeted messaging that reaches the customers most 
8 
likely to benefit from a specific program or offering. This 
9 
will be based on customers’ energy usage profile, as 
10
