F. Managed Charging 
8 
Q. 
Please describe the proposed Managed Charging Program. 
9 
A. 
The Managed Charging Program is designed to incentivize EV 
10 
owners to charge at off-peak times to maintain system 
11 
reliability. The Company’s EPM team will execute the 
12 
program, and will engage with a third-party technology 
13 
vendor, or vendors, to deploy cost-effective solutions to 
14 
monitor program participant’s energy consumption associated 
15 
with EV charging. Cost effective solutions may include 
16 
hardware solutions connected directly to the vehicle, or 
17 
software-based solutions through the use of telematics, 
18 
smart chargers, application programming interfaces 
19 
(“APIs”), or AMI. The Company will provide an enrollment 
20 
bonus to incentivize participation and cover start-up costs 
21 
associated with implementing the chosen solution (up to 
22 
$150 per vehicle). The technology solution will track the 
23 
location, time, and duration of charging for EV owners. The 
24 


--- Page 164 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
 
Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
 
 
- 164 - 
Company will then provide incentives to the program 
1 
participants if their charging habits meet the criteria of 
2 
the program. The criteria and associated incentives are as 
3 
follows: (i) $5.00 per month for active participation, 
4 
including keeping the solution active and the EV charging 
5 
in the service territory, (ii) $0.10 per kWh of charging 
6 
during off-peak hours (off-peak hours are defined as 
7 
12:00am-8:00am seven days a week), (iii) $20 per month from 
8 
June to September for avoiding charging between 2:00 pm and 
9 
6:00pm on weekdays. The maximum annual incentive across 
10 
these three criteria will be $500 per year, per EV. The 
11 
program is proposed to begin in 2022 and allow for up to 
12 
100 EVs to enroll each year throughout the period 2022 
13 
through 2024. Once enrolled, the participant is entitled to 
14 
receive incentives for the remainder of the enrollment year 
15 
and the two following years. For example, if a participant 
16 
enrolls in 2024, they are entitled to receive incentives 
17 
through the end of 2026. In full, the Managed Charging 
18 
program is a five-year program with up to 300 EVs enrolled. 
19 
Other costs associated with the program are for setting up 
20 
the data system to track program participant’s charging 
21 
behavior, and license fees paid to the third-party vendor.  
22 
Q. 
What is the justification for this program?   
23 


--- Page 165 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
 
Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
 
 
- 165 - 
A. 
The Managed Charging program provides several operational 
1 
and strategic benefits for the Company and its customers. 
2 
The program minimizes system reliability risks, provides 
3 
benefits to the program participants, and aligns with the 
4 
Company’s support of the State’s clean energy goals. 
5 
Reducing peak load will minimize the risk to reliability 
6 
and may defer or eliminate the need for infrastructure 
7 
upgrades in high penetration areas. Participants will 
8 
benefit from the program because of the financial 
9 
incentives that lower their charging costs and, 
10 
consequently, the total cost of owning an EV. The Managed 
11 
Charging program also presents an opportunity to establish 
12 
a new form of communication between the Company and EV 
13 
owners – a key customer group for the Company’s clean 
14 
energy future. Generating engagement through this program 
15 
will establish a partnership between the Company and EV 
16 
owners, one that can be called upon for future 
17 
communications and programs. The program will also provide 
18 
the Company with information regarding the charging 
19 
behavior of its customers that can inform the design of 
20 
future EV programs. The Company expects EV programs to 
21 
increase in importance from both a regulatory and 
22 
operational perspective, and the Managed Charging program 
23 


--- Page 166 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
 
Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
 
 
- 166 - 
will be a first step and important learning experience 
1 
along this path.   
2 
Q. 
Does the Company propose to recover costs associated with 
3 
the Managed Charging Program as part of this rate case?

V. 
Grid Modernization 
15 
Q. 
Please describe how the Company defines Grid Modernization. 
16 
A. 
The Company’s definition is set forth below.  
17 
Grid Modernization: Investments, some of which may be 
18 
considered foundational and/or DSP-enabling, that improve 
19 
the reliability, resiliency, efficiency, and automation of 
20 
the T&D system. Such investments can include the sensors, 
21 
data, and communications networks that enable enhanced 
22 
visibility and understanding of the behavior of the 
23 
network; technologies and equipment that facilitate greater 
24 


--- Page 167 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
 
Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
 
 
- 167 - 
customer engagement regarding energy usage and 
1 
alternatives; and the underlying systems, data management 
2 
and analytics that facilitate situational awareness, asset 
3 
management, contingency and risk analysis, outage 
4 
management and restoration. These necessary core 
5 
investments underpin the required focus on grid reliability 
6 
and resiliency. They provide the basis for increased 
7 
operational flexibility, can enable efforts toward 
8 
achieving the State’s policy goals, including the 
9 
integration of various types of DER, and are beneficial for 
10 
any resource mix. 
11 
Further definition and clarification are provided below for 
12 
some of the terms included in the grid modernization 
13 
definition that define and support the goals these 
14 
investments and systems seek to achieve. 
15 
Foundational: Enabling grid capabilities that provide 
16 
and/or support applications that increase reliability, 
17 
resiliency, safety, and enhanced situational awareness and 
18 
operational flexibility through advanced technology and 
19 
equipment including robust sensing and measurement, 
20 
information management, data management and analytics and 
21 
communications networking capabilities. Foundational 
22 
investments are “no regrets actions” that can support both 
23 


--- Page 168 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
 
Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
 
 
- 168 - 
current applications and future applications, such as 
1 
integration and utilization of DER, in a modular fashion. 
2 
Reliability: The ability of the electric system to receive 
3 
and deliver the aggregate electric power and energy 
4 
requirements of electricity consumers at all times, taking 
5 
into account scheduled and unscheduled outages of system 
6 
components, while maintaining the ability to withstand 
7 
sudden disturbances or unanticipated loss of system 
8 
components within accepted and defined risk tolerances and 
9 
goals. 
10 
Resiliency: Preparation for, and adaption to, changing 
11 
conditions and the ability to withstand or rapidly recover 
12 
from system disruptions. Disruptions can be caused by 
13 
deliberate attacks, accidents, or naturally occurring 
14 
threats or incidents. 
15 
Safety: Operation of the distribution grid in a manner that 
16 
prioritizes public and workforce welfare, operational risk 
17 
management, and appropriate failsafe modes. 
18 
Operational Flexibility: The ability to operate physically 
19 
connected generation, transmission, and distribution 
20 
facilities in a manner which accommodates dynamic grid 
21 
conditions and changing demand, type of generation and 
22 
resource availability. This also includes the efficiency of 
23 
utility operations. 
24 


--- Page 169 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
 
Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
 
 
- 169 - 
 
The Company defines these as terms for beneficial outcomes 
1 
achieved through investments that promote these functions 
2 
and attributes, which may be distinctly separate or may be 
3 
complementary with (or foundational to) investments made 
4 
for the express purpose of DER integration or value 
5 
capture.   
6 
Q.  
Why are the Company’s grid modernization investments 
7 
critical to achieving the CLCPA’s goals? 
8 
A. 
In order to achieve the CLCPA’s goals, the future electric 
9 
grid will need to support real-time operations across a 
10 
diverse resource mix including traditional assets, DERs, 
11 
and large-scale intermittent renewable generation connected 
12 
to the bulk electric power system (“EPS”). In addition, 
13 
with the Company’s implementation of AMI, O&R is able to 
14 
transmit and analyze extraordinary amounts of data shared 
15 
from thousands of devices. This will allow the Company to 
16 
facilitate coordination between wholesale and distribution 
17 
markets, while continuing to deliver reliability and 
18 
resiliency to customers. O&R’s approach to grid 
19 
modernization prioritizes investments to develop these 
20 
capabilities, deliver resiliency, and simultaneously 
21 
support the CLCPA’s decarbonization and DER deployment 
22 
goals.

A. ADMS and DERMS 
5 
Q. 
Please provide an overview of ADMS. 
6 
A. 
The ADMS is a foundational tool for enabling future 
7 
functionality in Distribution Operations. The system 
8 
combines distribution management applications with SCADA 
9 
and integrates with the OMS to form a single, integrated, 
10 
unified platform. This system provides a technology 
11 


--- Page 174 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
 
Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
 
 
- 174 - 
platform with a shared network model and a common user 
1 
experience for all roles that are required to monitor, 
2 
control, and optimize the secure operation of the 
3 
electrical distribution network. 
4 
An ADMS is fundamental to hosting and integrating many 
5 
advanced applications that will facilitate functionality 
6 
needed to implement advanced grid modernization, enhanced 
7 
system reliability and efficiency, and greater DER 
8 
penetration and future market functionality.  
9 
Q. 
What progress has the Company made in implementing an ADMS? 
10 
A. 
O&R completed its ADMS Scoping Study in 2016, which 
11 
confirmed the Company’s original position that an ADMS is a 
12 
foundational investment that will deliver significant 
13 
benefits with operational efficiencies through grid 
14 
optimization and leveraging investments in DA and AMI. 
15 
Since then, the Company selected an ADMS vendor and 
16 
completed the system design process and started 
17 
implementation of the first phase of the ADMS project. This 
18 
activity has focused on developing enhanced situational 
19 
awareness via alarms, reports, and graphs, enhanced ability 
20 
to integrate field devices, and more granular data for 
21 
planning and analytics. As part of this effort, the Company 
22 
is training operators on the new system and updating 
23 
processes to accommodate functionalities and operating 
24 


--- Page 175 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
 
Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
 
 
- 175 - 
requirements introduced by the ADMS. The first phase of 
1 
ADMS was proposed and approved in the 2018 O&R Electric 
2 
Rate Case and will be in service by the end of 2021. This 
3 
progress will lay the groundwork for the future 
4 
introduction of more advanced functions, allowing a greater 
5 
use of external devices, such as smart inverters, smart 
6 
capacitors and switching devices, and provide the ability 
7 
to monitor and control DER assets. 
8 
Q. 
What activities to continue to implement ADMS does the 
9 
Company plan for the period 2022 - 2024? 
10 
A. 
Activities during 2022 - 2024 will entail installing and 
11 
deploying advanced applications of the ADMS, increasing 
12 
capabilities by building on the functionality already 
13 
established. The Company is planning the development of the 
14 
following applications: 
15 
 
Fault Location, Isolation, and Service Restoration 
16 
(“FLISR”); 
17 
 
Volt VAR Optimization (“VVO”); 
18 
 
Load Flow; 
19 
 
Network Model Power Flow; 
20 
 
State Estimator; 
21 
 
Short Circuit Analysis; 
22 
 
Feeder Reconfiguration; and 
23 


--- Page 176 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
 
Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
 
 
- 176 - 
 
Fault Protection Analysis. 
1 
Q. 
What is the justification for this project? 
2 
A. 
ADMS is a fundamental component to enabling the DSP and 
3 
supporting the achievement of the CLCPA’s goals. All 
4 
planned advanced applications will enhance the 
5 
visibility, monitoring, and control that the Company has 
6 
across the system. The monitoring and controlling 
7 
capabilities of the system will enhance the situational 
8 
awareness of the DCC operators. In addition, some 
9 
applications such as the State Estimator are critical to 
10 
advance the DSP. The State Estimator application, once 
11 
functional, will be able to identify congestion on the 
12 
system and highlight issues impacting DERs, both during 
13 
normal and contingency conditions. As adoption of DER and 
14 
beneficial electrification (EVs and heating) increases, 
15 
this type of capability will be critical to maintaining 
16 
reliability and resiliency for customers. Two other 
17 
critical applications are FLISR and VVO. 
18 
 
The FLISR application, as part of the ADMS, will reduce 
19 
the number of sustained customer interruptions. Through 
20 
central control logic, FLISR provides a switching plan 
21 
for re-energizing portions of a distribution system that 
22 
have been de-energized as a result of a permanent feeder 
23 
fault. FLISR will execute the plan automatically to 
24 


--- Page 177 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
 
Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
 
 
- 177 - 
restore service to the maximum number of customers 
1 
possible, typically within minutes. The FLISR application 
2 
through near real-time feedback from sensors, equipment, 
3 
and communications, will provide the capability to (1) 
4 
detect a fault in the network, (2) locate the faulted 
5 
section(s) using, at a minimum, device status 
6 
information, loss of voltage, and fault current 
7 
indicators, (3) develop an optimum switch order(s) that 
8 
isolates the faulted section(s), and (4) execute a 
9 
restoration plan and commands to restore service to non-
10 
faulted sections. 
11 
 
The Company envisions that the second significant 
12 
initiative that will be realized with the new ADMS, will 
13 
be VVO, which provides for improved system operating 
14 
efficiency and voltage operating levels across the load 
15 
cycle. This will be accomplished by integrating 
16 
automation and smart device initiatives in both the 
17 
substation and distribution environments of the electric 
18 
delivery system. As equipment upgrades and advanced 
19 
technologies proliferate across the system, the Company 
20 
ultimately envisions ADMS providing a near real-time 
21 
integrated VVO control system employing SCADA Monitoring 
22

Q. 
Please provide an overview of the DERMS project. 
12 
A. 
A DERMS is an advanced software application that performs 
13 
DER-related functions, including DER asset management, 
14 
planning, forecasting, and in the longer term, monitoring 
15 
and dispatch. The purpose of a DERMS is to manage a large 
16 
number diverse DERs interconnected to the distribution 
17 
system, to understand the unique status and capabilities of 
18 
each, and present these capabilities to an ADMS and other 
19 
applications in a more useful and manageable way.  
20 
The DERMS investment includes the software as well as 
21 
communications, monitoring, and control infrastructure that 
22 
will be vital to the real-time monitoring and eventually 
23 
control of DER. The functionality gained will support the 
24 


--- Page 181 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
 
Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
 
 
- 181 - 
market optimization/dispatch work that will come in later 
1 
phases, as market rules are established and DER technology 
2 
and capabilities evolve. 
3 
Efforts to date have focused on integrating planning 
4 
functions (both near- and long-term) with DER data 
5 
capabilities into a DERMS environment. The Company has 
6 
implemented enhancements that link the Interconnection 
7 
Online Application Portal (“IOAP”) to the existing DG 
8 
database allowing for easier updates to DG-inclusive 
9 
modeling. This has supported the Company’s efforts to 
10 
incorporate more granular load modifiers into its 
11 
forecasting process, and it will also facilitate future 
12 
DERMS functionality. 
13 
The DERMS will leverage other investments, such as the IOAP 
14 
and ADMS projects. DERMS will also leverage the GIS, which 
15 
is seen as a foundational element to provide O&R with the 
16 
ability to locate and model accurately the various DER 
17 
attributes in a single system of record that is exported to 
18 
ADMS for load flow and optimization.  
19 
Q. 
What is the justification for this project? 
20 
A.  
A DERMS application will be required to realize the full 
21 
potential of the ever-increasing amount of DER throughout 
22 
the Company’s service territory while minimizing grid 
23 
impacts and resulting system upgrades. With a better 
24 


--- Page 182 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
 
Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
 
 
- 182 - 
understanding of the status and capabilities of DER on the 
1 
system, the Company will be able to manage an increasingly 
2 
complex and bi-directional electric system. DERMS is also 
3 
an essential advanced component of the ADMS and critical to 
4 
the Company’s role as the DSP. The monitoring and 
5 
controlling capabilities of the system will enhance the 
6 
situational awareness of the DCC operators. 
7 
In addition, a DERMS could help reduce costs for future NWA 
8 
projects and hybrid solutions, resulting in increased 
9 
opportunities to address distribution system needs or defer 
10 
traditional distribution system investments with DER or a 
11 
combination of DER and traditional infrastructure 
12 
solutions. The implementation of a DERMS will improve the 
13 
Company’s integration of DERs and support to the 
14 
achievement of the CLCPA’s goals.

B. Distribution Automation 
1 
Q. 
Please give an overview of the Company’s DA design 
2 
philosophy. 
3 
A. 
The Company’s DA design philosophy employs a three-tiered 
4 
approach: circuit optimization, field automation, and 
5 
centralized automation control as described below.   
6 
 
Circuit optimization includes designing an efficient 
7 
system through smart capacitors, phase balancing, and 
8 
PQ monitoring; smart capacitors to support VVO; and 
9 
advanced sensors for increased visibility.  
10 
 
Field automation involves auto loops to accomplish 
11 
automatic fault isolation and reduced customer 
12 
outages.   
13 
 
Centralized automation control includes monitoring and 
14 
control from the DCC, segmentation and customer 
15 
restoration via SCADA controlled devices, strategic 
16 
placement of MOABs, and advanced control systems that 
17 
will allow automatic system healing. 
18 
Q. 
Please describe the Company’s planned DA efforts. 
19 
A. 
The Company is planning for the following DA projects 
20 
during the period 2022 - 2024: (1) MOAB Upgrade Program, 
21 
(2) NY Smart Grid Automation Resiliency Project, (3) 
22 


--- Page 184 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
 
Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
 
 
- 184 - 
Distribution Automation RTU Replacement, and (4) NYSERDA 
1 
PON 4074 Westtown Distribution Automation. 
2 
Q. 
Please provide an overview of the MOAB Upgrade Program. 
3 
A. 
The Company will continue to implement a program that 
4 
replaces gang-operated air break (“GOAB”) switches with 
5 
MOAB switches, installing and upgrading field devices with 
6 
command and control schemes. The program will identify and 
7 
prioritize existing GOAB switch locations that have a high 
8 
frequency of operation, have high strategic value, and/or 
9 
are in distant or hard to reach locations. These devices 
10 
are separate from those being installed on specific system 
11 
upgrade or storm hardening engineering projects and are new 
12 
capital installations. The project will replace 
13 
approximately 18-20 GOAB switches per year. 
14 
Q. 
What is the justification for this program? 
15 
A. 
This program was proposed and approved in the 2018 O&R 
16 
Electric Rate Case and is ongoing. By proactively replacing 
17 
GOABs and Disconnects that have a high rate of operation 
18 
and/or are in remote or hard to reach locations, the 
19 
Company is minimizing the time it takes to find and resolve 
20 
a fault in the distribution system. The Company’s analysis 
21 
of the performance of the DA devices during storm activity 
22 
has shown that tens of thousands of customers were saved 
23 
from storm related outages or experienced reduced outage 
24 


--- Page 185 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
 
Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
 
 
- 185 - 
time due to these devices. The Company’s ongoing expansion 
1 
of this project will continue to storm harden the 
2 
distribution system. The Company anticipates that the GOABs 
3 
identified for replacement to be optimum candidate devices 
4 
whose upgrade will result in increased system reliability, 
5 
improved outage restoration times, increased employee 
6 
safety, and reduced maintenance requirements. The MOAB 
7 
Upgrade Program is an integral part of the Company’s storm 
8 
hardening effort, and their continued installation along 
9 
with other DA devices will also help the Company realize 
10 
the full benefits of its ADMS investment. 
11

C. Communications Infrastructure 
5 
Q. 
Please provide a description of the Company’s Grid Mod 4G – 
6 
5G program. 
7 
A. 
This program will expand the Company’s high-speed 
8 
communications infrastructure thereby enabling O&R to 
9 
provide a robust and secure solution throughout its service 
10 
territory. Such expansion is necessary for critical utility 
11 
and customer data transport among control centers, data 
12

13 
O&R currently uses a 220 MHz radio network to support the 
14 
last mile communication requirements for its DSCADA system. 
15 
To accommodate the immediate systems, applications, and 
16 
devices, the Company will expand or enhance existing 
17 
communications infrastructure through upgrades to 4G – 5G 
18 
modems. This project will introduce the 4G – 5G based 
19 
technology, engineer the solution into existing equipment 
20 
control boxes, and develop a process to program, configure, 
21 
and install the equipment in the field.  4G – 5G modems 
22 
will serve as the link that will provide the data capacity 
23 
and frequency to ADMS from the distribution automation 
24 


--- Page 192 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
 
Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
 
 
- 192 - 
devices. It will provide the last mile solution for 
1 
monitoring and control that the multi-year investments in 
2 
distribution automation and the ADMS. The number of 4G – 5G 
3 
modems and communication cards will follow the installation 
4 
rate of distribution automation devices including 
5 
reclosers, MOABs, SCADA capacitors, and high voltage PQ 
6 
nodes. 
7 
Q. 
What is the justification for this project? 
8 
A.

9 
Company projected that fewer than 300 reclosers would be 
10 
serviced by the network. As of Q2 2020, commissioned 
11 
distribution devices consisting of MOABS, SCADA capacitors, 
12 
PQ nodes, and reclosers currently total approximately 1,000 
13 
devices, and the number of these devices will increase, as 
14 
described earlier in this section of testimony. In addition 
15 
to the quantity of devices and the relative density of 
16 
their installations, additional data is available from the 
17 
devices for command and control as well as situational 
18 
awareness, and 4G – 5G modems will help unlock these 
19 
capabilities. A 4G-5G solution should be implemented to 
20 
provide the data capacity and frequency to take full 
21 
advantage of the benefits provided by ADMS and other DA 
22 
devices. 
23

Q. 
Please provide an overview of the Ladentown Breaker 
1 
Replacements project. 
2 
A. 
This project will replace the four Delle Alsthom type PK4C 
3 
345 kV circuit breakers at the Ladentown Substation with 
4 
new ABB 345 kV SF6 breakers. Ladentown Substation is co-
5 
owned by CECONY and O&R, with CECONY being the majority 
6 
owner. All costs associated with this station are allocated 
7 
according to CECONY and O&R’s ownership percentages. CECONY 
8 
is responsible for engineering in this station and, 
9 
therefore, has the lead in identifying and initiating this 
10 
project.  
11 
Q. 
What is the justification for this project? 
12 
A. 
The CECONY High Voltage Circuit Breaker Capital Program is 
13 
an on-going program that targets the replacement of 33 kV, 
14 
138 kV, and 345 kV circuit breakers with a history of poor 
15 
performance, environmental concerns, in-service failures, 
16 
and those which are uneconomical to maintain. The cost of 
17 
overhauling the 345 kV Delle Alsthom type PK4C circuit 
18 
breakers at Ladentown is greater than the cost of replacing 
19 
them. Also, the success rate is low for reducing leaks 
20 
after an overhaul of PK4C type breakers. The CECONY Circuit 
21 
Breaker Peer Team identified the four breakers at the 
22 
Ladentown 345 kV substation for replacement. The 
23 
replacement of these circuit breakers is the most cost-
24 


--- Page 203 ---
 
ORANGE AND ROCKLAND UTILITIES, INC. 
 
Electric Infrastructure and Operations 
 
 
- 203 - 
efficient way to achieve the desired level of reliability 
1 
and life cycle cost. 
2 
Q. 
What are the estimated Electric Plant Additions and the 
3 
projected in-service date? 
4 
A. 
The Electric Plant Additions estimate for this project is 
5 
$2,443,900, and the currently projected in-service date is 
6 
December 2023.

19 
that the Company is required to comply with in the ECC and 
20

21

driving increasing activities in cyber security and supply

Q. 
Please provide an overview and justification for the 
1 
Expanded Three-Phase Clearance Program. 
2 
A. 
The Three-Phase Clearance Program involves an increase in 
3 
three-phase conductor clearance on the Company’s 
4 
distribution system from 10 feet under, 10 feet to the 
5 
side, and 15 feet overtop, to 15 feet under, 15 feet to the 
6 
side, and 20 feet overtop. This increased clearance 
7 
currently takes place on Main Line sections and on 34.5 kV 
8 
three-phase lines.  This request involves the expansion of 
9 
this increased clearance to all three-phase conductor 
10 
throughout the O&R service territory.  It will allow the 
11 
Company to maintain necessary distances around the 
12 
conductors, thus improving the safety and performance of 
13 
the electric system, particularly during weather events. 
14 
Q. 
What is the proposed start date and cost for the Expanded 
15

16

17

18 
RY2, and $1.220 million in RY3. These costs will be 
19 
allocated 100 percent to O&M.

1. Project / Program Summary

Status:  ☒ Initiation  ☐ Planning  ☐ Execution  ☐ On-going  ☐  ☐ Other: ___________

Work Description: 
 
The Company requires an additional planning engineer to produce the advanced Climate Leadership 
and Community Protection Act (“CLCPA”) forecasts needed for distribution and transmission 
planning. Currently, the Company produces a detailed weather-adjusted ten-year peak demand 
forecast at the system, bank, and circuit level. This forecast considers baseline and new business load 
growth, as well as the impact of several load modifiers (electric vehicles (“EV”), demand response 
(“DR”), energy efficiency (“EE”), distributed energy resources (“DER”)) at the coincident summer 
peak. Since 2018, the Company has focused on incorporating greater granularity in its forecasting 
models to understand better how specific load modifiers and how their quantity, location, and 
temporal characteristics impact the system. 
 
In order to keep the Company on track to meet the goals outlined in the CLCPA, the Company will 
extend the current peak forecast period from a ten- to a twenty-year horizon. In addition, the Company 
plans to produce winter peak forecasts that will be used for planning analysis of the T&D systems to 
identify future projects needed to support the anticipated load associated with beneficial electrification. 
As DER penetration increases and new technologies are adopted, additional forecasts (e.g., shoulder 
periods) and scenario based analysis will need to be developed so that both the transmission and 
distribution system are operating to design standards while supporting the interconnection of new 
technologies. The new position will also be responsible for supporting the development of new 
software tools needed to forecast more accurately load modifiers (e.g., photovoltaics (“PV”), EVs, heat 
pumps, DG/combined heat and power (“CHP”)) 
 
In addition to forecasting, the position will function as an additional distribution planning engineer to 
support non-wires alternative (“NWA”) identification, justification, and implementation.  
 


--- Page 213 ---
 
 
EXHIBIT____ (EIOP-1) 
 
 
PAGE 4 OF 132 
 
 
 
4 
The annual cost for the Planning/Forecasting engineer is $100,000 per year and will be allocated 100% 
O&M. 
 
Justification Summary: 
 
As the penetration of new technologies (e.g., PV, EVs, heat pumps, DG/CHP, battery storage) increases 
on the system, accurate and more detailed forecasts are critical in determining the correct time and 
location of long-term transmission and distribution investments needed to meet the CLCPA’s goals. 
This includes extending the forecast horizon to 20 years, developing accurate peak winter forecasts, 
and performing more detailed analysis of shoulder periods as DER penetration increases.  
 
The new position will allow the Company to produce the required additional forecasts, as well as 
support new forecasting modeling tools and methods, NWA development (identification, analysis, 
support), and support the group with other traditional planning analysis. 
 
Relationship to 5-Year and Long-Range Plans and Enterprise Risk Management Strategy 
 
The addition of this position is in alignment with the Company’s goals to increase reliability through 
meeting design standard along with increasing hosting capacity and promoting the CLCPA’s goals. 
  
2. Supplemental Information 
 
Alternatives 
 
Alternative 1 description and reason for rejection 
• 
Additional and longer-term forecasts are needed so that the system can support the clean energy 
goals outlined in the CLCPA. Additions/enhancements/improvements in the forecasting 
process to this point have been implemented by the current planning group. To produce the 
forecasts needed going forward, either they would be completed by external consultants (which 
would be cost prohibitive), or other necessary planning functions by the current planners would 
need to be postponed/eliminated.   
 
Risk of No Action 
 
Risk 1 
Additional forecasts are needed for proper transmission and distribution planning. Lack of these 
forecasts could result in improperly timed or incorrect system investments. 
 
Non-Financial Benefits 
 
Additional and improved forecasting will result in the correct and timely system investments. This is 
critical to conduct proper analysis and planning for both the transmission and distribution system to 
support the CLCPA’s clean energy goals.

Electric Engineering 
2022 
1. Project / Program Summary 
 
Type:  ☒ Project  ☐ Program  
Category:  ☒ Capital  ☐ O&M 
Work Plan Category:  ☐ Regulatory Mandated  ☐ Operationally Required  ☒ Strategic 
Project/Program Title: O&R REV/DER/EEDM Forecasting Tool 
Project/Program Manager: John Catuogno  
Project/Program Number (Level 1): TBD 
Status:  ☐ Initiation  ☒ Planning  ☐ Execution  ☐ On-going  ☐  ☐ Other: ___________ 
Estimated Start Date:  April, 2022 
Estimated Date In Service:  May, 2023 
A. Total Funding Request ($000)  
Capital:  $1,600 
O&M:  
 
 
B.  
☐ 5-Year Gross Cost Savings ($000) 
☐ 5-Year Gross Cost Avoidance ($000) 
O&M: 
Capital: 
C. 5-Year Ongoing Maintenance Expense ($000) 
O&M: 
Capital: 
D. Investment Payback Period:   
(Years/months) (If applicable) 
Work Description: 
The O&R REV/DER/EEDM Forecasting Tool Capital Project will result in an application software that 
will organize data, sources of information, and contain algorithms all used to predict the quantities of 
the following six modules that are all part of New York State’s Reforming the Energy Vision (“REV”): 
Electrification of Heating (“EoH”), Battery Storage (“BS”), Photovoltaic (“PV”), Distributed Generation 
and Combined Heat and Power (“DG/CHP”), Electric Vehicles (“EV”), Demand Response (“DR”), and 
Energy Efficiency (“EE”).  This application software will forecast the impact of these modules on 
electric peak demand and electric volumetric energy at a system and substation level for a 20-year 
period. The EE module will cover programmatic measures through New Energy Efficiency New York 
(“NENY”) and New York State Research and Development Authority (“NYSERDA”), as well as 
organic EE through third wave technology, natural conservation, and the Climate Leadership 
Community Protection Act (“CLCPA”).  
 
This tool will allow for the management of the aforementioned load modifiers, in order to avoid 
overlap through balancing of the potential, and so that there is reasonability in the stock and flow of 
each. 
 
McKinsey & Company developed such an EEDM Tool in 2017 and a REV/DER Tool in 2020 for 
CECONY. This project looks to build on these tested and proven models. 
 
The application software to be developed will cover the 20-year electric forecasts for the following six 
modules: EoH, BS, EV, PV, DG/CHP, and EEDM.  It and will be performed for O&R’s electric service 
territory, specifying the effects of these six modules on: 
● 
Electric System Annual Delivered Volume (by month and service class); 


--- Page 218 ---
 
 
EXHIBIT____ (EIOP-1) 
 
 
PAGE 9 OF 132 
 
 
 
9 
● 
Electric System Annual Billable Demand (by month and service class); 
● 
Peak Load at Electric System Summer and System Winter Peak Hours;  
● 
Peak Load at Electric Summer and Winter Peak Hours for each Substation Bank; and 
● 
Peak Load at Electric Summer and Winter Peak Hours for each Feeder Circuit.  
 
Phase 1: Adapting Methodology 
This phase will adapt the methodology developed for CECONY to O&R for all six modules.  In 
performing such adaptation, the fundamental approach and model architecture will remain 
unchanged.  The changes will be as follows: 
● 
Gather and integrate the requisite data for O&R to use the forecasting modules (a full data request 
will be submitted before operation of a module; commencement of the eight-week operational 
period will be dependent on the availability of the relevant data); 
● 
Make minor adjustments to the model architecture and documentation to capture nuances of O&R 
that varies from CECONY (e.g., geographic breakdown of forecasts will no longer tie to the NYC 
boroughs); and 
● 
Test the methodology with identified O&R Subject Matter Experts (“SMEs”). 
The deliverables from Phase 1 would be: 
● 
Jupyter notebooks containing the O&R algorithm written in python 3.x (i.e., the same code format 
as used in developing the existing CECONY modules); 
● 
Changes to the input and output file structures that will be used in Phase 2 when extending the 
application software; 
● 
Structured assembly of the O&R data required to perform the forecasts, to be integrated to the 
application software in Phase 2; 
● 
Two to three test case output sets that O&R SMEs can use for testing the results; and 
● 
PowerPoint documentation of the of each modules’ underlying forecast calculation methodologies.  
Much of the work in this phase will be completed by McKinsey & Company, with support from O&R, 
CECONY Energy Management, and IT. In all cases, user testing of the algorithms with designated 
SMEs will occur prior to handoff. Completion of Phase 1 assumes: 
● 
A single designated O&R SME for each module who will have primary responsibility for working 
with the McKinsey & Company consultant so that the algorithms are well-suited to O&R’s needs; 
these O&R SMEs will have primary responsibility for conducting user testing of the modules 
toward the end of Phase 1; 
● 
Access to O&R data needed to complete the forecasts from the outset (e.g., pipeline of PV 
installations); identify these data needs prior to the work beginning and assume the data is in place 
when the eight-week operational period begins; and  


--- Page 219 ---
 
 
EXHIBIT____ (EIOP-1) 
 
 
PAGE 10 OF 132 
 
 
 
10 
● 
Working sessions for each module in the first two weeks following the operational period with the 
O&R SMEs who are closest to the forecasts today. 
Phase 2: Developing and Deploying the Application  
This phase will be conducted using the Agile/Scrum Approach. As the prior DER forecasting 
development effort, McKinsey will review development progress with the O&R team weekly or bi-
weekly and integrate O&R’s feedback as appropriate. Phase 2 deliverables will include: 
● 
Six O&R modules, developed, tested, and deployed on the IT cloud, and integrated into a single 
application.  All module users will be able to see the CECONY and O&R forecasts, to benchmark 
and streamline the development processes. Only the developers of a forecast will be allowed to 
edit that forecast; 
● 
Technical documentation; and 
● 
Perform user test cases and a complete the user testing and acceptance process. 
During Phase 2, O&R will provide: 
● 
A single Product Owner (“PO”) will be assigned during development (with more time in some 
weeks than in others) and acting as the single point of coordination; 
● 
Confirmation that the algorithms and output developed in Phase 1 are producing the expected 
results, and that they do not require logical changes to alter the results or the inputs during Phase 
2; 
● 
Identification at the outset of the core SMEs who will be responsible for testing and approving each 
module, and availability of sufficient time from these SMEs to perform user testing at the end of 
the effort (note that user testing of the results and algorithms will be conducted prior to the launch 
of Phase 2; this testing will be focused on the software functionality, rather than on the numerical 
results); 
● 
Identification at the outset of the Information Technology (“IT”) process requirements and a 
timeline to complete during Phase 2, including any required assignment of personnel time; 
● 
Ownership of the final production deployment, given many of the final deployment steps need to 
be conducted by O&R personnel; 
● 
Confirmation at the outset of the tools and standards required for acceptance of the solution in the 
IT approval processes. 
Nature of Work 
The estimated work required to complete the Phase 1 and Phase 2 includes the following: 
● 
50% user interface and middleware work (including testing); 
● 
35% algorithm modification to adapt the core calculation engine to O&R (including due diligence 
on existing forecasting methods and processes through interviews and documentation assessment, 
plus user testing); 
● 
10% data-structuring and assembly; and 


--- Page 220 ---
 
 
EXHIBIT____ (EIOP-1) 
 
 
PAGE 11 OF 132 
 
 
 
11 
● 
5% user training.  
This work skews heavily towards digital asset-building (e.g., user interface, middleware) because 
virtually all of the business process design and methodology development McKinsey has performed 
for CECONY is applicable to this project.  
This project is currently scheduled to commence in 2022 and to be tested and completed in 2023. 
 
Justification Summary: 
 
The uncertainty of the energy landscape has increased with its evolving complexity. This application 
software will reduce this uncertainty and provide a higher level of confidence in the summer peak 
demand, winter peak demand, and annual delivered volume forecasts for O&R electric. Such forecasts 
are essential for reliability planning, capital planning, budget management and bill impact modeling, 
and rate design. 
 
McKinsey has developed versions of this application software for CECONY.  CECONY currently is 
using these application software to develop Electric System Peak, Network/Load Area Peak, and 
Delivered Volume Forecasts.  
 
This application software provides a higher-level of confidence due to its stock and flow approach 
through gaging the potential/universe of several load modifiers and maintaining the best available 
data while the framework and mechanics of achieving a future carbon neutral economy evolves.  
 
This application software will allow for the management of the aforementioned load modifiers, in 
order to avoid overlap through balancing of their potential, and so that there is reasonability in the 
stock and flow of each. 
 
The technology being applied is the first of its kind in the energy industry and is best available tool for 
the required job. 
 
The benefits of such a project can be described through the following example: 
 
Estimated improved forecast accuracy of 10%. (2% for organization, 2% for balancing across load 
modifiers, 3% for improved algorithm, and 3% for improved data streams). 
 
Such improvements will lead to: 
 
• 
For reduction in an over-forecast - Avoided supply costs, purchasing the next incremental 
MW, which is dirtier, as well avoided load relief at certain substations; and 
 
• 
For reduction in an under-forecast - Avoided last minute surprise day ahead supply costs, 
which are higher than planning in advance, unforeseen outages/load shedding, and enhanced 
ability to comply with electric performance metrics. 
 
This project focuses on consistency, staying in pace with a volatile market and evolving regulatory 
landscape, and having an up to date forecasting process. The status quo will not work with our ever-
changing energy landscape, tighter regulations, and the need to minimize climate change.

Relationship to 5-Year and Long-Range Plans and Enterprise Risk Management Strategy 
 
The project is in alignment with the Company’s goals to provide safe and reliable services to the local 
community and to support CLCPA goals. 
 
2. Supplemental Information 
Alternatives 
 
Alternative 1 description and reason for rejection 
The alternative would be to continue to perform manual and independent forecasts for EE, DR, 
DG/CHP, PV, EoH, BS, and EV. Such forecasts are not balanced against one another to the level that 
the new software application performs and are not organized and fed from updated data sources and 
stock and flow. 
 
Risk of No Action 
 
Risk 1 
 
The risk of no action is that the uncertainty in our forecasts will increase under the current policy 
environment. Forecast error can lead to diminished reliability, higher supply costs, and increased O&M.  
 
Non-Financial Benefits 
 
The non-financial benefits of this project are an enhanced ability to comply with regulatory 
requirements, improve supply plans, improve energy scheduling, and effect efficiencies in the overall 
forecasting process.

modifiers, 3% for improved algorithm, and 3% for improved data streams). Such improvements will 
lead to: 
 
 
 For reduction in an over-forecast - Avoided supply costs, purchasing the next 
incremental MW, which is dirtier, as well avoided load relief at certain substations. 
 
For reduction in an under-forecast - Avoided last minute surprise day ahead supply

and enhanced ability to comply with electric performance metrics.

Electric Engineering 
2021 
1. Project / Program Summary 
 
Type:  ☒ Project  ☐ Program  
Category:  ☒ Capital  ☐ O&M 
Work Plan Category:  ☐ Regulatory Mandated  ☒ Operationally Required  ☐ Strategic 
Project/Program Title: Monsey Two Underground Circuit Exit Upgrades 
Project/Program Manager: Wayne Banker 
Project/Program Number (Level 1): 21501392 
Status:  ☒ Initiation  ☐ Planning  ☐ Execution  ☐ On-going  ☐  ☐ Other: ___________ 
Estimated Start Date: 2021 
Estimated Date In Service: 2022 
A. Total Funding Request ($000)  
Capital: $2,990 
O&M:  
 
B.  
☐ 5-Year Gross Cost Savings ($000) 
☐ 5-Year Gross Cost Avoidance ($000) 
O&M: 
Capital: 
C. 5-Year Ongoing Maintenance Expense ($000) 
O&M: 
Capital: 
D. Investment Payback Period:   
(Years/months) (If applicable) 
Work Description: 
 
This project involves the installation of two (2) additional 750MCM underground (“UG”) circuits (44-
3A-13 and 44-5A-13) served from the Monsey Substation. With no spare breaker cubicles at the station, 
two UG distribution circuits (44-3A-13 and 44-5A-13) will be connected to existing Monsey station 
breakers 44-3-2b and 44-5-2b, respectively, and will continue UG crossing NYS Route 59 to North 
Saddle River Road in Monsey. Circuit 44-3A-13 will rise on N Saddle River Road approximately 900 
feet from the station and circuit 44-5A-13 will continue up N Saddle River Road to Maple Ave to NYS 
Rt 306 approximately 4,800 feet where it will rise on a new riser pole. Distribution Planning also 
suggests a spare duct along the same path as the 44-5A-13 be installed for a potential circuit if the 
Monsey substation is expanded in the future. With the installation of two additional UG circuits, this 
project will reduce circuit loading on the 44-6-13, 44-2-13, and 44-4-13 and meet Distribution Design 
Standard. This project is scheduled to start in 2021, with an in-service date of 2022. 
 
Justification Summary: 
 
In 2017, O&R issued an RFP for the Monsey area to defer capital infrastructure required to upgrade the 
Monsey Substation and associated distribution circuits in order to meet short-term and long-term 
customer needs. The Company was focusing on least cost non-wire alternatives (“NWAs”) that would 
reduce peak demand in areas where substantial capital investments are needed to improve system 
reliability and resiliency.  The Monsey Substation is comprised of two 138kv -13.2kV, 25MVA 
transformer banks (Bank 144 and 244) each serving three distribution circuits. These banks have 
experienced significant load growth which will overload banks and associated distribution circuits 
during system contingencies, resulting is violations of O&R’s planning criteria.  
 
 


--- Page 226 ---
 
 
EXHIBIT____ (EIOP-1) 
 
 
PAGE 17 OF 132 
 
 
 
17 
The Monsey NWA would serve two distinct purposes:  
1) For bank contingency purposes, reduce peak electric load within the area served by the 
Monsey Substation and Banks 144 and 244; and 
2) For single distribution circuit contingency purposes, reduce peak electrical load on Monsey 
distribution circuits 44-2-13, 44-3-13, and 44-6-13.   
After three-years and many reviews, the vendor was unable to secure site approval to install a battery 
energy storage system (“BESS”) on distribution circuits 44-6-13 or 44-2-13 in the Monsey area. In the 
event of no BESS on distribution Bk 144 or 244 (Ckt: 44-5-13 and 44-2-13) the Monsey banks likely will 
fail the Company’s design criteria in 2026.  
 
The most highly loaded circuit (44-6-13) currently fails Distribution Planning criteria. From 2017 to 
2020, the Company completed several overhead projects in the Monsey area, and those projects have 
brought some load relief in the Monsey area. The completion of the two (2) UG hybrid circuits (44-3A-
13 and 44-5A-13) will provide relief to 44-6-13 and 44-2-13. 
 
Circuits 44-6-13 & 44-5A-13 
The most highly loaded circuit (44-6-13) currently fails our Distribution Planning criteria, with an 
additional 2MW of load that will come online in 2021 as a result of a new large condominium project 
located at the tail end of the circuit.  During the 2019 summer operating period, circuit 44-6-13 was 
operating at 10.3 MW (467amps) and was approaching our relief rating (475amps) for the circuit. To 
address the loading on circuit 44-6-13, the company will construct a hybrid circuit solution that will 
require installation of 750MCM UG conductor to serve circuit 44-5A-13 that will be connected to the 
existing Monsey station breaker (44-5-2b). The UG installation will begin at the station breaker and 
continue north on Saddle River to N Saddle River Road to W Maple to NYS Rt 306 rising on a new 
inter-built pole. This will consist of approximately 5,500 feet of undergrounding, with associated pull 
boxes and/or manholes. Distribution Planning suggests that a spare duck bank be installed along the 
same path for a potential circuit when the Monsey substation is expanded in the future. The new 
circuit 44-5A-13 will be connected to existing circuit 44-4-13, which will be reconfigured upon 
completion of this project.  This new circuit (44-5A-13) will reduce circuit loading on 44-4-13 and will 
have the capacity to back up circuit 44-3A-13 (a portion of former circuit 44-6-13). Upon completion of 
the UG project, circuit (44-4-13 and 44-5A-13) will be reconfigured and will allow a future overhead 
mainline project to be completed on Ralph Drive, which currently serves over 941 customers from a set 
of cutouts. The purpose of that project will be to establish a potential new circuit tie with 19-14-13 or 
19-10-13 to bring the circuit up to Distribution Planning Standards. 
 
Circuits 44-3-13 & 44-3A-13 
To reduce circuit loading on circuit 44-6-13, the Company will construct a new hybrid UG circuit 
solution starting at the Monsey Substation (station breaker 44-3-2B). It will require the installation of 
750MCM three-phase UG conductor (approximately 1,300 feet) and will connect to a new station exit 
riser pole (P# 57241/40360) located on N Saddle River Road, to be known as 44-3A-13. The existing 
circuit (44-2-13) located on N Saddle River will be reconfigured upon completion of this project. This 
new circuit (44-3A-13) will reconfigure circuit 44-2-13 and the circuit 44-6-13 feeder path. The three-
phase 750MCM UG installation will begin at the station breaker 44-3-2b and continue north 
terminating on N Saddle river road at new riser pole# 57240/40363. Approximately 1,300 feet of three 
phase UG conductor will be required.  
 
Relationship to 5-Year and Long-Range Plans and Enterprise Risk Management Strategy 
 
The project is in alignment with the Company’s goals to meet design standard, upgrade aging 
infrastructure along with increasing hosting capacity to promote the Climate Leadership and

Community Protection Act’s (“CLCPA”) goals. In addition, the project addresses Corporate risk for

2. Supplemental Information 
 
Alternatives 
 
Alternative 1 description and reason for rejection 
The Company’s original plan was to upgrade the Monsey substation with two new 40MVA banks. The 
new transformers would have solved the bank contingency and circuit contingencies and prepared for 
system contingencies in the surrounding area. Due to issues with offloading the station for a prolonged 
period of time and space constraints at the existing site, Distribution Planning considered the 
installation of two new circuit positions at Monsey to meet the immediate need on circuit 44-6-13. 
 
Alternative 2 description and reason for rejection 
In response to circuit 44-2-13, 44-6-13 deficiencies, in 2018, the Company solicited for an NWA solution 
to bring relief to circuits 44-6-13 and 44-2-13. This would have allowed the two circuits to meet the 
design standard and provided relief to both Monsey banks. After an exhaustive property search, sites 
could not be located on the circuits where the relief is required. The Company attempted to reconfigure 
circuits was attempted to make a nearby cell-site work. However, the Company could not reach an 
agreement for use of that site. At this point, the Company can still pursue batteries to provide bank 
relief. However, circuit relief will require the installation of two new circuit positions at Monsey. 
 
Risk of No Action 
 
Risk of No Action:  
 
There are several risks associated with not completing the installation of two additional 750MCM UG 
circuits (44-3A-13 and 44-5A-13) served from the Monsey Substation:  
o 
Circuit 44-6-13 would continue to fail Distribution Design Standard; 
o 
Monsey Bank 24 would no longer have 100 percent bank backup for loss of Bank 144, 
which would result in customer hours of interruption; 
o 
Monsey banks have experienced significant load growth which has begun to overload 
the banks and associated distribution circuits during system contingencies (i.e., loss of 
service of a substation transformer bank) resulting in violations of O&R design 
standards; there are new business loads expected in the area, hence the 44-6-13 and 44-
2-13 and 44-4-13 will continue to fail design standards; 
o 
The total station load at Monsey would exceed the LTE rating of the Banks at 37.8MVA, 
requiring customers to be interrupted until adjacent ties (which are also heavily loaded) 
can be used to reduce the area load below LTE rating of the banks to serve the customers; 
and 
o 
Burns circuits 19-10-13 and 19-14-13 will fail design and reduce backup for Burns and 
Tallman circuits serving Monsey area.

Non-Financial Benefits 
 
Non-financial Benefits:  
 
Construction of two additional 750MCM UG circuits (44-3A-13 and 44-5A-13) served from the Monsey

follows:  
o 
Construction of the additional circuits will allow the circuits to be available for system 
contingencies; 
o

o

o

rating and reduce exposure/momentary outages for the customers on circuits 44-6-13, 
44-3-13, 44-4-13, and 44-2-13; and 
o 
The additional circuits will also increase hosting capacity to promote the CLCPA’s goals.

Work Description: 
 
Line 31 is part of a 69 kV transmission loop that feeds several 69 kV substations in the Central Division.  
Line 31 emanates from the Hillburn Substation and ends at the Sloatsburg Substation.  This project 
involves modifying or replacing structures associated with Line 31.  Specific non-compliance with design 
standard that must be addressed include: 
• 
Installation of downlead grounding for adequate grounding of overhead ground wires and guy 
wires; 
• 
Replacement and/or addition of guying to provide adequate structural support; 
• 
Modification of conductor sag and conductor hardware to provide additional clearance; and 
• 
Modification or replacement of structures or structure components to provide adequate structural 
capacity. 
 
 
Justification Summary: 
 
The Company has determined that certain structures associated with Line 31 do not comply with design 
criteria. Updated survey and design documentation in accordance with the technical specification is also 
needed.   
 
Relationship to 5-Year and Long-Range Plans and Enterprise Risk Management Strategy 
 
The project is in alignment with the Company’s goal to meet design standard.  In addition, the project 
addresses Corporate risks for Loss of Transmission and Substation facilities.

Electric Engineering 
2015 
1. Project / Program Summary 
 
Type:  ☒ Project  ☐ Program  
Category:  ☒ Capital  ☐ O&M 
Work Plan Category:  ☐ Regulatory Mandated  ☒ Operationally Required  ☐ Strategic 
Project/Program Title: New Blooming Grove Substation, Transmission System Extension and 
Distribution Circuit Exits 
Project/Program Manager: John Cioffi  
Project/Program Number (Level 1): 10075223, 
10075390 and 10075224 
Status:  ☐ Initiation  ☐ Planning  ☒ Execution  ☐ On-going  ☐  ☐ Other: ___________ 
Estimated Start Date: March 2015 
Estimated Date In Service: May 2023 
A. Total Funding Request ($000)  
Capital: $24.8M 
O&M:  
 
B.  
☐ 5-Year Gross Cost Savings ($000) 
☐ 5-Year Gross Cost Avoidance ($000) 
O&M: 
Capital: 
C. 5-Year Ongoing Maintenance Expense ($000) 
O&M: 
Capital: 
D. Investment Payback Period:   
(Years/months) (If applicable) 
Work Description: 
 
This project will replace the existing 25 MVA single-bank Blooming Grove Substation with a new two-
bank station on nearby Company-owned property.  The new station will consist of two (2) 50 MVA, 
69/13.2 kV transformers with Load Tap Changers (“LTCs”), 13.2kV switchgear with a total of twelve 
circuit positions.  Six circuits will be placed in service initially, four will be provisioned for future use, 
and two circuits will be used to connect modular battery storage. 
 
The existing 69kV Blooming Grove Substation is a single bank substation with one (1) 25MVA, 
69/13.2kV transformer.  The substation serves approximately 6,161 customers from four (4) 13.2kV 
distribution circuits. The northern portion of the load area served by Blooming Grove borders Central 
Hudson’s service territory. This limits distribution tie capability to the two circuit ties to the south 
(along Routes 94 and 208).  Base electric load annual growth in this area is averaging 1.5 percent with 
additional growth expected from a large proposed high-density subdivision. The area is also 
experiencing applications for large, distributed energy resource (“DER”) interconnections such as 
photovoltaic (“PV”) generation and battery storage.  In the event of a loss of Bank 276 at peak time, 
distribution ties would not be able to pick up enough of the load and prevent enough customer hours 
of interruption to meet design standards. 
 
Initially transmission backup will continue to be provided by the WM Line from Central Hudson and 
both Lines 96 and the WM Lines will be rerouted to the new Substation.  Recently, Central Hudson has 
indicated that it is considering retiring the WM Line. If such retirement occurs, a new 69kV 
transmission source will be required from the O&R transmission system to meet N-1 Transmission 
Design Standards.  This related, but independent, project is described in detail elsewhere in a separate 
white paper within this Exhibit.    


--- Page 236 ---
 
 
EXHIBIT____ (EIOP-1) 
 
 
PAGE 27 OF 132 
 
 
 
27 
In late 2018, the Company issued a Non-Wires Alternative (“NWA”) request for proposals to solve for 
the loss of Bank 276. While the Company received several proposals, a thorough third-party and 
internal review determined that none were technically viable and/or in the spirit of New York State’s 
Reforming the Energy Vision (“REV”) initiative as they relied heavily on fossil fuel generation. As a 
result, the traditional solution was re-prioritized in the budget and is currently scheduled for 
completion in 2023. 
 
Justification Summary: 
1. The new two-bank Blooming Grove Substation will provide 100 percent bank-backup for the 
loss of either bank for the design life of the station. Adding a second bank at this station is 
critical due to the limited backup from adjacent distribution circuit ties.   
2. The additional distribution circuits will provide load relief and backup for the existing 
Blooming Grove circuits. This will allow loop schemes to be installed which will significantly 
improve reliability for the circuits at the edge of the O&R service territory, specifically 
Washingtonville and the northern portion of Blooming Grove.   
3. The additional circuit/bank capacity will increase area DER hosting capacity and support the 
increased load associated with electrification of heating and transportation.  
4. Two circuits (one from each bank) will be reserved for energy storage. These batteries will be 
used to capture excess local generation during the day and store the energy on-site. In the late 
afternoon, when PV generation decreases, the batteries will be discharged and used for peak 
shaving. This will reduce peak demand, improve station load factor, and support New York 
State’s Climate Leadership and Consumer Protection Act’s (“CLCPA”) goals for energy 
storage. 
Relationship to 5-Year and Long-Range Plans and Enterprise Risk Management Strategy 
 
The project is in alignment with the Company’s goals to meet design standard, upgrade aging 
infrastructure along with increasing hosting capacity to promote the CLCPA’s goals.  In addition, the 
project addresses Corporate risks for Loss of Transmission and Substation facilities through the 
construction of the new substation. 
 
 
2. Supplemental Information 
 
Alternatives 
 
Alternative 1 description and reason for rejection 
In December 2018, the Company issued a request for proposals to determine if an NWA solution was 
available to improve area reliability by solving for the loss of Bank 276. The Company envisioned the 
potential solution would be to isolate electrically a portion of the Blooming Grove load area and allow 
it to be served from a local microgrid. While the Company received several proposals, none proved to 
be technically viable or in the spirit of REV. As a result, the Company accelerated the traditional 
substation project to 2023. 
 


--- Page 237 ---

PAGE 28 OF 132 
 
 
 
28 
Risk of No Action 
 
Risk 1 
In 2020, for the loss of Bank 276 at peak time, the Blooming Grove distribution circuit ties are only able 
to pick up approximately 42.6 percent of the station load until a mobile transformer can be installed. 
This would result in approximately 75,247 customer hours of interruption. Therefore, Bank 276 fails to 
meet the distribution design standard for having less than 60 percent backup and exceeding 60,000 
customer hours of interruption. 
 
Non-Financial Benefits 
• 
Increased reliability through:  
o 
100 percent bank-backup for the loss of either bank; 
o 
Providing load relief and backup for the existing Blooming Grove circuits;  
o 
Reduced peak demand; and  
o 
Improved station load factor. 
• 
Additional hosting capacity for DER. 
• 
Support for a number of local contractor jobs over the course of construction 
• 
Support in meeting the State’s CLCPA goals. 
Summary of Financial Benefits and Costs (attach backup)

2. Major financial benefits 
 
3. Total cost 
 
The Company estimates that the total cost of this project to be $24.9M. 
 
4. Basis for estimate 
 
The Company used historical data from previous similar projects to determine the estimated cost for 
this series of projects.  
 
5. Conclusion 
 
The Company must construct this series of projects as planned to provide increased reliability. In 2020, 
for loss of Bank 276 at peak time, distribution ties are only able to pick up approximately 42.6 percent 
of the station load until a mobile transformer can be installed. This would result in approximately 
75,247 customer hours of interruption. Therefore, Bank 276 fails to meet the design standard for having 
less than 60 percent backup and exceeding 60,000 customer hours of interruption.  
 
Project Risks and Mitigation Plan 
 
Risk 1                                                                        Mitigation plan 
Technical Evaluation / Analysis 
 
The existing substation is served by a radial transmission feed, Line 96, from the Monroe Substation with 
available transmission switchable backup from Central Hudson across their WM Line. Although the 
non-LTC 25 MVA transformer has a normal rating of 29.8 MVA and an LTE rating of 37.8 MVA, the 1275 
Amp bus (and 1200 Amp bus switch) limits both ratings to 27.4 MVA.  The 2019 independent peak for 


--- Page 238 ---
 
 
EXHIBIT____ (EIOP-1) 
 
 
PAGE 29 OF 132 
 
 
 
29 
Bank 276 at Blooming Grove was 19 MVA. The 2020 weather normalized forecasted peak is 19.3 
MVA.  Until 2009, Bank 276 only served three distribution circuits, with two of them feeding the edge of 
the O&R service territory into Washingtonville.  Due to load growth and limited backup, both circuits 
failed the Distribution Design Standard and were among the Company’s top 20 worst performing 
distribution circuits.  In 2009, a fourth distribution circuit was added to provide load relief and backup 
for the existing circuits. This allowed all the circuits to pass the design standards, as well as improve 
performance for both normal and storm conditions.  While this improved circuit performance, backup 
for loss of Bank 276 is limited to two long distribution ties to the south. In 2020, for loss of Bank 276 at 
peak time, distribution ties are only able to pick up approximately 42.6 percent of the station load until 
a mobile transformer can be installed. This would result in approximately 75,247 customer hours of 
interruption. Therefore, Bank 276 fails to meet the design standard for having less than 60 percent backup 
and exceeding 60,000 customer hours of interruption

The following projects must be constructed together: 
 
1. The New Blooming Grove Substation; 
2. The New Blooming Grove Substation Underground Circuit Exits; and 
3. The New Blooming Grove Transmission Extensions.

1. Project / Program Summary 
 
Type:  ☒ Project  ☐ Program  
Category:  ☒ Capital  ☐ O&M 
Work Plan Category:  ☐ Regulatory Mandated  ☒ Operationally Required  ☐ Strategic 
Project/Program Title: Lovett 345 kV Substation and Transmission 
Project/Program Manager: Vivian Winters 
Project/Program Number (Level 1): 10106572/ 
10106554/ 22081610 
Status:  ☐ Initiation  ☐ Planning  ☒ Execution  ☐ On-going  ☐  ☐ Other: ___________ 
Estimated Start Date: January 2010 
Estimated Date In Service: May 2023 
A. Total Funding Request ($000)  
Capital: $43,819.3 
O&M:  
 
B.  
☐ 5-Year Gross Cost Savings ($000) 
☐ 5-Year Gross Cost Avoidance ($000) 
O&M: 
Capital: 
C. 5-Year Ongoing Maintenance Expense ($000) 
O&M: 
Capital: 
D. Investment Payback Period:   
(Years/months) (If applicable) 
Work Description: 
 
The Project consists of the construction of a new 345kV Gas Insulated Switchgear (“GIS”) Substation in 
Tompkins Cove, Town of Stony Point, Rockland County, New York. This new substation, known as the 
Lovett 345kV Substation, will be located immediately adjacent to the existing Lovett 138kV Substation 
as well as existing 345kV overhead Transmission Lines Y88 and Y94. The substation will consist of a 
345kV four (4) breaker ring bus which will supply a 400 MVA 345/138kV autotransformer.  The 
autotransformer will provide an additional supply to the Lovett 138kV substation. Existing Line Y88 
from Ladentown to Buchanan will be divided into two (2) separate lines.  Line Y88 will run from 
Ladentown to Lovett and newly designed Line Y66 will run from Lovett to Buchanan. Both lines will be 
connected to ring bus positions at the new GIS.  As part of this project, Line Y94 will need to be 
relocated to accommodate the construction of this new 345kV substation.  The remaining fourth 
position within the GIS will be designated as a spare position which can accommodate a future 
connection to the ring bus.  This project has a proposed in-service date of May 2023. 
 
Justification Summary: 
 
The New York Independent System Operator (“NYISO”) 2010 Reliability Needs Assessment Study 
(“RNA”) determined that there is a reliability need on the O&R transmission system.  This reliability 
need occurs at summer peak load conditions for the simultaneous loss of 345kV Lines 67 and 68, 
which run between the Ladentown Substation and the Bowline Generating Station.  For this 
contingency 138kV Line 60, which runs between the Ramapo and Tallman Substations, and 69kV 
Line 652, which runs between South Mahwah and Upper Saddle River Substations, will be loaded 
above their emergency ratings.  The proposed solution is to install an autotransformer to be 
connected to existing 345kV Line Y88, and future Line Y66, to provide an additional source to the 
existing Lovett 138kV Substation.  


--- Page 241 ---
 
 
EXHIBIT____ (EIOP-1) 
 
 
PAGE 32 OF 132 
 
 
 
32 
Relationship to 5-Year and Long-Range Plans and Enterprise Risk Management Strategy 
 
Transmission system reliability 
The proposed Lovett 345kV substation will provide another interface into O & R’s Eastern Division, 
particularly the eastern load pocket and will relieve the loading on the existing 400 MVA 345/138kV 
transformer banks in the area during normal operation. During contingency conditions, the double 
tower loss of Line 67 and 68 will de-energize Bank 194 and Bank 455, respectively, forcing the power 
flow though the Ramapo Banks (1300 & 2300) and the South Mahwah Bank (Bank 258). At this 
contingency condition, Line 60 and Line 652 will exceed their respective short-term emergency ratings.  
This will necessitate significant load shedding in the eastern division to reduce the flow on these lines 
to below their normal ratings. By installing the North Rockland Bank, the overloading on Line 60 and 
Line 652 is not expected to occur within the next 30 years. Also, the reactive power from the bulk 
power system flowing through the North Rockland Bank will mitigate voltage problems on the various 
138kV and 6 kV eastern division busses at various single contingency conditions during the summer 
peak. 
 
 
 
 
2. Supplemental Information 
 
Alternatives 
 
Alternative 1 description and reason for rejection 
Several alternatives have been investigated, including the installation of a new 138kV line from the 
existing Hillburn Substation, to the proposed Pomona Substation, and on to the West Haverstraw 
Substation.  This route is a total of approximately fifteen (15) miles.  Since the proposed route would 
cross mountainous terrain as well as New York State and Rockland County parklands the majority, if 
not all, of this new transmission line would have to be placed underground at a considerable cost.  In 
addition, a 138kV Yard would need to be established at the Hillburn Substation as currently there is only 
a 69kV Yard at Hillburn.  The total cost of both these projects would far exceed the cost of the proposed 
project without the added benefit of an additional source into the O&R transmission system from the 
bulk power system.  
 
Alternative 2 description and reason for rejection 
Non-Wire Alternatives (“NWA”): The Company applied the NWA Suitability Criteria matrix and 
determined that Lovett 345kV Station project was not a suitable project for an NWA solution. The electric 
delivery system in the area does not meet the Company’s design standards presently; that is, the need is 
now and not a future forecasted need that could allow the Company to possibly plan for and implement 
potential cost-beneficial alternative solutions. The substantial amount of capacity deficit to address 
contingency and operating problems that both the Company and the NYISO have identified are issues 
that are not practical for NWA solutions to address. As discussed in prior testimony, the Company 
utilized Navigant as a third-party consultant to review the project need and determine project suitability 
pursuant to the Company’s NWA Suitability Criteria Matrix. 
 
Risk of No Action 
 
Risk 1 
No action results in a security violation on the O&R transmission system at system peak with the 
simultaneous loss of Lines 67 and 68.  During this contingency, Lines 60 and 652 would be loaded 


--- Page 242 ---
 
 
EXHIBIT____ (EIOP-1) 
 
 
PAGE 33 OF 132 
 
 
 
33 
above their emergency ratings and the contingency situation will only worsen as load continues to 
grow in the area. 
 
Non-Financial Benefits 
The completion of the Lovett 345kV Station will greatly improve the capacity and reliability of the 
O&R transmission system.  By providing an additional source into the transmission system in the 
North Rockland area, the overloading of Lines 60 and 652 will no longer occur with the loss of Lines 67 
and 68 at system peak.  Also, the reactive power from the bulk power system flowing through the 
Lovett 345/138kV Bank will mitigate voltage problems on various 138kV and 69kV Eastern Division 
busses during (other) various single contingency conditions during system peak. 
 
Summary of Financial Benefits and Costs (attach backup)

3. Total cost 
 
The Total Cost for this project is $53.4 million.  
 
4. Basis for estimate 
 
The cost basis for this project estimate is similar historical project costs as well as vendor proposals for 
engineering, design, major equipment, material procurement, and construction. 
 
5. Conclusion 
 
This project must be completed in order to meet design standard and address the potential simultaneous 
loss of Lines 67 & 68 and to achieve improved capacity and reliability of the O&R transmission system. 
 
Project Risks and Mitigation Plan 
 
Risk 1: Permitting delays.  This risk can potentially delay the start of field construction and/or result in 
project cost overruns or failure of the project entirely.    
                                  
Mitigation plan: Risk avoidance by defining permit required dates and ownership for each permit. 
Obtain input from contractors, review past projects and experience of others to establish a realistic 
schedule. 
 
Risk 2: Transmission Outage Requests.  Potential scheduling conflicts or delays with respect to 
obtaining the required outages for associated work on Lines Y88 and Y94. This can cause delays in the 
project schedule and planned energization date.  
 
Mitigation plan: Risk avoidance by determining outages required as soon as practical and submitting 
outage requests as soon as possible.

Based on the RNA study that the NYISO performed, the loss of Lines 67 and 68 will de-energize 
345/138kV Bank 194 at West Haverstraw and Bank 455 at Bowline forcing the power flow through 
345/138kV Ramapo Banks 1300 & 2300 and South Mahwah Bank 258. During this contingency 
condition, 138kV Line 60 and 69kV Line 652 will exceed their emergency ratings.  This will necessitate 
significant load shedding in Orange and Rockland’s Eastern Division to reduce the flow on these lines

to below their normal ratings. By installing the Lovett 345/138kV Bank, the overloading on Line 60 and 
Line 652 is not expected to occur within the next 30 years and therefore no-load shedding would need 
to occur during the loss of Lines 67 and 68. Also, the reactive power from the bulk power system 
flowing through the Lovett 345/138kV Bank will mitigate voltage problems on various 138kV and 
69kV Eastern Division busses during (other) various single contingency conditions during system 
peak.
