1. Project / Program Summary 
 
Type:  ☒ Project  ☐ Program  
Category:  ☒ Capital  ☐ O&M 
Work Plan Category:  ☐ Regulatory Mandated  ☒ Operationally Required  ☐ Strategic 
Project/Program Title:  Silver Lake Bank 2113 Upgrade 
Project/Program Manager: Frank Glynn 
Project/Program Number (Level 1): 23204819 
Status:  ☒ Initiation  ☐ Planning  ☐ Execution  ☐ On-going  ☐  ☐ Other: ___________ 
Estimated Start Date:  March 2021 
Estimated Date In Service: June 2023 
A. Total Funding Request ($000)  
Capital: $3,970.6 
O&M:  
 
B.  
☐ 5-Year Gross Cost Savings ($000) 
☐ 5-Year Gross Cost Avoidance ($000) 
O&M: 
Capital: 
C. 5-Year Ongoing Maintenance Expense ($000) 
O&M: 
Capital: 
D. Investment Payback Period:   
(Years/months) (If applicable) 
Work Description: 
      The Silver Lake Bank 2113 Upgrade will include the replacement of the existing 25 MVA, 69kV to 
13.2 kV distribution transformer with a new 40 MVA, 69kV to 13.2 kV transformer.  The scope of work 
will also include the installation of a new concrete foundation and associated appurtenances such as 
new conduits and modifications to the bus duct to connect the new transformer to the existing 
distribution switchgear.  
 
 
Justification Summary: 
       The Silver Lake Substation is a two-bank substation that serves approximately 7,800 customers in 
the Western Division of the Orange and Rockland Service territory. Bank 1113 is a 35 MVA, 69kV to 
13.2kV transformer with a normal rating of 48 MVA and a Long-Term Emergency (“LTE”) rating of 58 
MVA. Bank 2113 is a smaller 25 MVA, 69kV to13.2kV transformer with a normal rating of 32 MVA and 
an LTE rating of 38 MVA. The 2020 Weather Normalized (“WN”) forecasted peak loads for each bank 
are 19.9 MVA and 18.1 MVA respectively, with a total station load of 38.0 MVA. There is a 3,000 amp, 
13.2kV bus tie between the banks with a normally open tie breaker. For a loss of either bank, the bus tie 
breaker will close automatically and restore the lost load. 
 
For a loss of Bank 2113 at peak time, Bank 1113 can support the entire station load (38MVA in 2020) 
and remain within its normal rating. For a loss of Bank 1113, Bank 2113 would exceed its normal rating 
and be at/over its LTE rating. Loading Bank 2113 above its LTE rating would require system operators 
to shed load to avoid damage to the bank. This would result in many customers being interrupted 
within the Route 211 corridor, consisting of several large shopping centers, grocery stores, nursing 
homes, and large industrial customers. In addition, a large industrial customer is expanding 
production and a new large commercial development is expected to move forward (the 92-acre Golden 
Triangle development project). This new load growth will exacerbate the overload condition, which 


--- Page 246 ---
 
 
EXHIBIT____ (EIOP-1) 
 
 
PAGE 37 OF 132 
 
 
 
37 
will require additional load shedding to keep Bank 2113 below its LTE rating.  When the station was 
upgraded from a single-bank to a two-bank station in 2006, the existing 25MVA Bank 2113 was not 
replaced as it was not necessary at that time and its exclusion reduced overall project costs.  Deferring 
the replacement of Bank 2113 beyond 2023 will start to impact area reliability negatively as loads 
increase. By 2023, Bank 2113 will have been in service 50 years and will be approaching the end of its 
expected service life. The replacement of Bank 2113 will reduce the exposure on the system during 
peak loading conditions if there is a long-term outage of Bank 1113. 
 
Relationship to 5-Year and Long-Range Plans and Enterprise Risk Management Strategy 
     The project is in alignment with the Company’s goals to goals to increase reliability through 
meeting design standard and upgrade aging infrastructure along with increasing hosting capacity to 
promote Climate Leadership and Community Protection Act (“CLCPA”) goals.  
         
 
2. Supplemental Information 
 
Alternatives 
1. This project is needed to maintain area reliability and address equipment obsolesce. As a 
result, the project does not pass the suitability criteria as a project type for a Non-Wires 
Alternative (“NWA”) deferral. 
  
2. Replacement of Bank 2113 has already been deferred from 2006 (when the second 69kV to 
13.2kV bank was installed (Bank 1113). Deferring the replacement of Bank 2113 beyond 2023 
will begin to impact area reliability negatively as loads increase. By 2023, Bank 2113 will have 
been in service 50 years and will be approaching the end of its expected service life.   
 
Risk of No Action 
       Failure to replace Bank 2113 will put the distribution system served out of the Silver Lake 
Substation at an ever-increasing risk of extended outages with the loss of Bank 1113 under peak load 
conditions. 
 
Non-Financial Benefits 
 
       This project improves power system performance, reliability, and efficiency.  Further, it increases 
local hosting capacity for distributed energy resources (“DER”), supporting the State’s CLCPA goals. 
 
 
Summary of Financial Benefits and Costs (attach backup) 
1. Cost-benefit analysis (if required) 
 
2. Major financial benefits 
 
3. Total cost 
 
The total estimated cost for this project is $4.0 million. 
 
4. Basis for estimate 
 
The cost basis for this project estimate is similar historical project costs.

5. Conclusion 
 
This project should be completed to improve reliability and decrease the number of customers 
impacted and avoid load shedding due to an outage of Bank 1113. 
 
Project Risks and Mitigation Plan

        The Silver Lake Substation is a two-bank substation that supplies approximately 7,800 customers

Type:  ☒ Project  ☐ Program

Work Plan Category:  ☐ Regulatory Mandated  ☒ Operationally Required  ☐ Strategic 
Project/Program Title: Woodbury Batteries 
Project/Program Manager: T. Buonincontri 
Project/Program Number (Level 1): 24545574 
Status:  ☒ Initiation  ☐ Planning  ☐ Execution  ☐ On-going  ☐  ☐ Other: ___________ 
Estimated Start Date: January 2021 
Estimated Date In Service: December 2023 
A. Total Funding Request ($000)  
Capital: $10,003.8 
O&M:  
 
B.  
☐ 5-Year Gross Cost Savings ($000) 
☐ 5-Year Gross Cost Avoidance ($000) 
O&M:

C. 5-Year Ongoing Maintenance Expense ($000) 
O&M: 
Capital: 
D. Investment Payback Period:   
(Years/months) (If applicable) 
Work Description: 
 
This project involves the installation of two (2) Mobile Battery Sites in the general vicinity of Acres 
Road, adjacent to the Town of Palm Tree and the Village of Woodbury. These batteries are needed to 
serve peak demand in this area until a new station (see Woodbury Substation project) can be 
constructed to serve the local communities.  
 
To accommodate the Town of Palm Tree’s planned load growth, the Company is proposing a new 
Woodbury Substation.  An extensive property search is underway, and negotiations with existing 
property owners, to subdivide and purchase a suitable substation parcel, have begun. The proposed 
Woodbury Substation is planned to be located in the general vicinity of County Rt 105, in the Village of 
Woodbury, which borders of Town of Palm Tree. The new Woodbury Substation is scheduled to go 
into service in 2025.  It will serve the Village of Kiryas Joel and Town of Palm Tree load, as well as 
some additional load currently served from the Harriman Substation.  
To provide temporary relief, one additional circuit will be constructed from the Monroe Substation 
using a spare distribution breaker position.  Also, two (2) overhead distribution projects will need to be 
completed in Monroe (Knight Street and Oakland Ave). Upon completion of these projects in 2021, 
Monroe Circuit 61-3-13 will be rerouted as an express circuit to Palm Tree.  These projects, along with 
the proposed batteries, are needed as a near-term solution for the area until property can be acquired 
and a new substation constructed.  
Following a review of the location, timing, and demand of proposed area projects, the Company 
determined that two (2) Mobile Battery sites along Acres Road need to be constructed.  The first site 


--- Page 250 ---
 
 
EXHIBIT____ (EIOP-1) 
 
 
PAGE 41 OF 132 
 
 
 
41 
would be located near the intersection of Forest Avenue and Acres Road and the second site would be 
located on Acres Road in the general area of Coronet Lake.  
 
Each location will be designed to supply a peak demand of 3 MW for a duration of four hours 
(12MWh). This load relief will prevent thermal issues on existing equipment and allow the Company 
to meet projected load demand until completion of the proposed Woodbury Substation in 2026. At that 
time, the batteries will no longer be required at that location and can be re-used at other locations or 
stored for future use.  
 
 
Justification Summary: 
The Town of Palm Tree is a newly incorporated town with approximately 7,800 electric customers that 
encompasses the former Village of Kiryas Joel and several tracts of land that were previously in the 
Town of Monroe. The Town and adjacent areas are currently served by (5) 13.2kV distribution circuits 
from the nearby Monroe and Harriman Substations. The 2019 actual peak demand for the load area 
was approximately 39 MW.  With the establishment of the new Town, zoning has changed for many of 
the parcels (previously outside the Village) from single family residential to high-density, multi-family 
residential with additional commercial use. As part of the development plan, the community recently 
completed upgrades to the water system (NYC aqueduct tap/water transmission line/filtration 
building) to serve the planned growth. At this time, the Town has approved an additional 9,000 new 
multi-family housing units and 1.6 million square feet of commercial and religious buildings. These are 
to be constructed over the next five years (2020 – 2025).  
Distribution Planning reviewed the size and location of the new projects and determined an additional 
18-25MW of power at peak will be required to serve the new load. When the new development is 
complete, the Town of Palm Tree and surrounding area will have 16,800 customers with a total load of 
64MW. Additional growth is expected as smaller older homes are replaced by new larger multifamily 
homes. Also, an additional new village is being proposed that includes several tracts of land outside of 
the Town of Palm Tree. 
The existing infrastructure in the area will not be able to support the proposed growth. By 2021, several 
circuits will be over relief rating and will not pass the Distribution Design Standard. By 2026, the 
additional load on the Harriman Substation will cause both banks (471 and 771) to fail the Distribution 
Design Standard.  By 2028, continued growth will cause the Monroe Substation banks to fail the 
Distribution Design Standard in 2028.  This assumes the new housing units will use natural gas for 
both heating and cooking requirements. If natural gas is not available, the customers will be required 
to use electricity for heating, cooking, hot water and laundry. This would result in a winter peaking 
load area that could be significantly higher than the area summer peak. This would cause the circuits 
in the area to fail the Distribution Design Standard. If the winter peak demand is large enough, both 
Harriman banks (Banks 471 and 771) and the Monroe banks (Banks 161 and 261) will fail the 
Distribution Design Standard much sooner.  
A new area substation (see Woodbury Substation project described in this Exhibit) is required by June 
2026 to meet the projected demand in the rapidly growing municipalities of the Village/Town of 
Monroe, Woodbury, Palm Tree, and Harriman. Until the substation project is completed, mobile 
batteries at two locations are required to meet the peak load needs in this area. This load relief will 


--- Page 251 ---
 
 
EXHIBIT____ (EIOP-1) 
 
 
PAGE 42 OF 132 
 
 
 
42 
prevent thermal issues on existing equipment and allow the Company to meet projected load demand 
until completion of the proposed Woodbury Substation in 2026.  
 
Relationship to 5-Year and Long-Range Plans and Enterprise Risk Management Strategy 
The project is in alignment with the Company’s goals to meet design standard and upgrade aging 
infrastructure along with increasing hosting capacity and promoting the Climate Leadership and 
Community Protection Act (CLCPA) goals.  In addition, the project addresses Corporate risks for Loss 
of Transmission and Substation facilities through the upgrade of the subject electrical systems. 
 
2. Supplemental Information 
 
Alternatives 
 
Alternative 1 description and reason for rejection 
The primary alternative would be to move the new Woodbury substation up to an earlier in-service 
date. However, the feasibility of accelerating the traditional project due to property 
constraints/permitting is not realistic. Installation of the mobile batteries allows the Company to meet 
the growing demands of this rapidly growing area until the new station can be constructed in 2026.  
 
 
Risk of No Action 
 
Risk 1 
The projected loads from area projects in some areas will exceed the thermal rating of existing 
equipment. This could result in the inability for the Company to serve new customer connections. 
 
Risk 2 
The existing infrastructure in the area will not be able to support the proposed growth. By 2021, several 
circuits will be over relief rating and will not pass the Distribution Design Standard. By 2026, the 
additional load on the Harriman Substation will cause both banks (471 and 771) to fail the Distribution 
Design Standard.  By 2028, continued growth will cause the Monroe Substation banks to fail the 
Distribution Design Standard. A combination of distribution projects, mobile batteries, and a new 
station are needed to meet area reliability needs and to serve the projected load growth.    
 
Non-Financial Benefits 
 
1. In the short term, the mobile batteries will allow the Company to meet the projected electric 
demand in the area and serve new customer connections until the substation is in service. 
 
2. Once the new station is in service, the mobile batteries can be re-used at another location or 
stored for future use.  
 
3. This project will enable further economic development in the area. 
 
4. The batteries will support meeting energy storage goals outlined in the CLCPA.

1. Project / Program Summary 
 
Type:  ☒ Project  ☐ Program  
Category:  ☒ Capital  ☐ O&M 
Work Plan Category:  ☐ Regulatory Mandated  ☒ Operationally Required  ☐ Strategic 
Project/Program Title: Line 51 Upgrade 
Project/Program Manager: J. Heady 
Project/Program Number (Level 1): 21487958 
Status:  ☐ Initiation  ☒ Planning  ☐ Execution  ☐ On-going  ☐  ☐ Other: ___________ 
Estimated Start Date:  June 2021 
Estimated Date In Service: December 2023 
A. Total Funding Request ($000)  
Capital: $6,025.2 
O&M:  
 
B.  
☐ 5-Year Gross Cost Savings ($000) 
☐ 5-Year Gross Cost Avoidance ($000) 
O&M: 
Capital: 
C. 5-Year Ongoing Maintenance Expense ($000) 
O&M: 
Capital: 
D. Investment Payback Period:   
(Years/months) (If applicable) 
Work Description: 
 
This project will replace a portion of 795 circular mils (“MCM”) aluminum conductor steel-reinforced 
(“ACSR”) conductor on Line 51 with larger conductor and relocate a portion of Line 51 to increase the 
line’s overall thermal rating by approximately 20 percent.  It will also eliminate the two (2) crossings of 
Line 51 over Transmission Lines 52 and 60 in the vicinity of the Ramapo Substation.  The increase in 
thermal rating will make the operation of Line 51 more reliable at system peak and during emergency 
conditions for at least the next 20 years. As discussed below, the elimination of these two transmission 
line crossings will result in greater reliability of the transmission system in the Ramapo area, as well as 
the Eastern Division in general.  This project has a proposed in-service date of December 2023. 
 
 
Justification Summary: 
 
Line 51 is a 138kV line that originates at the Ramapo Substation (New York) and terminates at the South 
Mahwah Substation (New Jersey). Although the majority of its five-mile run consists of 1033.5 MCM 
ACSR conductor, the limiting element is approximately 900 feet of 795 MCM ACSR conductor 
immediately outside the Ramapo Substation. Recent summer studies have indicated that a contingency 
on South Mahwah 345/138 kV Bank 258 will load Line 51 slightly above its Long-Term Emergency 
(“LTE”) rating. This situation will worsen with time as load in the South Mahwah area grows. 
 
In addition, this portion of Line 51 which is supported by wood structures, crosses over the 138kV 
Transmission Line 26 (Ramapo to Sterling Forest) and 138kV Transmission Line 60 (Ramapo to Tallman). 
A failure of one of the structures supporting Line 51 in this area could result in an outage of all three (3) 
of these 138kV transmission lines. 
 


--- Page 255 ---
 
 
EXHIBIT____ (EIOP-1) 
 
 
PAGE 46 OF 132 
 
 
 
46 
Relationship to 5-Year and Long-Range Plans and Enterprise Risk Management Strategy 
 
The project is in alignment with the Company’s goals to meet design standard, upgrading aging 
infrastructure along with increasing hosting capacity to promote Climate Leadership and Community 
Protection Act (“CLCPA”) goals. In addition, the project addresses Corporate risks for Loss of 
Transmission and Substation facilities through the upgrade of the subject electrical systems. 
 
 
2. Supplemental Information 
 
Alternatives 
 
Alternative 1 description and reason for rejection 
The Company considered transitioning and placing Transmission Line 51 underground in this area.  
The new underground cables could be sized to eliminate the LTE rating concerns, but it is a more 
costly option.  In addition, the establishment of an overhead to underground transition yard outside 
the Ramapo Substation would be difficult given the limited available space.  Such limitation is due to 
the proximity to additional adjacent transmission lines and the area being inhabited by a large 
population of timber rattlesnakes.  Obtaining the required approvals would likely be both lengthy and 
difficult.  
 
Alternative 2 description and reason for rejection 
Non-Wires Alternatives (“NWA”): The Company applied the NWA Suitability Criteria matrix and 
determined that the Line 51 Upgrade was not a suitable project for an NWA solution. The Line 51 
Upgrade combines improvement in line capacity along with risk reduction associated with the removal 
of transmission line crossings that could lead to expanded line outages upon line conductor failures.  
This risk reduction cannot be achieved with an NWA.  
 
Risk of No Action 
 
Risk 1 
 
If the forecasted line overloads persist, load shedding would need to commence during system peak or 
emergency conditions in order to prevent damage to the existing overhead conductor.  Even with load 
shedding, the exposure to a triple circuit transmission outage remains with the crossing of Line 51 over 
Lines 52 and 60. 
 
Non-Financial Benefits 
 
The replacement of approximately 900 feet of the 795 MCM ACSR portion of Line 51 with larger 
conductor will increase the thermal ratings of the Line to 278 MW (normal), 318 MW (LTE) and 334 MW 
(STE). The increase in the thermal ratings will solve the N-1 violation issue and make the operation of 
Line 51 more reliable, especially during system peaks.

1. Cost-benefit analysis (if required) 
 
2. Major financial benefits

The reliability and integrity of three (3) transmission lines originating from the Ramapo Substation are

1. Project / Program Summary 
 
Type:  ☒ Project  ☐ Program  
Category:  ☒ Capital  ☐ O&M 
Work Plan Category:  ☐ Regulatory Mandated  ☒ Operationally Required  ☐ Strategic 
Project/Program Title: Wurtsboro Substation Upgrade – 34.5kV 
Project/Program Manager: M. Licari 
Project/Program Number (Level 1): 24545586 
Status:  ☐ Initiation  ☒ Planning  ☐ Execution  ☐ On-going  ☐  ☐ Other: ___________ 
Estimated Start Date: January 2021 
Estimated Date In Service: December 2023 
A. Total Funding Request ($000)  
Capital: $1,294.7 
O&M:  
 
B.  
☐ 5-Year Gross Cost Savings ($000) 
☐ 5-Year Gross Cost Avoidance ($000) 
O&M: 
Capital: 
C. 5-Year Ongoing Maintenance Expense ($000) 
O&M: 
Capital: 
D. Investment Payback Period:   
(Years/months) (If applicable) 
Work Description: 
 
The purpose of this project is to install two 10MVA, 34.5/13.2kV pad mount transformers at the 
existing Wurtsboro Substation site and begin the conversion of the Wurtsboro area distribution circuits 
from 4.8kV to 13.2kV. Each pad mount transformer will be connected to the 34.5kV transmission 
system through a Distribution Supervisor Control and Data Acquisition (“DSCADA”) controlled 
recloser from Circuits 5-3-34 (originating at the Cuddebackville Substation) and 109-4-34 (originating at 
the Washington Heights Substation). For distribution protection and control, a DSCADA controlled 
recloser will be installed on the 13.2kV side of each transformer. This design will allow for two 
separate 13.2kV circuits to supply the Village of Wurtsboro and provide for remote monitoring/control 
capability from the Company’s Distribution Control Center. For a contingency on either transformer or 
circuit, the remaining transformer or circuit will be capable of supplying the entire station load. 
Following the installation and energization of the pad mounted transformers, Wurtsboro distribution 
Circuits 9-1-48 and 9-2-48 will be converted to 13.2kV and the existing Wurtsboro Substation will be 
retired. 
 
Justification Summary: 
 
The Wurtsboro Substation is a single-bank station that serves approximately 2,100 customers near the 
border of the O&R service territory in the Western (Northern) Division. The station contains a single 
5MVA, 34.5/4.8kV transformer (Bank 29) that feeds two (2) 4.8kV distribution circuits. Bank 29 does 
not have a load tap changer.  The 2020 weather normalized forecasted peak for the station is 3.3MVA. 
Backup for the loss of Bank 29 is primarily from three sets of 34.5/4.8kV step down transformers. For a 
bank failure at peak time, the customer hours of interruption would be approximately 7,372 hours. 
Because the Company no longer has a mobile transformer that can provide 4.8kV on the secondary 
side, the Town would remain supplied from the step-down transformers until Bank 29 could be 


--- Page 259 ---
 
 
EXHIBIT____ (EIOP-1) 
 
 
PAGE 50 OF 132 
 
 
 
50 
repaired or replaced. Without a load tap changer, it is very difficult to maintain adequate voltage 
during normal or contingency conditions. As one of three remaining substations without 
communication, there is no ability to monitor breaker status or voltage without sending a crew to the 
site to obtain this information. There are also electrical Minimum Approach Distance (“MAD”) 
concerns inside the station which requires both distribution circuits to be offloaded and de-energized 
to work on the circuit breakers. 
 
Installing the two pad mount transformers is a short-term, lower-cost solution that will allow the 
distribution circuits in area to be converted to 13.2kV. This will improve area reliability by establishing 
a 13.2kV tie with the Summitville Substation and allow for distribution automation to be deployed in 
the area. Once work is complete, for a contingency on either transformer or circuit, the remaining 
transformer or circuit will be capable of supplying the entire station load. The new devices will have 
DSCADA communication with capability of remote monitoring and control. Safety (MAD) clearance 
concerns associated with the old station will also be eliminated.  
 
Relationship to 5-Year and Long-Range Plans and Enterprise Risk Management Strategy 
 
The project is in alignment with the Company’s goals to meet design standard, upgrading aging 
infrastructure along with increasing hosting capacity to promote the Climate Leadership and 
Community Protect Act’s (“CLCPA”) goals. In addition, the project addresses Corporate risks for Loss 
of Transmission and Substation facilities through the upgrade of the subject electrical systems. 
 
2. Supplemental Information 
 
Alternatives 
 
Alternative 1 description and reason for rejection 
 
The Company considered a Non-Wires Alternative (“NWA”) for the area. However, due to the area’s 
service reliability issues, station safety concerns (MAD), lack of station communications, and a mobile 
transformer no longer available for backup, this approach did not pass the suitability criteria for an 
NWA project. 
 
Alternative 2 description and reason for rejection 
 
The Company considered moving forward with a complete rebuild of the station. To defer this more 
expensive project several years, the Company decided to install the pad mount transformers and 
convert the area to 13.2kV to improve area reliability significantly at a much lower cost.  
 
Risk of No Action 
 
Risk 1 
 
Potential extensive customer interruption hours due to no second transformer bank at the station or 
voltage compatible mobile transformer in the Company’s mobile fleet. 
 
Non-Financial Benefits 
 
The existing Wurtsboro Substation is constructed on the former D & H Canal Tow Path.  This path is 
now a park and historical walking trail.  The Village of Wurtsboro has been working with other 


--- Page 260 ---
 
 
EXHIBIT____ (EIOP-1) 
 
 
PAGE 51 OF 132 
 
 
 
51 
agencies on the restoration and revival of the former Tow Path.  The Village has been seeking to, at 
some future time, restore the Tow Path to its original location.  This can be accomplished once the old 
station is removed. 
 
Converting the area from 4.8kV to 13.2kV will also increase the area’s distributed energy resources 
(“DER”) hosting capacity which will assist in achieving the clean energy goals outlined in the CLCPA. 
 
The establishment of DSCADA communication will improve monitoring capabilities and service 
reliability in the area. 
 
Safety (MAD) clearance concerns associated with the old station will be eliminated.   
 
 
Summary of Financial Benefits and Costs (attach backup) 
1. Cost-benefit analysis (if required) 
 
2. Major financial benefits 
 
3. Total cost 
 
The total estimated cost for this project is $1.3 million. 
 
4. Basis for estimate 
 
Projects of similar scope on the Orange and Rockland transmission and distribution systems are the 
basis for this project estimate. 
 
5. Conclusion 
 
This project should be done to improve the reliability and performance of the distribution circuits in 
the Wurtsboro area.

Electric Engineering

1. Project / Program Summary 
 
Type:  ☒ Project  ☐ Program

Work Plan Category:  ☐ Regulatory Mandated  ☒ Operationally Required  ☐ Strategic 
Project/Program Title:  Little Tor Substation 
• 
Little Tor Substation 
• 
Little Tor Substation Transmission Tap 
• 
Little Tor Substation UG Circuit Exits 
Project/Program Manager: Joanne Tesik

Work Description: 
 
Orange and Rockland plans to construct a new 138kV to 13.2kV substation at the southwest corner of 
South Mountain Road and North Little Tor Road in New City, NY.  Two (2) transmission poles will 
replace an existing lattice tower on Transmission Line 541 between the West Haverstraw and Burns 
Substations to supply the Little Tor Substation 138kV ring bus with four (4) circuit breakers. The new 
substation will include two (2) 138/13.2kV, 50 MVA distribution power transformers with Load Tap 
Changers (“LTCs”), one (1) 32 MVAR capacitor bank and a metal clad switchgear with provisions for 
eight 13.2kV distribution circuits. Six (6) of these circuits will be commissioned with the station, with 
two (2) of these circuits available for future use. 
 
One of the distribution circuits will exit the substation and travel east along South Mountain Road to 
supply local customers and the Tilcon rock quarry in Congers, NY. Tilcon is currently fed from West 
Haverstraw Circuit 27-2-13 through a 13.2/34.5kV step-up transformer located near the West 
Haverstraw Substation. 
 
The project has been proceeding through an extensive New York State Environmental Quality Review 
Act (SEQRA) and municipal permitting process.  It is currently on hold due to an Article 78 proceeding 
filed by O&R against the Town of Clarkstown.  The Town Board denied the project a Certificate of 
Appropriateness under a municipal historic road statute.  Despite the delay of litigation, an in-service 
date of May 2023 remains viable. 
 
 


--- Page 264 ---
 
 
EXHIBIT____ (EIOP-1) 
 
 
PAGE 55 OF 132 
 
 
 
55 
Justification Summary: 
 
The New City area is located between the New Hempstead, Congers, and West Haverstraw Substations 
in New York. As of 2020, these three (3) substations and the temporary mobile transformer at Little Tor 
site serve a combined total of approximately 38,429 customers and 174MVA of modified load at peak 
time. Approximately 48 percent of this load is supplied from the New Hempstead Substation and the 
Little Tor mobile transformer. 
 
The construction of the Little Tor Substation is needed to address the following (described in greater 
detail below): 
• 
For New Hempstead Circuit 45-8-13 to meet design standard and avoid cascade switching or 
manual load shed in the event of a contingency during peak time; 
• 
Provide relief to Congers Circuits 22-1-13 and 22-4-13; 
• 
Allowing Circuit 27-2-13 to pass the Distribution Design Standard while retiring a long outage-
prone line; and  
• 
Allow for the mobile substation to be returned to the company emergency fleet. 
  
The New Hempstead Substation has two (2) 50 MVA, 138kV to 13.2kV transformer banks with ten (10) 
distribution circuits. The 2020 weather-normalized (“WN”) forecasted modified peak loads for these 
banks are 37.2 MVA and 36.9 MVA respectively. The 2020 WN forecast for the mobile transformer at the 
Little Tor site is 8.6 MVA. With the mobile transformer in service, the New Hempstead Banks/Circuits 
pass the Distribution Design Standard. Although the banks pass the design standard, for a loss of one 
bank at peak time, the total station load exceeds the Long-Term Emergency (“LTE”) rating of the 
remaining bank. In this situation, the Distribution Control Center (“DCC”) would need to transfer load 
immediately through Distribution Supervisory Control and Data Acquisition (“DSCADA”) or 
implement manual load-shed. Although the circuits pass the design standard, New Hempstead Circuit 
45-8-13 fails design criteria and requires cascade switching to provide backup at peak time. The Little 
Tor Substation will assume the load of the mobile transformer and provide relief to the New Hempstead 
circuits. 
 
The Congers Substation has two (2) 35 MVA, 138kV to 13.2kV transformer banks.  The 2020 modified 
WN forecasted loads for both banks is below the nameplate rating with loads of 20.4 MVA and 25.5 
MVA. With the Mobile transformer at Little Tor, all the Congers circuits have 100 percent backup for an 
individual circuit contingency. When the Little Tor Substation is complete, it will provide relief to 
Congers Circuits 22-1-13 and 22-4-13. 
 
The West Haverstraw Substation has two (2) 35 MVA, 138kV to 13.2kV transformer banks. The 2020 
modified WN loads for West Haverstraw Banks 127 and 227 are 26.0 MVA and 19.4 MVA respectively. 
The substation supplies a total of eight (8) circuits (four from each bank). Circuit 27-2-13 supplies 2,562 
customers including a 13.2/34.5kV transformer that feeds a dedicated overhead line to a single customer 
(Tilcon). This overhead line travels south along the transmission right-of-way approximately 7,000 feet 
from West Haverstraw to the Little Tor Substation site. At this point, the line continues east for an 
additional 19,000 feet to the customer. Due to the length and route that this circuit takes, it has high 
exposure to tree contacts and other reliability concerns. The majority of the line is difficult to access either 
due to backyard construction, water crossings, or marshland. When an outage occurs on this line to 
Tilcon, it causes a momentary outage to all customers on Circuit 27-2-13 and it is typically a long duration 
outage for Tilcon. Feeding Tilcon from the Little Tor Substation will allow for the retirement of this line 
and allow Circuit 27-2-13 to pass the Distribution Design Standard. 
 
 
 


--- Page 265 ---
 
 
EXHIBIT____ (EIOP-1)

Relationship to 5-Year and Long-Range Plans and Enterprise Risk Management Strategy 
 
The project is in alignment with the Company’s goals to meet design standard, upgrading aging 
infrastructure along with increasing hosting capacity to promote Climate Leadership and Community 
Protection Act (“CLCPA”) goals. In addition, the project addresses Corporate risks for Loss of 
Transmission and Substation facilities through the upgrade of the subject electrical systems. 
 
2. Supplemental Information 
 
Alternatives 
 
Alternative 1 description and reason for rejection 
Continued operation of the mobile transformer: The long-term application of the mobile transformer to 
maintain local area operating requirements is neither practical nor appropriate for its intended use.  
This results in further risk to our customers as the mobile transformer is not available for potential 
future contingencies in other areas of the Company’s electric delivery system. 
 
Alternative 2 description and reason for rejection 
Non-Wires Alternatives (“NWA”): The Company applied the NWA Suitability Criteria matrix and 
determined that the Little Tor Substation was not a suitable project for an NWA solution. The area’s 
electric delivery system does not presently meet the Company’s design; that is, the need is a present 
and not a future forecasted need that could allow the Company to possibly plan and implement 
potential cost-beneficial alternative solutions. There are operating problems for local area circuits 
under contingency conditions, particularly without the mobile transformer operating. As discussed in 
prior testimony the Company used Navigant as a third-party consultant to review the project need and 
determine project suitability pursuant to the Company’s NWA Suitability Criteria Matrix.  This project 
was also put “in flight” following the Company’s assessments. The Company has expended significant 
time and resources in completing the design and permitting, as well as beginning the procurement 
process for the major long-lead equipment required for this traditional project.  The Company 
currently projects that construction will commence in Rate Year 1. 
 
 
Risk of No Action 
 
Risk 1 
The mobile transformer is installed indefinitely at the Little Tor site and not available for use in the 
event of bank contingencies on other parts of the O&R system. 
 
Risk 2 
The mobile transformer is currently at the Little Tor site providing relief and backup to the 
surrounding area. If it were removed, the surrounding circuits and banks would need to assume the 
mobile transformer’s load. As a result, the following would occur: 
• 
New Hempstead Circuits 45-8-13, 45-3-13, and Congers Circuit 22-1-13 would be over the 
conductor relief rating of 475 amps. This is the trigger in the Design Standard to act and reduce 
circuit load back to the design rating of 300 amps.  
• 
New Hempstead Circuit 45-8-13 will continue to fail the Distribution Design Standard.  
• 
The Congers Substation would no longer have 100 percent bank backup for loss of Bank 322. This 
would result in significant customer hours of interruption. 
• 
The total station load at the New Hempstead Substation would exceed the bus rating of 79.9 MVA. 
This would require the bus tie to be disabled at peak loads to prevent auto transfer for loss of a 


--- Page 266 ---
 
 
EXHIBIT____ (EIOP-1) 
 
 
PAGE 57 OF 132 
 
 
 
57 
bank. Although both banks would still pass the design standard, customers would be interrupted 
until adjacent ties could be used to reduce the area load below the bus and LTE rating of the banks 
(prior to closing the bus tie). 
• 
West Haverstraw Circuit 27-2-13 would continue to fail the Design Standard. 
• 
Customers on Circuit 27-2-13 would remain exposed to momentary outages from incidents on the 
Tilcon line until the recloser can sectionalize the fault. 
 
Non-Financial Benefits 
 
• 
Constructing the substation and removing the mobile transformer will improve reliability to the 
area and provide load relief to the Congers, New Hempstead, and West Haverstraw Substations.  
• 
Once the station is energized, the mobile transformer can be made available for system 
contingencies. 
• 
Although the New Hempstead Substation was recently rebuilt, the total station load currently 
exceeds the LTE rating of a single bank. The new substation will provide enough relief to bring the 
New Hempstead Substation load below the normal rating of one transformer. 
• 
Additional circuits in the area will allow for the expansion of DSCADA controlled smart grid 
devices to improve reliability and permit certain outage restoration to be performed from the 
control center.  
• 
West Haverstraw Circuit 27-2-13 will pass the Design Standard.  
• 
Tilcon will be supplied from the Little Tor Substation. This will reduce exposure and momentary 
outages for the 2,562 customers on circuit 27-2-13.  
• 
Once Tilcon is fed at 13.2kV from South Mountain Road, the current 34.5kV supply line, which 
traverses the environmentally sensitive area of Lake Lucille can be removed, reducing restoration 
time and O&M expense.  
• 
This project will support a number of local contractor jobs over the course of construction.

Project/Program Title:  Shoreline Protection (Lines 11/14 Pole 68)

(Years/months) (If applicable) 
Work Description: 
 
This series of projects provides for the reestablishment of eroded shoreline areas immediately adjacent 
to overhead electric transmission structures throughout the service territory. The Company completes 
assessments of all known shoreline erosion locations, in the vicinity of transmission structures, 
following major storm events to determine the potential scope of work required at each location. 
 
The overall scope of this work includes, but is not limited to, the restoration of shorelines adjacent to 
structures along brooks, streams, rivers and wetlands.  This is accomplished by re-grading, fill 
placement, or armoring of the shoreline with an engineered system potentially consisting of shot rock, 
rip-rap, crushed stone, concrete blocks and/or filter fabric.  The type of engineered system to be 
employed is dependent on specific site needs.  In some cases, structure and foundation reinforcement 
will be required. 
 
Justification Summary: 
 
Shoreline erosion in the immediate vicinity of transmission facilities will eventually compromise the 
structural integrity of the towers or poles and could ultimately compromise the reliability of these 
transmission lines.  Restoring the shoreline will reestablish the structural integrity of the transmission 
towers or poles and reduce the probability of failure of the structures due to erosion.  
 
Relationship to 5-Year and Long-Range Plans and Enterprise Risk Management Strategy 
 
The project is in alignment with the Company’s goals to meet design standard, upgrading aging 
infrastructure along with increasing hosting capacity to promote Climate Leadership and Community 


--- Page 271 ---
 
 
EXHIBIT____ (EIOP-1) 
 
 
PAGE 62 OF 132 
 
 
 
62 
Protection Act (“CLCPA”) goals. In addition, the project addresses Corporate risks for Loss of 
Transmission and Substation facilities through the upgrade of the subject civil infrastructure systems. 
 
2. Supplemental Information 
 
Alternatives 
 
The only alternative the Company considered was to relocate the towers or poles in order to re-route 
the line(s) away from the respective shoreline locations.  The costs and difficulties relative to 
permitting, easements, and construction to relocate these facilities would be significantly greater than 
the cost to restore and reinforce the shoreline. 
 
 
Risk of No Action 
 
Ultimately the wood pole embedment or tower foundations could be compromised to the point where 
the structure fails, resulting in extended outages.  Safety to the public could also be compromised. 
 
 
Non-Financial Benefits 
 
Correcting this issue will provide safe and continuous operation for both employees and the public. 
 
 
Summary of Financial Benefits and Costs (attach backup) 
1. Cost-benefit analysis (if required) 
 
2. Major financial benefits 
 
3. Total cost 
 
Total cost is estimated to be $9.9M 
 
4. Basis for estimate 
 
The Company used historical data on previous similar Company projects to determine the estimated 
cost for these projects. 
 
5. Conclusion 
 
These projects must be completed for the continued safe and reliable operation of the electric 
transmission system. 
 
Project Risks and Mitigation Plan 
 
Risk 1                                                                        Mitigation plan 
Technical Evaluation / Analysis 
 
Orange and Rockland's Transmission and Substation Engineering department in conjunction with 
consultants continue to perform field surveys to determine the extent of shoreline erosion in various 


--- Page 272 ---
 
 
EXHIBIT____ (EIOP-1) 
 
 
PAGE 63 OF 132 
 
 
 
63 
locations as well as associated restoration priorities.  Periodic evaluation will continue indefinitely for 
the Company’s transmission lines as future storm events occur.  Although the restoration design of 
each location is generally similar in scope, they are performed on an individual basis to meet site 
specific needs. 
 
Project Relationships (if applicable) 
 
Significant storm events continue to compromise transmission structures along shoreline areas 
including brooks, streams, rivers and wetlands.  Projects have historically been addressed on an 
individual basis as the Company identified shoreline erosion issues.  With the intensity of storms 
increasing in recent years, the Company has identified numerous locations as needing restoration and 
the rate of these discoveries has increased.  An overall evaluation of project locations has been 
performed to systematically combine certain work areas into single projects based on State and County 
location, permitting needs, joint use agreements, split cost agreements and priority. The Shoreline 
Protection program will centralize budgeting, permitting, scheduling, construction, and closeout of the 
various projects to the greatest extent practical.  The intent is to improve cost, schedule, and resource 
efficiencies associated with shoreline restoration projects.

1. Project / Program Summary 
 
Type:  ☒ Project  ☐ Program  
Category:  ☒ Capital  ☐ O&M 
Work Plan Category:  ☐ Regulatory Mandated  ☒ Operationally Required  ☐ Strategic 
Project/Program Title: Glen Spey – Mohican Lake Road  
Project/Program Manager: Kevin Waldron 
Project/Program Number (Level 1): 24508217 
Status:  ☒ Initiation  ☐ Planning  ☐ Execution  ☐ On-going  ☐  ☐ Other: ___________ 
Estimated Start Date: 2022 
Estimated Date In Service: 2022 
A. Total Funding Request ($000)  
Capital: $1,395.1 
O&M:  
 
B.  
☐ 5-Year Gross Cost Savings ($000) 
☐ 5-Year Gross Cost Avoidance ($000) 
O&M: 
Capital: 
C. 5-Year Ongoing Maintenance Expense ($000) 
O&M: 
Capital: 
D. Investment Payback Period:   
(Years/months) (If applicable) 
Work Description: 
 
This storm hardening (reinforced spacer cable system) overhead distribution project is the final part of 
a four-part project, designed to construct a new three-phase mainline distribution circuit on the Rio 3-
1N-34 circuit. This four-part project will ultimately upgrade the Rio 3-1N-34 mainline circuit to 13.2kv 
and provide for alternate sources for backup in the Mohican Lake area to improve reliability. 
 
This project will begin on Mohican Lake Road (pole 36761/55091) and will continue along Mohican Lake 
Road and down to High Road (CR-41) at junction pole 36815/53905. The scope of this project is to 
upgrade the circuit by replacing the existing three-phase primary along Mohican Lake Road and High 
Road (CR-41) with approximately 13,300 feet of three-phase, 13.2kV, (3) – 477 AAC Hendrix Spacer Cable 
along with (1) – 052 AWA messenger cable.  This will also address aging infrastructure and will require 
the replacement of aging poles. Further, the project will include the addition of switching devices to 
assist with isolating faults and reducing the number of customers impacted by outages. 
 
 
Justification Summary: 
 
The 3-1N-34 is consistently among the top-ten worst performing circuits in the Western Division due to 
the absence of any alternate source ties. The purpose of this project is to construct a new reinforced 
spacer cable three-phase mainline distribution circuit to provide backup, in the event of a contingency 
on Rio circuit 3-1N-34, to the approximately 550 customers (46% of 3-1N-34) currently fed from a radial 
in the Mohican Lake area in Lumberland, NY. The Hendrix Spacer Cable is a pre-engineered electrical 
distribution system designed for high reliability, tree contact resistant, and compact design to reduce 
tree trimming clearances. The conductors are covered with two layers of polymer designed to allow 
intermittent tree contacts without causing an outage or nuisance tripping. The conductors are 


--- Page 275 ---
 
 
EXHIBIT____ (EIOP-1) 
 
 
PAGE 66 OF 132 
 
 
 
66 
supported by a high strength messenger which provides mechanical support, a system neutral, and 
acts as a shield wire against lightning. This project also includes the addition of multiple distribution 
automation devices that will assist with restoration and fault isolation. The Hendrix Spacer Cable 
overhead system will provide storm hardening benefits to the area by being more resistant to tree 
contacts, and resiliency will be further increased in the area due to the added switching devices.  
 
 
 
Relationship to 5-Year and Long-Range Plans and Enterprise Risk Management Strategy 
 
This project is in alignment with our goals to increase reliability through storm hardening, meet design 
standard, and upgrade aging infrastructure. In addition, the project addresses Corporate risk for Major 
Storms by providing storm hardening benefits. 
 
2. Supplemental Information 
 
Alternatives 
 
Alternative 1 description and reason for rejection 
Armless wire construction versus a Hendrix Spacer Cable system was initially considered. However, 
due to the extensive tree coverage along Forestburgh Road (CR-43), and the extra expense of clearing 
vegetation to install an armless wire system, the Hendrix Spacer Cable system was the preferred design.  
 
Alternative 2 description and reason for rejection 
Underground construction was not considered due to the area being primarily overhead distribution, 
along with the additional cost associated with undergrounding.    
 
Alternative 3 description and reason for rejection 
This is a storm hardening reliability project construction design, and as such a non-wires alternative 
(“NWA”) solution would not be suitable to address it. 
 
 
Risk of No Action 
 
The existing overhead system is a radial system serving approximately 550 customers with no existing 
backup. If no action is taken, during storm conditions, the probability exists that this radial circuit 
would be lost for any tree related outage or a major vehicle accident on any single pole leaving these 
customers out of service until repairs are completed.  
 
 
Non-Financial Benefits 
 
The new reinforced spacer cable system overhead circuit will provide a back feed maintaining system 
reliability and providing additional storm hardening benefits to the surrounding area.   
 
Summary of Financial Benefits and Costs (attach backup) 
1. Cost-benefit analysis (if required)

1. Project / Program Summary 
 
Type:  ☒ Project  ☐ Program  
Category:  ☒ Capital  ☐ O&M 
Work Plan Category:  ☐ Regulatory Mandated  ☒ Operationally Required  ☐ Strategic 
Project/Program Title: Burns - 19-12-13 - 4,000 ft – UG Mainline 
Project/Program Manager: Wayne Banker 
Project/Program Number (Level 1): 23191045 
Status:  ☒ Initiation  ☐ Planning  ☐ Execution  ☐ On-going  ☐  ☐ Other: ___________ 
Estimated Start Date: 2022 
Estimated Date In Service: 2023 
A. Total Funding Request ($000)  
Capital: $2,390.3 
O&M:  
 
B.  
☐ 5-Year Gross Cost Savings ($000) 
☐ 5-Year Gross Cost Avoidance ($000) 
O&M: 
Capital: 
C. 5-Year Ongoing Maintenance Expense ($000) 
O&M: 
Capital: 
D. Investment Payback Period:   
(Years/months) (If applicable) 
Work Description: 
 
This project is to construct a new mainline (600amp) underground (“UG”) conduit system that will 
house circuit 19-12-13. The UG conduit system will consist of four (4) six-inch PVC conduits that will 
be steel reinforced and encased in concrete. Two conduits will be used for 19-12-13 and two will be 
extended to Northbrook Road to prepare for a future Burns circuit exit. The overall cable system will 
span approximately 4,000 feet from transition points to eliminate the existing overhead (“OH”) double 
circuit.  The UG conduit system will include multiple structures for cable splicing.  The cable system 
will consist of approximately 4,000 feet of three phase 750Cu, CNJ 15kV cable. 
 
Justification Summary: 
 
Burns Circuits 19-8-13 and 19-12-13 serve a total of approximately 4,500 customers in the Town of 
Clarkstown, Town of Ramapo, and Village of Spring Valley. The circuits exit the station UG and rise on 
a double circuit pole line on Pascack Road (east of the station). From there, the two circuits run north 
approximately 3,500 feet on a common OH pole line.  
 
Because the two circuits share common structures, during storm/non-storm both circuits can 
experience an outage from a single contingency (e.g., tree contact, motor vehicle accident). In many 
cases, only one circuit is affected but both need to be offloaded to complete repairs safely. During 
storms this is even more critical as multiple area circuits can be impacted further limiting tie capability. 
This results in a larger number of customers interrupted with extended restoration times. 
 
To improve both storm and non-storm reliability, all new substation circuits are designed with longer 
UG exits to a point of path diversity. In addition, the Company continues to underground selectively 
existing overhead station exits to eliminate OH double circuit construction. Consistent with this 


--- Page 279 ---
 
 
EXHIBIT____ (EIOP-1) 
 
 
PAGE 70 OF 132 
 
 
 
70 
philosophy, this project will provide storm hardening benefit by selectively undergrounding a critical 
Burns circuit. This project will provide additional value by preparing the path for a future Burns 
circuit. Because the majority of the civil system will share a common trench, the incremental cost to 
install the conduit for the future circuit is relatively low.  
 
 
Relationship to 5-Year and Long-Range Plans and Enterprise Risk Management Strategy 
 
The project is in alignment with the Company’s goals to increase reliability through storm hardening, 
meet design standard, and upgrade aging infrastructure. In addition, the project addresses Corporate 
risk for Major Storms by providing storm hardening benefits. 
 
 
2. Supplemental Information 
 
Alternatives 
 
Alternative 1 description and reason for rejection 
The Company completed a detailed review to identify alternative OH routes to exit the station. Due to 
limited circuit paths, the only option to improve storm performance in the area is to underground the 
19-12-13 to a point of path diversity. This will provide the additional benefit of preparing for a future 
Burns circuit.  
 
Alternative 2 description and reason for rejection 
This is a Storm Hardening reliability project construction design, and as such a non-wires alternative 
(“NWA”) solution would not be suitable to address it. 
 
Risk of No Action 
 
The existing OH system is constructed using a double circuit design. During storm conditions, the 
probability exists that both circuits would be lost for any tree related outage or a major vehicle accident 
on any single pole. Relocating one of the circuits UG increases reliability and storm hardens the 
surrounding area. 
 
Non-Financial Benefits 
 
The new underground mainline is required in this area to maintain system reliability and provide 
additional storm hardening benefits to the surrounding area. 
 
This project will provide the additional benefit of preparing for a future Burns circuit exit.   
 
 
 
 
Summary of Financial Benefits and Costs (attach backup) 
1. Cost-benefit analysis (if required) 
 
2. Major financial benefits

1. Project / Program Summary 
 
Type:  ☒ Project  ☐ Program  
Category:  ☒ Capital  ☐ O&M 
Work Plan Category:  ☐ Regulatory Mandated  ☒ Operationally Required  ☐ Strategic 
Project/Program Title: U/G Storm Hardening – Urd Dip – Congers Ave, Congers NY 
Project/Program Manager: Wayne Banker 
Project/Program Number (Level 1):  24792183 
Status:  ☒ Initiation  ☐ Planning  ☐ Execution  ☐ On-going  ☐  ☐ Other: ___________ 
Estimated Start Date: 2022 
Estimated Date In Service: 2023 
A. Total Funding Request ($000)  
Capital: $3,500 
O&M:  
 
B.  
☐ 5-Year Gross Cost Savings ($000) 
☐ 5-Year Gross Cost Avoidance ($000) 
O&M: 
Capital: 
C. 5-Year Ongoing Maintenance Expense ($000) 
O&M: 
Capital: 
D. Investment Payback Period:   
(Years/months) (If applicable) 
Work Description: 
 
This is a Storm Hardening project to eliminate a double circuit distribution configuration along 
Congers Ave in Congers NY, to address poor service reliability on the distribution system associated 
with both small- and large-scale storms, motor vehicle accidents, and equipment failures. This project 
will replace approximately 6,200 feet of overhead distribution with underground main-line 
distribution system between station breaker and the rise on N Congers Ave. (near pole# 60730/41967). 
The underground cable system will consist of three-phase 750cu, CNJ 15kv cable, constructed with (4) 
four six-inch scheduled 40 steel reinforced conduit system concrete encased, with a spare duct system 
for a potential future circuit. This project will improve poor reliability issues associated with the 
overhead circuits during storm events. The underground system will provide an alternate path in the 
event of a contingency issue. The project design will reduce the exposure on a double circuit pole line 
configuration and provide an alternate path to reroute a portion of the circuit that is most prone to 
damage.  
 
 
Justification Summary: 
 
The scope of this project will be to storm harden the local distribution system and eliminate a double 
circuit configuration along the distribution path on Congers Ave. Currently Congers circuits (22-7-13, 
22-4-13) are fed from a double circuit pole line along Congers Ave . The purpose of this project will be 
to underground the 22-4-13 circuit between the Congers station breaker and rise on N Congers Ave 
(pole#: 60730/41967).  Circuit 22-4-13 serves 973 customers, and circuit 22-7-13 serves 2,464 customers 
During past storms, this portion of Congers Ave has been damaged due to tree contacts, resulting in 
large scale outages. Eliminating the double circuit will improve the reliability for the Company’s 
customers in Congers, NY. 


--- Page 283 ---
 
 
EXHIBIT____ (EIOP-1) 
 
 
PAGE 74 OF 132 
 
 
 
74 
Relationship to 5-Year and Long-Range Plans and Enterprise Risk Management Strategy 
 
This project is in alignment with the Company’s goals to increase reliability through storm hardening, 
meet design standard, and upgrade aging infrastructure. In addition, the project addresses Corporate 
risk for Major Storms by providing storm hardening benefits. 
 
2. Supplemental Information 
 
Alternatives 
 
Alternative 1 description and reason for rejection 
The Company completed a detailed review to identify alternative routes. However, no options are 
available, and the underground distribution dip is the only solution.  This underground storm 
hardening project will benefit 3,437 customers, and improve public safety.   
 
Alternative 2 description and reason for rejection 
This is a Storm Hardening reliability project construction design, and as such a non-wires alternative 
(“NWA”) solution would not be suitable to address it. 
 
 
Risk of No Action 
 
The existing overhead system is constructed using double circuit design. During storm conditions, the 
probability exists that both circuits would be lost for any tree related outage or a motor vehicle 
accident (“MVA”) on any single pole. By relocating one of the circuits underground, reliability is 
increased, and the surrounding area is storm hardened. 
 
 
Non-Financial Benefits 
 
The new underground mainline is required in this area to maintain system reliability and provide 
additional storm hardening benefits to the surrounding area.  
 
Summary of Financial Benefits and Costs (attach backup) 
1. Cost-benefit analysis (if required) 
 
2. Major financial benefits

Total current estimated cost for the above-mentioned project is $3.5 million.

Historical Company capital expenditures of similar underground distribution projects.

Project/Program Title: Rio 5-3-34 URD Dip

(Years/months) (If applicable) 
Work Description: 
 
This is a Storm Hardening project to eliminate 4,500 feet of overhead (off-road construction) for circuit 
5-3-34 in Wurtsboro, NY to address poor service reliability on the distribution system associated with 
storms and equipment failure. This project will install a new underground dip just outside the 
Wurtsboro Substation (near pole# 45882/57320) and rise on Lindberg Ave (Pole# 45712/57424) 
approximately 2,600 feet in length. The underground cable system will consist of three-phase 750cu, CNJ 
35kv cable, constructed with (2) two six-inch scheduled 40 steel reinforced conduit system concrete 
encased. This project will improve system reliability associated with the overhead circuit during storm 
events. The underground system will provide an alternate path and eliminate the off-road construction. 
The project design will reduce exposure and provide an alternate path to reroute a portion of the circuit 
that is most prone to damage.  
 
 
Justification Summary: 
 
This project will storm harden the local distribution system and eliminate off-road construction just 
outside the Wurtsboro Substation. Distribution circuit (Ckt: 5-3-34) serves local distribution from 
Cuddebackville Substation and Wurtsboro (#9) and the Summitville Substation (#10). The 
Cuddebackville (Ckt: 5-3-34) circuit is approximately 11 miles in length to the Wurtsboro Substation. 
The project will eliminate 4,500 feet of off-road construction just outside the Wurtsboro Substation. 
During past storms events, this portion of feeder has been severely damaged as a result of tree contacts 
and is difficult to access in the winter periods. Eliminating the off-road construction will improve 
reliability for Circuit 5-3-34 and the Wurtsboro Substation, and benefit 3,300 customers.

Risk of No Action

conditions, this area has experienced severe damage which results in long restoration times due to the 
off-road construction. If no action is taken, this section of the circuit will continue to experience major 
damage during storms with long restoration times.

additional storm hardening benefits to the surrounding area.

Project/Program Title: U/G Storm Hardening – Blue Lake Circuit Exit 
Project/Program Manager: Wayne Banker 
Project/Program Number (Level 1): 24792135 
Status:  ☒ Initiation  ☐ Planning  ☐ Execution  ☐ On-going  ☐  ☐ Other: ___________

Work Description: 
 
This is a Storm Hardening project to install a new station exit (Ckt: 68-3-2B) from the Blue Lake 
Substation to Sterling Mine Road in the Town of Tuxedo. The Company received an application for a 
new large (5MW) project to be constructed on Sterling Mine Road near the border of Orange and 
Rockland Counties. A new station exit will have to be constructed along Long Meadow Road and rise 
on Sterling Mine Road in Tuxedo. The underground feeder will be approximately 5,900 feet in length, 
and the underground cable system will consist of three-phase 750cu, CNJ 35kv cable, constructed with 
(2) two six-inch scheduled 40 steel reinforced conduit system concrete encased. This project will begin 
at Blue Lake spare station breaker (68-3-2B) and rise on pole# 52626/41870 located on Sterling Mine 
Road.     
 
This project will improve system reliability associated with the overhead circuit during storm events. 
Undergrounding the feeder will help avoid the installation of a double circuit pole line which is not 
permitted per current Company standards.  
 
 
Justification Summary: 
 
The scope of this project will be to storm harden the local distribution system and provide an alternate 
distribution path to back feed both Ringwood Substation and Blue Lake circuit (68-4-13) in the event 
of equipment failure, motor vehicle accident, or vegetation contact. The installation a double circuit 
construction is not permitted per Company design standards.  This project will benefit 1,390 customers 
for both the 68-4-13 and 78-1-13 circuits and will meet all design standards associated with contingency 
conditions.

1. Project / Program Summary 
 
Type:  ☒ Project  ☐ Program

Work Plan Category:  ☒ Regulatory Mandated  ☒ Operationally Required  ☐ Strategic 
Project/Program Title: Old Nyack Turnpike UG Ckt 21-16-13 
Project/Program Manager: Wayne Banker 
Project/Program Number (Level 1): 22594290 
Status:  ☒ Initiation  ☐ Planning  ☐ Execution  ☐ On-going  ☐  ☐ Other: ___________ 
Estimated Start Date: 2021 
Estimated Date In Service: 2023 
A. Total Funding Request ($000)  
Capital: $1,194.9

☐ 5-Year Gross Cost Savings ($000) 
☐ 5-Year Gross Cost Avoidance ($000)

(Years/months) (If applicable) 
Work Description: 
 
This project is to storm harden the local distribution system and eliminate a double circuit configuration 
along West Nyack Road. Currently West Nyack circuits (21-16-13, 21-9-13) are fed from a double circuit 
pole line along West Nyack Road. At the completion of this project, 2,200 feet of overhead (“OH”) 
construction will be removed starting on Demarest Ave and ending near the Palisades Interstate 
Parkway.  This is a storm hardening project to address service reliability on the distribution system 
associated with both small- and large-scale storms located in West Nyack NY. The underground (“UG”) 
cable system will consist of three-phase 750cu, CNJ 15kv cable, constructed with (2) two six-inch 
scheduled 40 steel reinforced conduit system concrete encased. The overall cable system will span 
approximately 2,800 feet from transition points to eliminate the existing OH double circuit. The UG 
system will include multiple structures to house the cable terminations.  This project will take advantage 
of a larger construction project (Line 705) that will be constructed along the same path on West Nyack 
Road.  Combining both major capital projects, using the same trench, will reduce overall construction 
cost and storm harden the system. 
 
 
Justification Summary: 
 
This is a storm hardening project to address service reliability on the distribution system associated with 
both small- and large-scale storms located in West Nyack, NY. Circuit 21-16-13 serves 516 customers, 
and circuit 21-9-13 serves 468 customers.  Because the two circuits share common structures, during 
storm/non-storm conditions both circuits can experience an outage from a single contingency (e.g., tree 
contact or motor vehicle accident). In many cases, only one circuit is affected but both need to be 
offloaded to complete repairs safely. During major storms this is even more critical as multiple area 
circuits can be impacted further limiting tie capability. This results in a larger number of customers 


--- Page 295 ---

PAGE 86 OF 132

interrupted with extended restoration times.  This project will improve both storm and non-storm 
reliability in the West Nyack area. 
 
 
Relationship to 5-Year and Long-Range Plans and Enterprise Risk Management Strategy 
 
This project is in alignment with the Company’s goals to increase reliability through storm hardening, 
meet design standard, and upgrade aging infrastructure. In addition, this project addresses Corporate 
risk for Major Storms by providing storm hardening benefits.

circuit 21-16-13.  
 
Alternative 2 description and reason for rejection 
This is a storm hardening reliability project construction design, and as such a non-wires alternative 
(“NWA”) solution would not be suitable to address it. 
 
 
Risk of No Action 
 
The existing overhead system is constructed using double circuit design. If no action is taken, during 
storm and non-storm conditions, the probability exists that both circuits would be lost for any tree 
related outage or an MVA on any single pole.  
 
Non-Financial Benefits 
 
The new underground mainline is required in this area to maintain system reliability and provide 
additional storm hardening benefits to the surrounding area. This underground storm hardening 
project will benefit approximately 1,000 customers, and improve public safety.

Work Description: 
 
This project is to storm harden the local distribution system and eliminate a double circuit configuration 
along Pascack Road to the intersection of Smith Road. Currently Burns circuits (19-13-13, 19-9-13) are 
fed from a double circuit pole line along Pascack Road. At the completion of this project 2,200 feet of 
overhead (“OH”) construction will be removed starting at the Burns Substation and ending at Smith 
Road. The underground (“UG”) cable system will consist of three-phase 750cu, CNJ 15kv cable, 
constructed with (2) two six-inch scheduled 40 steel reinforced conduit system concrete encased.  The 
overall cable system will span approximately 2,200 feet from transition points to eliminate the existing 
OH double circuit.  The UG system will include multiple structures to house the cable terminations.  This 
project will take advantage of a larger construction project (Line 705) that will be constructed along the 
same path as Pascack Road and Smith Road in Spring Valley. Combining both major capital projects 
using the same trench will reduce overall construction cost between the Burns Substation and Smith 
Road in Spring Valley NY.  
 
Justification Summary: 
 
This is a storm hardening project to address service reliability on the distribution system associated with 
both small- and large-scale storms located in Spring Valley NY.  Circuit 19-13-13 serves 1,621 customers, 
and circuit 19-9-13 serves 1,796 customers.  Because the two circuits share common structures, during 
storm/non-storm conditions both circuits can experience an outage from a single contingency (e.g., tree 
contact, motor vehicle accident (“MVA”)). In many cases, only one circuit is affected but both need to be 
offloaded to complete repairs safely. During storms this is even more critical as multiple area circuits 
can be impacted further limiting tie capability. This results in a larger number of customers interrupted 
with extended restoration times.  This project will improve both storm and non-storm reliability in the 
Spring Valley area.

Electric Engineering  
2023 
1. Project / Program Summary 
 
Type:  ☒ Project  ☐ Program  
Category:  ☒ Capital  ☐ O&M 
Work Plan Category:  ☐ Regulatory Mandated  ☒ Operationally Required  ☐ Strategic 
Project/Program Title: Little Tor Sub - Rt 45 (S Mtn to Rt 202) - 3,800 ft - Urd Dip 
Project/Program Manager: Wayne Banker 
Project/Program Number (Level 1): 23844985 
Status:  ☒ Initiation  ☐ Planning  ☐ Execution  ☐ On-going  ☐  ☐ Other: ___________ 
Estimated Start Date: 2023 
Estimated Date In Service: 2024 
A. Total Funding Request ($000)  
Capital: $2,390.2 
O&M:  
 
B.  
☐ 5-Year Gross Cost Savings ($000) 
☐ 5-Year Gross Cost Avoidance ($000) 
O&M: 
Capital: 
C. 5-Year Ongoing Maintenance Expense ($000) 
O&M: 
Capital: 
D. Investment Payback Period:   
(Years/months) (If applicable) 
Work Description: 
 
In order to meet the distribution design criteria and reliably serve the area, a new substation (see Little 
Tor Substation project) will be constructed in New City, N.Y. The new substation will be built with two 
– 50MVA, 138/13.2kV transformers with ten distribution circuits. Six of the circuits will be placed in 
service initially, with four provisioned for future use. This project is required to provide a circuit path 
for one of the six distribution circuit exits. The underground (“UG”) construction will start at the 
intersection of RT 45 & South Mountain Road and continue approximately 3,800 feet along RT 45 to the 
intersection of RT 45 & Old RT 202. UG construction is needed for circuit path diversity and to avoid 
overhead double circuit construction (which is no longer Company standard design). When complete, 
this circuit will serve both the Pomona and Mt Ivy / Garnerville area and provide relief to New 
Hempstead circuit (45-5-13) and the West Haverstraw circuit (27-7-13).   
 
This project will construct a new mainline (600amp) UG conduit system. The UG conduit system will 
consist of two six-inch PVC conduits. The conduit system will be steel reinforced and encased in 
concrete.  The overall cable system will span approximately 3,800 feet from transition points to 
eliminate the future installation of an overhead double circuit.  The UG conduit system will include 
multiple structures to house the cable terminations.  The cable system will consist of approximately 
3,800 feet of three phase 750Cu, CNJ 15kV cable.  
 
 
Justification Summary: 
 
The New City area is located between the New Hempstead, Congers, and West Haverstraw Substations.  
As of 2020, these three substations and the temporary mobile transformer at Little Tor site serve a 
combined total of approximately 38,500 customers and 174MVA of modified load at peak time. 


--- Page 303 ---
 
 
EXHIBIT____ (EIOP-1) 
 
 
PAGE 94 OF 132 
 
 
 
94 
Approximately 48 percent of this load is supplied from the New Hempstead Substation and the Little 
Tor mobile transformer. 
 
The construction of the Little Tor Substation is needed to address the following: 
• 
For New Hempstead Circuit 45-8-13 to meet design standard and avoid cascade switching or 
manual load shed in the event of a contingency during peak time; 
• 
Provide relief to Congers Circuits 22-1-13 and 22-4-13; 
• 
Allow Circuit 27-2-13 to pass the Distribution Design Standard while retiring a long outage-
prone line; and  
• 
Allow for the mobile substation to be returned to the Company’s emergency fleet to be used for 
its intended purpose. 
 
This project is required to provide a circuit path for one of the six distribution circuit exits for the new 
substation. UG construction is needed to provide circuit path diversity and avoid double circuit 
construction (which is no longer Company standard design). When complete, this circuit will serve 
both the Pomona and Mt Ivy / Garnerville area and provide relief to New Hempstead circuit (45-5-13) 
and the West Haverstraw circuit (27-7-13).   
 
 
Relationship to 5-Year and Long-Range Plans and Enterprise Risk Management Strategy 
 
This project is in alignment with the Company’s goals to increase reliability through storm hardening, 
meet design standard, and upgrade aging infrastructure along with increasing hosting capacity to 
promote CLCPA goals. In addition, the project addresses Corporate risk for Major Storms by providing 
storm hardening benefits. 
 
 
2. Supplemental Information 
 
Alternatives 
 
Alternative 1 description and reason for rejection 
The Company completed a detailed review to identify alternative routes. However, no options would 
benefit the circuit other than installation of an express UG feeder to serve the load. Any other overhead 
design would result in a double circuit, which would reduce storm resiliency.  
 
Alternative 2 description and reason for rejection 
Non-wires alternative (“NWA"):  The Company applied the NWA Suitability Criteria matrix and 
determined that the Little Tor Substation was not a suitable project for an NWA solution. This 
conclusion was also reached by an independent third-party consultant. 
 
Risk of No Action 
 
The existing infrastructure in the area is not capable of supporting the proposed growth.  If growth in 
the area continues, the increased load will cause the distribution circuits to fail the Company’s 
Distribution Design Standard. 
 
 
 


--- Page 304 ---
 
 
EXHIBIT____ (EIOP-1)

95 
Non-Financial Benefits 
 
The new UG mainline extension is required in this area to maintain system reliability and to meet 
distribution planning criteria requirements for the distribution circuits that serve the surrounding area. 
Since this project will be installed UG, additional storm hardening benefits will be realized.  
 
Summary of Financial Benefits and Costs (attach backup)

2. Major financial benefits 
 
3. Total cost 
 
Total current estimated cost for the above-mentioned project is $2.39 million. 
 
4. Basis for estimate 
 
Historical Company capital expenditures relating to similar underground distribution projects. 
 
5. Conclusion 
 
This project should be done to improve the reliability and provide load relief to several distribution 
circuits in the West Haverstraw area.
