K. 5-Year Ongoing Maintenance Expense ($000) 
O&M:  
Capital: $800 
L. Investment Payback Period:   
(Years/months) (If applicable) 
Work Description: 
 
The Managed Charging program will incentivize electric vehicle (“EV”) owners to charge at off-peak 
times which will encourage EV adoption, help drivers realize the benefits of electrification, minimize 
the impact of EVs on the grid, and ultimately support electrification as part of a broader clean energy 
future. The Company proposes to recover these costs through a regulatory asset, amortized over ten 
years, and collected via a surcharge. 
 
Through the Managed Charging program, the Company will offer incentives to eligible EV owners for 
charging in the Company’s service territory during off-peak hours (i.e., 12:00 am-8:00 am, seven days a 
week).2  
 
To implement the program, the Company will engage with a third-party vendor (or vendors) to deploy 
cost-effective solution(s) to monitor EV charging behavior, and enroll participants in the Managed 
Charging program to encourage off-peak charging. Once enrolled, the Company will verify 
participants’ charging habits, and interact with participants to encourage beneficial charging behavior. 
To verify beneficial charging behavior, the Company will confirm the location, time, and duration of 
charging via the third-party provided solution. The Company will encourage initial enrollment by 
providing enrollment bonuses up to $150 per vehicle.  
 
 
 
 
2 Customers already enrolled in O&R’s TOU rate will be eligible to participate in the SmartCharge program, 
but rewards will be adjusted to account for the cost savings achieved through TOU rate program.  
 


--- Page 369 ---
 
 
EXHIBIT ____ (EIOP-2) 
 
 
PAGE 28 OF 32 
28 
 
Participating customers will receive additional incentives based upon their usage:  
• 
$5.00 per month for active participation which includes keeping the technology solution 
activated and the EV charging in the Company’s service territory  
• 
$0.10 per kWh of charging during off-peak hours ; and 
• 
During the summer (i.e., June 1 to September 30), customers receive an additional $20 per 
month when they avoid charging between 2:00pm and 6:00pm on weekdays  
 
Annual incentives (excluding enrollment bonus costs) are capped at $500 per year per vehicle 
throughout the duration of this program.  
 
The Company will oversee and administer the Managed Charging program and will rely on third-
party vendors to interact with the customer and execute the program. The Company is considering 
partnering with a vendor to offer participants a device that connects to their on-board diagnostics port. 
However, if other solutions to monitor charging behavior develop and emerge (i.e., through telematics, 
smart chargers, APIs, and/or AMI), the Company could partner with other solution providers to 
execute the Managed Charging program. Regardless of the chosen technology, the third-party vendors 
will be responsible for the following activities:  
 
• 
Participant enrollment and onboarding; 
• 
Management of an online web-portal; 
• 
Quality control and fraud prevention; 
• 
Incentive payment processing and distribution; and 
• 
Development of dashboards and reports for the Company’s review.  
 
The cost of the Managed Charging Program will vary based upon participation levels. Program costs 
are projected on a per-vehicle basis: enrollment bonus costs of up to $150/vehicle, third-party costs 
estimated to be $339/vehicle/year (on average, including any system set up costs), and incentives 
estimated to be $500/vehicle/year. The Company anticipates 100 vehicles enrolled per year for the 
three-year rate period, and project spend will be capped per the schedule below. O&R will provide 
program details and enrollment instructions on the Company’s EV web page3, and will promote the 
program in partnership with selected third-party vendors through typical marketing channels which 
may include email, bill inserts, direct mail, press releases, or social media. Enrollment will be on a first 
come, first served basis. All participants enrolled in the program will receive three years of benefits. 
For instance – assuming Program Year (PY) 3 is 2024 – participants enrolled in 2024 (i.e., PY 3), will 
receive benefits through the end of 2026. These projections equate to the following expenses (and 
requests) each year:

Justification Summary: 
 
The Managed Charging Program minimizes system reliability risks, provides benefits to customers, 
and supports the Company’s pursuit of a Clean Energy Future.  
 
One way to manage the potential increased load driven by EV adoption is through a program which 
incentivizes off-peak charging, resulting in benefits for multiple stakeholders.  Through the Managed 
Charging Program, the Company can reduce peak load by incentivizing EV owners to charge during 
off-peak hours. In turn, this minimizes the risk to system reliability and reduces the need for 
infrastructure upgrades. The customer will support the program because of the financial relief 
provided by the program’s incentives. 
 
Finally, the program will provide the opportunity to gather invaluable charging behavior data, which 
will inform future EV programs focused on managed charging or vehicle-to-grid capability. The 
Company anticipates that other EV efforts, such as the Make-Ready Program, will continue to increase 
in focus as EV adoption rates increase. The Company will benefit from the experience gained from the 
Managed Charging Program.   
 
Relationship to 5-Year and Long-Range Plans and Enterprise Risk Management Strategy 
 
The program is in alignment with the Company’s goal to enhance the customer experience by 
providing a targeted, tailored program that caters to the needs of the customer. The program supports 
the Company’s support of the Climate Leadership and Community Protection Act’s (“CLCPA”) goals, 
specifically with respect to EVs. This program addresses enterprise risk by reducing the impact of new 
load (via electrification) on the distribution system.  
 
2. Supplemental Information 
 
Alternatives 
 
Alternative 1 description and reason for rejection 
The Company could perform a version of this program using outreach and education to motivate EV 
owners to use the Company’s time-of-use rates. However, without a method to track participants’ 
charging habits (through existing or emerging tech), the Company would not have insight into EV 
owner charging behaviors to administer the incentives. The elimination of incentives for this approach, 
too, would likely limit the success of the program. The Company does not recommend this alternative 
as it may result in higher costs to customers. It also  does not provide the Company with valuable 
insights on charging behavior and customer willingness to pay which will inform the Company’s EV 
efforts and support the State’s clean energy programs and goals. 
 
Alternative 2 description and reason for rejection 
An alternative approach for this program would be to lower or remove the enrollment bonus and / or 
limit the incentives available to participating customers. Removing the enrollment bonus would 
produce a significant barrier to entry for the customer and could prevent the program from gaining 
critical mass.

Risk of No Action 
 
Risk 1 
 
If the Company takes no action with respect to the Managed Charging program, the increased load 
resulting from EV adoption may lead to an increase in peak demand.  
 
Risk 2 
 
If the Company takes no action with respect to the Managed Charging program, EV adoption in the 
Company’s territory will likely be sub-optimal, which will impact the State’s ability to achieve its clean 
energy goals. 
 
Non-Financial Benefits 
 
• 
Optimize the use of installed capacity driven by reduced peak demand. 
• 
Establish communication between the Company, its customers, and EV owners, potentially leading 
to future collaboration and programs. 
• 
Lessons learned regarding the EV as a deployable asset that may be used for future managed 
charging or vehicle-to-grid integration.

4. Basis for estimate 
 
This estimate is based on discussions with solutions providers, benchmarking with peer utilities, and 
consulting with internal teams that manage similar, incentive-based adoption programs. The estimate 
includes the following per-vehicles cost components: enrollment bonus costs, third-party vendor costs, 
and incentives. This estimate assumes full enrollment at 100 new vehicles each year for three years.  
 
5. Conclusion 
 
The Managed Charging program is a targeted program that can positively impact the Company’s grid 
operations and support CLCPA’s goals. It connects the Company with participating customers in a 
new way and facilitates a relationship that can be fostered for future programs.

1. Project / Program Summary 
 
Type:  ☒ Project  ☐ Program

Project/Program Title: ADMS

22081626

Capital: $16,462.96

☐ 5-Year Gross Cost Avoidance ($000)

(Years/months) (If applicable) 
Work Description: 
 
The Advanced Distribution Management System (“ADMS”) is a foundational tool for enabling future 
functionality in Distribution Operations. The system combines distribution management applications 
with supervisory control and data acquisition (“SCADA”) and integrates with the Outage 
Management System (“OMS”) to form a single, integrated, unified platform. This system provides a 
technology platform with a shared network model and a common user experience for all roles that are 
required to monitor, control and optimize the secure operation of the electrical distribution network. 
The project has been broken down into multiple phases. 
 
This rate period request specifically focuses on increasing capabilities by building on the functionality 
established during the previous phase of the project.  This phase entails installing and deploying 
advanced applications of the ADMS. The functionality provided by these applications collectively 
enable the real-time monitoring and control that is critical to enabling the continued expansion of 
enhanced operational capabilities and market functionality across the electric delivery system. The 
Company is planning for the deployment of the following applications: 
• Load Flow; 
• Network Model Power Flow; 
• State Estimator; 
• Short Circuit Analysis; 
• Fault Location, Isolation, and Service Restoration (“FLISR”); 
• Feeder Reconfiguration; 
• Fault Protection Analysis; and 
• Volt VAR Optimization (“VVO”). 
   


--- Page 377 ---
 
 
EXHIBIT __ (EIOP-3) 
 
 
PAGE 4 OF 23 
4 
 
The timeline and sequence of the development of specific applications is based on the modelling, data 
access, and contingent applications and functionality. 
 
Justification Summary: 
 
ADMS is a fundamental component to enabling the Distributed System Platform (“DSP”). An ADMS 
can identify, monitor, perform real-time analysis, and record data from abnormal system conditions 
resulting from planned and unplanned events that modify the design configuration of the electric 
delivery system. An ADMS enhances situational awareness, and through monitoring and control 
(“M&C”), improve reliability, resiliency, and system efficiency. It will also act in near real-time to 
coordinate through external interfaces, equipment, and communications to administer FLISR and 
VVO.  
 
The ADMS advanced applications will enhance the visibility, monitoring and control that the 
Company has across the system. In addition, some applications such as the State Estimator are critical 
to advance the DSP. The state estimator application, once functional, will be able to identify congestion 
on the system and highlight issues impacting DERs, both during normal and contingency conditions. 
 
The FLISR application as part of the ADMS will reduce the number of sustained customer 
interruptions and improve key reliability metrics (e.g., System Average Interruption Frequency Index 
(“SAIFI”) and System Average Interruption Duration Index (“SAIDI”)). Through central control logic, 
FLISR provides a switching plan for re-energizing portions of a distribution system that have been de-
energized as a result of a permanent feeder fault. Once the switching plan is operational, FLISR will 
execute the plan automatically to restore service to the maximum number of customers possible, 
typically within minutes, following the initial fault occurrence. 
 
The Company envisions that the second significant initiative realized with the new ADMS, will be 
VVO, which provides for improved system operating efficiency and voltage operating levels across the 
load cycle. This will be accomplished by integrating automation and smart device initiatives in both 
the substation and distribution environments of the electric delivery system. 
 
The implementation of these ADMS advanced applications and others will help the Company realize 
ADMS’s full potential, provide more responsive and reliable service to customers, and better integrate 
DERs. 
 
 
Relationship to 5-Year and Long-Range Plans and Enterprise Risk Management Strategy 
 
The project is in alignment with the Company’s goals to increase hosting capacity and integrate DER to 
promote the Climate Leadership and Community Protection Act’s (“CLCPA’s”) goals.

prevent the Company from taking full advantage of the ADMS investment that O&R has already made 
as part of the previous phase of the project. 
 
Risk of No Action

increase reliability and integrate DERs while also not fully realizing the capabilities and benefits 
available to O&R from its ADMS investment.

Electric Engineering 
 2022  
1. Project / Program Summary 
 
Type:  ☐ Project  ☐ Program  
Category:  ☒ Capital  ☒ O&M 
Work Plan Category:  ☐ Regulatory Mandated  ☒ Operationally Required  ☐ Strategic 
Project/Program Title: One (1) ADMS Engineer and One (1) Sr. Systems Analyst 
 
Project/Program Manager: Keith Brideweser 
Project/Program Number (Level 1): N/A 
Status:  ☒ Initiation  ☐ Planning  ☐ Execution  ☐ On-going  ☐  ☐ Other: ___________ 
Estimated Start Date:  Jan 2022 
Estimated Date In Service: N/A 
A. Total Funding Request ($000)  
Capital: $82.5 (annually)  
O&M: $137.5 (annually) 
 
B.  
☐ 5-Year Gross Cost Savings ($000) 
☐ 5-Year Gross Cost Avoidance ($000) 
O&M:

C. 5-Year Ongoing Maintenance Expense ($000) 
O&M: $575.4 
Capital: 
D. Investment Payback Period:   
(Years/months) (If applicable) 
Work Description: 
 
The Company requires an additional engineer to manage the Advanced Distribution Management 
System (“ADMS”) and the Distribution Supervisory Control and Data Acquisition (“DSCADA”) 
system. Responsibilities of this engineer will include: 
• 
Supporting the deployment of additional remote terminal units (“RTU”); 
• 
Developing and configuring data views for Operators of the ADMS system and GRID 
engineers; 
• 
Providing distribution automation device commissioning, control, and alarm documents for 
the field technicians and Distribution Operators; and 
• 
Developing and maintaining data maps, alarms, and events for new and existing equipment 
such as Volt VAR Optimization (“VVO”) controls, electric grid sensors, and distributed 
generation (“DG”) interconnections 
 
The Company also requires an additional systems analyst to support the integration of ADMS with the 
Company’s Outage Management System (“OMS”), the Oracle Network Management System (“NMS”). 
Responsibilities for this systems analyst will include: 
• 
Testing and technical/functional support; 
• 
Supporting the integration, enhancement, and maintenance of associated adapters as the 
Oracle NMS system undergoes upgrades; 
• 
Supporting electric model maintenance and reconciliation among Oracle NMS, O&R 
Geographic Information System (“GIS”), and ADMS; 
• 
Supporting the end-to-end testing of the OMS ecosystem (a semi-annual regulatory 
requirement); and 
• 
Supporting enhancements to the end-to-end testing process to include ADMS. 


--- Page 382 ---
 
 
EXHIBIT __ (EIOP-3) 
 
 
PAGE 9 OF 23 
9 
 
 
The Distribution Control Center (“DCC”) will use the ADMS system to monitor and control 
distribution automation devices.  This system will provide the DCC with visibility into the distribution 
system and the ability to place devices into live line clearance mode for safe work practices. The data 
the system will provide will allow Operators to respond to system alarms, view meter readings, and 
switch the distribution system in near to real time operation.  
 
The Company is installing and implementing a new ADMS which has components to extend and 
replace the DSCADA System.  The ADMS and DSCADA systems will support the objectives of the 
New York Public Service Commission (“PSC”) Reforming the Energy Vision (“REV”) initiative for 
Distributed System Platform (“DSP”) enablement, as documented in the Company Distributed System 
Implementation Plan (“DSIP”).  Distribution automation with centralized model-based control is a 
cornerstone to O&R’s support of the Climate Leadership and Community Protection Act’s (“CLCPA”) 
goals and principles.  The engineer will be responsible for completing all system modifications by 
adding, modifying, and configuring distribution automation devices which are introduced onto the 
Distribution System.  This includes devices connected to the system to monitor and control Distributed 
Energy Resources (“DERs”).   
 
In addition to support for ADMS, the engineer will be responsible for providing support, testing, and 
development on the current DSCADA system. Currently there are over 950 DSCADA commissioned 
RTUs.  With continuing deployment of automation equipment, the Company estimates it will have 
over 1,300 units on its system by the end of 2021.  These numbers do not include the expansion of VVO 
equipment and projected inclusion of DERs into DSCADA.  On the New York distribution system, the 
Company has currently deployed around 492 cap banks and 104 voltage regulators, all will need 
DSCADA commissioning.  Automation expansions and the need for data is moving the Company to 
deploy more RTUs of various vintages and vendors. Currently there are three vintages of RTUs 
displayed on the distribution system.  The Company expects to have newer RTU types that require 
design, evaluation, and ongoing support.  Therefore, the Company needs to be prepared with this new 
position to support the additional RTUs being deployed with the distribution DSCADA expansion. 
 
 
The annual cost for the ADMS Engineer is $110,000 per year and will be allocated 75% capital and 25% 
O&M. 
 
The annual cost for the Sr. Systems Analyst is $110,000 per year and will be allocated 100% to O&M. 
 
Justification Summary: 
 
The increasing number of DER projects, rapid deployment and integration of distribution automation 
devices, and amplified loading for monitoring and control of field equipment have increased the 
requirements of the ADMS system.  The DSCADA system will soon be insufficient to monitor and 
control the devices due to their number and complexity. The Company is currently in the process of 
installing and implementing an ADMS.  The ADMS is a more complete and sophisticated centralized 
control system and will require additional training, configuration, integration, and translation.  As a 
model-based system with telemetry, the ADMS will provide additional opportunities for statistical 
modelling and data analytics. The Go-Live schedule for the ADMS Phase 1 is the first quarter of 2021. 
 
As the automation and integration of devices on the distribution system increases, the volume and 
demand for additional communication interfaces, operational ADMS support, and SCADA interfaces 
will expand.  The ADMS engineer will be tasked with developing and supporting the new ADMS and 
its components while maintaining and expanding the current DSCADA System. The Sr. Systems

2. Supplemental Information 
 
Alternatives 
 
Alternative 1 description and reason for rejection 
There is no alternative to hiring the ADMS Engineer and the Sr. Systems Analyst. An additional 
engineer is needed to check and verify any integration of devices before they enter the system. 
Currently the same person both introduces and promotes device integrations into the system. Also, the 
acceleration of the number of devices that need to be introduced and commissioned in the DSCADA 
environment has overwhelmed the work capacity of the single engineer. An additional Sr. Systems 
Analyst is needed to support the integration of ADMS and Oracle NMS. Without these additional 
positions, either the integration and automation work will not be completed, or the quality of the work 
will be diminished. 
 
Risk of No Action 
 
Risk 1 
Due to the increase in the workload, and without the separation of duties, there is a risk to the stability 
and availability of the system.

provide timely and accurate status of outages to its customers.

1. Project / Program Summary 
 
Type:  ☒ Project  ☐ Program  
Category:  ☒ Capital  ☐ O&M 
Work Plan Category:  ☐ Regulatory Mandated  ☐ Operationally Required  ☒ Strategic 
Project/Program Title: NYSERDA PON 4074 Westtown Distribution Automation 
Project/Program Manager: Ben Brown 
Project/Program Number (Level 1): 24545750 
Status:  ☐ Initiation  ☐ Planning  ☒ Execution  ☐ On-going  ☐ Other: ___________ 
Estimated Start Date: 11/1/2020

Capital: $1,988

(Years/months) (If applicable) 
Work Description: 
 
The objective of this project is to deploy advanced applications and technologies that can provide 
substantial real-time situational awareness into the operating point conditions of the distribution 
system. This project will include the deployment, configuration, and testing of the applications and 
state-of-the-art technologies listed below. The testing activities will include both field testing and 
simulation testing in a pre-selected area of O&R’s Distribution System.  
 
Remotely operated switching devices will be deployed to quickly isolate and restore customers 
affected by system issues. Advanced power quality (“PQ”) sensors and remotely controllable capacitor 
banks will be optimally located to allow dynamic volt VAR optimization (“VVO”) capabilities. To 
address limitations with the current radio systems’ capacity to bring data back from the field, O&R will 
be installing and testing alternate means of communication back to the Company’s control room. This 
will address the current bandwidth issue and provide communication redundancy with field devices. 
Substation devices will also be upgraded to accept control commands from the Advanced Distribution 
Management System (“ADMS”) and collect additional data points for system modeling and historian 
applications. The project will also include a cyber security analysis of the integration all these devices 
into the ADMS. This analysis will help develop back office systems to compile and analyze the large 
amounts of data that, inevitably, will be available to engineers and control room operators. 
 
The technologies and systems being tested and deployed are listed below:  
- 
Suite of ADMS Applications 
o 
VVO 
o 
Fault Location, Isolation & Service Restoration (“FLISR”) 
o 
Switch Order Management (“SOM”) 
- 
Field Communications Infrastructure 


--- Page 387 ---
 
 
EXHIBIT __ (EIOP-3) 
 
 
PAGE 14 OF 23 
14 
 
o 
4G and/or 5G 
o 
Other(s) 
- 
Distribution Automation Equipment 
o 
Capacitor Banks 
o 
Sensors 
o 
Fault Current Indicators 
o 
Reclosers 
o 
Switches 
o 
Smart Inverters 
o 
Static VAR compensator  
 
This request is specifically for the O&R capital contribution for the project that will be implemented in 
partnership with the New York State Research and Development Authority (“NYSERDA”). 
 
Justification Summary: 
 
O&R has worked to accelerate the realization of an advanced, digitally enhanced, and dynamically 
managed electric grid. These efforts include embracing smart grid devices (e.g. modern reclosers, 
capacitor controllers, fault indicators) to enhance monitoring, control, and optimization of the grid; 
deploying smart meters and Advanced Metering Infrastructure (“AMI”) to monitor system conditions 
and outages closer to real-time; collaborating with grid sensor vendors to improve their ability to 
provide accurate visibility into the system; and implementing a modern Outage Management System 
(“OMS”) and ADMS. 
 
Despite the significant effort and positive strides taken to date, a comprehensive, single study or 
demonstration of all of these new smart devices has not yet been undertaken to fully understand how 
they might perform most efficiently in concert. As technological advances continue and more 
distributed energy resources (“DER”) are introduced into the system, the stability of an unmonitored 
grid would continue to decrease. It is imperative that O&R works to increase the visibility, control, and 
forecasting of the distribution system at pace with DER penetration.  
 
With this proposed project, O&R will pull together all the lessons learned from individual projects that 
have been completed (monitoring and control devices, AMI, PQ sensors, OMS, ADMS) and apply 
them to this project. O&R will develop the basic components of the “Distribution System of the Future” 
using a small area of the service territory that has a high penetration of DER. The goal will be to 
establish engineering and design standards to improve the reliability and resiliency of the grid, 
constantly and continuously improve the system operating point under different circumstances, 
reduce system losses, improve the visibility of connected DER, and provide more accurate and timely 
data for load flow and/or state estimation-based operating conditions analysis. 
 
The demonstration will include three inter-connected circuits from the O&R Westtown Substation, 
which will be outfitted with technologically advanced devices and systems to facilitate FLISR, VVO, 
and DER integration into Distribution Operations. The project will test multiple communication media 
and the integration of these advanced technologies into O&R’s ADMS. Effectively, this project will 
demonstrate what the “Distribution System of the Future” will look like and how the overall 
management of the system may change. Using the information gathered from this project, broader 
recommendations will be formulated to describe the optimal deployment of new technologies based on 
quantifiable feeder characteristics. The new array of devices and technologies could then be cascaded 
to other feeders in the O&R territory, and the recommendations shared across other utilities in New 
York State.  
 
 


--- Page 388 ---
 
 
EXHIBIT __ (EIOP-3) 
 
 
PAGE 15 OF 23 
15 
 
Relationship to 5-Year and Long-Range Plans and Enterprise Risk Management Strategy 
The NYSERDA Westtown project is an integral part of the Company’s goal to improve system 
resiliency and storm harden the distribution system. This goal is being accomplished by automating 
the distribution system through 1) installing field devices that have Supervisory Control and Data 
Acquisition (“SCADA”) capabilities and 2) installing a command and control system in the Control 
Center to automatically execute isolation and restoration switching steps. The NYSERDA Westtown 
project, paired with the ADMS project, will accomplish this long-term goal.  
  
The project is also in alignment with the State’s Climate Leadership and Community Protection Act’s 
(“CLCPA”) goals for enablement of DER and modernization of the distribution system. 
 
2. Supplemental Information 
 
Alternatives 
 
The purpose of this project is to test out the integration of various technologies, including 4G 
communications, into the ADMS. One alternative to testing out the integration in a small area of the 
territory and applying the lessons learned to a greater deployment is to simply deploy across the entire 
service territory and address issues as they arise. This would be inefficient and more costly than the 
systematic approach proposed in this project.  
 
 
Risk of No Action 
 
The lessons learned from this project will greatly inform the Company of the ability of the ADMS to 
execute its advanced functions which will support the Company’s efforts to design an efficient, 
resilient and storm hardened electrical distribution system and will continue to provide a positive 
customer experience. Without the lessons learned from this project, the full-scale deployment of new 
technologies and advanced functions of the ADMS will be delayed and additional money will be spent 
mitigating issues that could have been avoided.     
 
Risk 1 
New technologies and communication methods will be deployed without the benefit of testing the 
applicability and efficiency on a small scale.  
 
Non-Financial Benefits 
 
The NYSERDA Westtown project will have many non-financial benefits:  
• 
Increased safety. The Control room can mitigate dangerous situations caused by downed wires 
without sending a line crew to the location.  
• 
Improved reliability. The project will have a positive impact on SAIFI and CAIDI. Auto loop 
operations reduce the number of customers affected by system activity and remotely operated 
switches reduce outage times. 
• 
Increased system efficiency. Testing of the advanced ADMS VVO application will demonstrate 
that with the increased DER on the distribution system, the O&R system can remain efficient.   
• 
 Increased customer satisfaction. By avoiding customer outages, reducing outage duration and 
improving public safety, the Company will provide a positive customer experience.

This project will allow O&R to understand and gain valuable lessons in operating the “Distribution

benefits of its ADMS investment. 
 
Project Risks and Mitigation Plan 
 
Risk 1:  Material Procurement.                             
Mitigation plan:  Pre-ordering allows the vendor to procure parts and build the devices well before 
they are needed.  
 
Risk 2:  Storm/rain delays. This could lead to budget underruns and the annual plan not being 
completed.                                                                     
Mitigation plan:  Review previous year’s performance and account for an average number of 
storm/rain days. Manage the plan on a monthly basis to mitigate any delays.  
 
Technical Evaluation / Analysis 
 
DA Performance during Tropical Storm Isaias (8/2020) 
• 
The DCC remotely executed 426 switching steps and 264 safety related (Hot Line Tag) steps.  
• 
This equated to 690 truck rolls saved and approximately 74,000 customers seeing 
reduced outage times. 
• 
The auto-loops and mid-point reclosers operated automatically to save approximately 64,000 
customers from ever seeing an outage. 
• 
20% of O&R’s customer base did not see an outage during the storm.  
The outcomes and lessons learned from this NYSERDA project will improve upon the statistics above 
by testing out the advanced functions of the ADMS. These advanced functions will provide greater 
command and control over the devices in the field and will enable automated FLISR.

Type:  ☐ Project  ☒ Program

Project/Program Title: Grid Mod 4G – 5G Program 
Project/Program Manager: Keith Brideweser 
Project/Program Number (Level 1): 24545614 
Status:  ☒ Initiation  ☒ Planning  ☐ Execution  ☐ On-going  ☐  ☐ Other: ___________

O&M: $650.0

D. Investment Payback Period:   
(Years/months) (If applicable) 
Work Description: 
 
This white paper specifically addresses the ongoing O&M costs associated with the Company’s 4G – 
5G Program described below. 
 
Advanced communications infrastructure is integral to enabling the Company’s continued automation 
deployment and Distributed System Platform (“DSP”) implementation. Expanding high-speed 
communications infrastructure will enable O&R to provide a robust and secure solution throughout its 
service territory, which is necessary for critical utility and customer data transport among control 
centers, data centers, server farms, remote data collectors, and devices.  
 
To accommodate the communications needs of systems, applications, and devices, the Company will 
expand or enhance existing communications infrastructure to meet the needs of each application. The 
Company identified communications solutions through system, application, and device requirements 
gathering, and at the last mile the optimal solution is upgrading to 4G – 5G modems. 
 
The need to upgrade communications infrastructure is paramount to a robust system that can manage 
the amount of data available on the system and realize the low-latency real-time monitoring and 
control functionalities required by the new Distribution Supervisory Control and Data Acquisition 
(“DSCADA”), Advanced Distribution Management System (“ADMS”), and Distributed Energy 
Resources Management System (“DERMS”) systems. 
 
In the next few years, a major focus will be to advance investment and deployment of key 
communication network technologies to facilitate highly secure/low latency control, as well as 
accommodate the backhaul of large amounts of data through high bandwidth solutions. Substantial 
testing, troubleshooting, and refining of appropriate solutions will be required to integrate new and 


--- Page 393 ---
 
 
EXHIBIT __ (EIOP-3) 
 
 
PAGE 20 OF 23 
20 
 
expanded communication network technologies. This will enable more devices, such as smart 
inverters, that can be integrated into the Company’s control systems. 
 
This program will introduce the 4G – 5G based technology in the field.  The number of 4G – 5G 
modems and communication cards will follow the installation rate of distribution automation (“DA”) 
devices including reclosers, motor operated air break switches (“MOABs”), Supervisory Control and 
Data Acquisition (“SCADA”) capacitors, and high voltage Power Quality nodes. 
 
To support the capital investment in 4G – 5G modems and communication cards, this program will 
include an ongoing operation and maintenance (“O&M”) component to support devices on the system.  
These costs will include ongoing carrier costs for bandwidth and the cost for damaged modem 
replacement, including labor.   
 
 
Justification Summary: 
 
O&R leases and maintains a 220 MHz radio network which supports the last mile communication 
requirements for the DSCADA system.  It was originally chosen for its superior area coverage and 
economic advantages. The base stations were located at existing O&R owned towers.  At the time of 
conception, fewer than 300 reclosers were anticipated to be serviced by the network system wide.  
Currently the commissioned distribution devices consisting of MOABs, SCADA 
Capacitors, Power Quality Nodes, and Reclosers total nearly 1,000, and that number will continue to 
grow in the coming years. 
 
In addition to the quantity of devices and the relative density of their installations, additional data is 
available from the devices for command and control, as well as situational awareness. The ADMS, 
which is currently being implemented, will extend the capability of the Distribution Operators and 
Electric Operations through a model of the electrical system.  It will combine Advanced Metering 
Infrastructure (“AMI”) data, mapping data, substation SCADA data, and distribution automation 
measurements.  ADMS will provide an integrated view of the system which will be valuable to 
forecasting, outage restoration, system contingency planning, and system behavior analysis. 
 
O&R requires a 4G-5G solution to provide the data communication solution necessary for to operate 
the grid in a safe, reliable, and efficient manner.  It is the link that will provide the data capacity and 
frequency to ADMS from distribution automation devices.  It will provide the last mile solution for 
monitoring and control necessary to accommodate the needs generated by the multi-year investments 
in DA and the ADMS. 
 
 
Relationship to 5-Year and Long-Range Plans and Enterprise Risk Management Strategy 
 
The GRID Mod 4G – 5G Program is a critical component to the Company’s plans for DA and ADMS.  It 
provides the channel to communicate the status, measurements, and control.  This is in alignment with 
our goals to integrate distributed energy resources (“DER”) and support the achievement of the 
Climate Leadership and Community Protection Act’s (“CLCPA”) goals.  
 
 
 


--- Page 394 ---
 
 
EXHIBIT __ (EIOP-3) 
 
 
PAGE 21 OF 23 
21 
 
2. Supplemental Information 
 
Alternatives 
 
Alternative 1 description and reason for rejection 
 
Remain with the current low band 220 MHz system, which would limit the Company’s ability to 
connect additional devices at the last mile and limit the capabilities of those devices O&R has already 
connected.  
 
 
Risk of No Action 
 
Risk 1 
 
Saturate the 220 MHz system and lose communication capability from the ADMS control system to the 
DA devices. 
 
Non-Financial Benefits 
 
Increased safety, reliability, and efficiency of field forces by fully utilizing DA devices and ADMS 
applications. 
 
 
Summary of Financial Benefits and Costs (attach backup)

2. Major financial benefits 
 
3. Total cost 
 
$650,000 in 2021 - 2025 
 
4. Basis for estimate 
 
Vendor quotes, and experience supporting similar communication devices. 
 
5. Conclusion 
 
A 4G-5G solution should be implemented to provide the data capacity and frequency to take full 
advantage of the benefits provided by ADMS and other DA devices.

Control Center and Substation Operations 
2021 
1. Project / Program Summary 
 
Type:  ☒ Project  ☐ Program  
Category:  ☐ Capital  ☒ O&M 
Work Plan Category:  ☒ Regulatory Mandated  ☐ Operationally Required  ☐ Strategic 
Project/Program Title: New Software License for Metering Data Reconciliation and Reporting 
Project/Program Manager: Scott Dunwoody 
Project/Program Number (Level 1): 
Status:  ☒ Initiation  ☐ Planning  ☐ Execution  ☐ On-going  ☐  ☐ Other: ___________ 
Estimated Start Date: 2021 
Estimated Date In Service: 
A. Total Funding Request ($000)  
Capital:  
O&M: $16/year 
 
B.  
☐ 5-Year Gross Cost Savings ($000) 
☐ 5-Year Gross Cost Avoidance ($000) 
O&M: 
Capital: 
C. 5-Year Ongoing Maintenance Expense ($000) 
O&M: $15/year plus escalation required 
by vendor 
Capital: 
D. Investment Payback Period:   
(Years/months) (If applicable) 
Work Description: 
The software license requested would provide access to a software platform the Energy Control Center 
(ECC) Support Services Group (SSG) would use for daily and monthly meter reconciliation and 
uploads to NYISO and PJM. This metering software will collect and send the required data to New 
York Independent System Operator (NYISO) and PJM for compliance purposes. The selected 
application is intended for use by both Orange and Rockland and Con Edison, with Orange and 
Rockland’s portion of the license fee being $16K per year. 
 
Justification Summary: 
 
The software requested would support a new application to automate the metering reconciliation 
process, which currently is performed manually by the Orange and Rockland SSG team. SSG uses an 
antiquated access database to intake this compliance data and then send to NYISO and PJM. The 
acquisition of the requested metering software would streamline this process by providing access to 
the latest technology. 
 
Relationship to 5-Year and Long-Range Plans and Enterprise Risk Management Strategy 
 
The requested software license to support the new metering application for reconciliation and 
reporting will streamline the process by which SSG collects this compliance data and sends it to NYISO 
and PJM. Automating this process will result in time savings for SSG and improve the accuracy of the 
data reported to NYISO and PJM. 
 
 


--- Page 400 ---
 
 
EXHIBIT ____ (EIOP-4) 
 
 
PAGE 4 OF 30 
4 
 
2. Supplemental Information 
 
Alternatives 
 
Alternative 1 description and reason for rejection 
The alternative is for Orange and Rockland to continue to use the current methods for data metering 
reconciliation and reporting. Orange and Rockland does not recommend this alternative because the 
current Access database is not supported by IT and therefore cannot be updated.  Accordingly, if 
another tie-line meter is placed into service, the Company will be unable to provide the associated 
values to NYISO and PJM.  
 
Risk of No Action 
 
Risk 1 
The risk of not acquiring this software license is because IT is not supporting the current Access 
database, the Company will not be able to add new tie lines to the metering process. This presents a 
compliance risk. In addition, the Company’s current manual processes are time consuming and can 
potentially yield less accurate reporting results. 
 
Non-Financial Benefits 
• 
Ability to incorporate new metering into the reconciliation process in the future as new tie line 
meters are added. 
• 
Increased reliability and accuracy in metering data reconciliation and reporting. 
• 
Reduction of time required for metering data reconciliation and reporting. 
• 
Providing software application consistency between Con Edison and Orange and Rockland. 
 
Summary of Financial Benefits and Costs (attach backup) 
1. Cost-benefit analysis (if required) 
 
2. Major financial benefits 
   
3. Total cost 
The metering software would cost approximately $16K per year, plus escalation. 
 
4. Basis for estimate 
This estimate is based on current market trends and historical data.

Control Center and Substation Operations 
2022 
1. Project / Program Summary 
 
Type:  ☐ Project  ☒ Program  
Category:  ☒ Capital  ☒ O&M 
Work Plan Category:  ☐ Regulatory Mandated  ☒ Operationally Required  ☐ Strategic 
Project/Program Title:  Senior Specialist, Compliance / Electrician Supervisor 
Project/Program Manager: Ralph DeVitto 
Project/Program Number (Level 1): 
Status:  ☒ Initiation  ☐ Planning  ☐ Execution  ☐ On-going  ☐  ☐ Other: ___________ 
Estimated Start Date: January 1, 2022 
Estimated Date In Service: 
C. Total Funding Request ($000)  
Capital: $238.8 
O&M: $742.9 
 
D.  
☐ 5-Year Gross Cost Savings ($000) 
☐ 5-Year Gross Cost Avoidance ($000) 
O&M: 0 
Capital: 0 
E. 5-Year Ongoing Maintenance Expense ($000) 
O&M: $742.9 
Capital:  $238.8 
F. Investment Payback Period:   
(Years/months) (If applicable) 
Work Description: 
Orange and Rockland’s Substation Department is responsible for ninety substation facilities across the 
Company’s service territory.  Responsibilities include transmission/distribution equipment real time 
operation and maintenance, maintaining system reliability, emergency response, physical site/security 
maintenance, and regulatory compliance.  
 
The department’s electrician union headcount in the Eastern Division is currently 18 Full Time 
Equivalents (FTEs) with one management Electrician Supervisor.  The department is planning an 
increase of one Electrician Supervisor (starting in Rate Year 1) and one Senior Specialist – Compliance 
(starting in Rate Year 2), as described below.  This increase will serve to decrease the span of control for 
the current Electrician Supervisors and dedicate an employee to facilitate continued compliance.   
 
 
Justification Summary: 
During the past three years, the Company’s total asset inventory has increased as Orange and 
Rockland has constructed two new substations (i.e., Deerpark and Oak Street Substations) and added 
new equipment at seven existing stations/locations (see list below).  These projects placed additional 
facilities, substation equipment and associated ancillary systems into service which has increased the 
department’s workload.  This equipment requires routine periodic maintenance.  The department is 
also responsible for real time issues that arise at these facilities and for investigating/responding to 
equipment issues as they occur.  The Company’s response, maintenance, and testing requirements are 
driven by regulatory requirements (e.g., North American Electric Reliability Corporation (NERC), 
Northeast Power Coordinating Council (NPCC)) and good utility practice to maintain the transmission 
and distribution system in a safe, efficient, and reliable condition for the workforce and customers. 
Substation’s responsibility regarding NERC standards has grown to over 100 requirements across 15 
standards.  Internal Company requirements dictate that over 1,000 pieces of equipment are maintained 


--- Page 404 ---
 
 
EXHIBIT ____ (EIOP-4) 
 
 
PAGE 8 OF 30 
8 
 
by Substation personnel on a rotating basis, equating to approximately 4,000 hours of work per year.  
These complex requirements and standards require a full time Senior Specialist to monitor and track 
the department’s compliance on an ongoing and routine basis for the safe and reliable operation of the 
Company’s electric transmission and distribution system.  During the same period, the department’s 
relay and equipment maintenance backlog has grown and is an area of concern.  This individual will 
manage this backlog and more efficiently manage maintenance activities to reduce and eventually 
eliminate this backlog.  
 
The two existing electrician supervisors have spans of control of 1:18 and 1:12.  The addition of one 
Electrician Supervisor will reduce the spans of control to more optimum levels (an average of 1:10) and 
allow for a more organized and productive approach to the department.  The addition of 
one Electrician Supervisor will also enable the Company to respond more efficiently to adverse 
weather and system emergencies.  This will increase the number of qualified individuals able to direct 
crews during storm emergencies.

Relationship to 5-Year and Long-Range Plans and Enterprise Risk Management Strategy 
The addition of an Electrician Supervisor will decrease the span of control to 1:10, thereby increasing 
productivity.   
The addition of a Senior Specialist – Compliance will dedicate an FTE to assist in meeting all regulatory 
and internal good utility practice maintenance requirements.

2. Supplemental Information 
 
Alternatives 
 
Alternative 1 description and reason for rejection 
Develop and maintain substation compliance with the current Substation staffing level:   
• 
Orange and Rockland does not recommend this alternative because the Substation compliance program 
continues to evolve due to both changing regulatory requirements and additional system equipment.  
Assigning these additional and evolving regulatory requirements to the same number of Substation Team 
members will increase the chance that a compliance requirement will be overlooked and not met, resulting in 
a possible safety or reliability incident and potential financial penalties to the Company.  
 
Alternative 2 description and reason for rejection 
Maintain substation supervision with the current Substation staffing level:   
• 
Orange and Rockland does not recommend this alternative as the Eastern Substation Supervisor is 
currently at a 1:18 span of control.  This leads to reduced productivity and a potential drift of focus away 
from safety.  
 
Risk of No Action 
 
Risk 1 
Not having a dedicated compliance specialist within the substation team will increase the chance that a 
compliance requirement will be overlooked and not met, resulting in a possible safety or reliability incident and 
potential financial penalties to the Company.   
 
Risk 2 
Not decreasing the span of control for our current Eastern Electrician Supervisor will lead to a loss of 
productivity and a potential drift of focus away from safety.  It will also result in a continuation of the existing 
work backlog. 
 
Non-Financial Benefits 
• 
Senior Specialist – Compliance 
Facilitating regulatory compliance 
• 
Supervisor, Electrician  
Reduced span of control  
Increased safety focus and job planning productivity.   
• 
Reducing the work backlog. 
 
Summary of Financial Benefits and Costs (attach backup) 
1. Cost-benefit analysis (if required) 
 
2. Major financial benefits 
 
3. Total cost 
Labor cost for Rate Year 1 is estimated at $120k with a split of 65% O&M, 35% Capital. Labor Cost for 
Rate Year 2 is $244k with a 3% salary increase for future years. Additionally, $63k for one vehicle 
(Capital) and $11k per year per vehicle for fuel and maintenance starting in Rate Year 1. 
 
4. Basis for estimate 
The estimate for labor costs is based on historical data for Band 2 Senior Specialists and Supervisors in 
the O&R Substations department. The Supervisor starts in Rate Year 1, and the Senior Specialist starts 
in Rate Year 2. The supervisor labor costs are split at 65% O&M and 35% Capital for all years.

Control Center and Substation Operations 
2022 
1. Project / Program Summary 
 
Type:  ☒ Project  ☒ Program  
Category:  ☒ Capital  ☒ O&M 
Work Plan Category:  ☐ Regulatory Mandated  ☒ Operationally Required  ☐ Strategic 
Project/Program Title: Four Vehicles for Incumbent Substation Electricians 
Project/Program Manager: Ralph DeVitto 
Project/Program Number (Level 1): 
Status:  ☒ Initiation  ☐ Planning  ☐ Execution  ☒ On-going  ☐  ☐ Other: ___________ 
Estimated Start Date: January 1, 2022

E. Total Funding Request ($000)

O&M: $317.9 
 
F.  
☐ 5-Year Gross Cost Savings ($000) 
☐ 5-Year Gross Cost Avoidance ($000)

G. 5-Year Ongoing Maintenance Expense ($000) 
O&M: $$317.9 
Capital: $344 (year 1 only) 
H. Investment Payback Period:   
(Years/months) (If applicable) 
Work Description: 
Orange and Rockland’s Substation Department is responsible for ninety substation facilities across the 
Company’s service territory.  Responsibilities include transmission/distribution equipment real time 
operation and maintenance, maintaining system reliability, emergency response, physical site/security 
maintenance, and regulatory compliance.   
  
The department’s Substation Electrician (L503) headcount has grown to thirty full time employee’s 
(FTEs).  Currently, the department shares twenty vehicles. Orange and Rockland is planning to 
increase this to 24 vehicles, as described below.  This increase will serve to better utilize qualified FTEs 
for recently negotiated ‘one-person tasks.’ The Company will also see an increase in productivity due 
to a more efficient dispatch of personnel over its geographically disperse service territory.   
 
Justification Summary: 
During the past three years, the Company’s total asset inventory has increased as Orange and 
Rockland has constructed two new substation facilities (i.e., Deerpark and Oak Street Substations) and 
added new equipment at seven existing stations/locations. These projects placed additional facilities, 
substation equipment and associated ancillary systems into service which has increased the 
department’s workload. This equipment requires routine periodic maintenance on time schedules 
ranging to six-year intervals. The department is also responsible for real time issues that arise at these 
facilities and for investigating/responding to equipment issues as they occur. The Company’s 
response, maintenance, and testing requirements are driven by regulatory requirements (e.g., North 
American Electric Reliability Corporation (NERC), Northeast Power Coordinating Council (NPCC)) 
and good utility practice to maintain the transmission and distribution system in a safe, efficient, and 
reliable condition for the workforce and customers.  
 


--- Page 409 ---
 
 
EXHIBIT ____ (EIOP-4) 
 
 
PAGE 13 OF 30 
13 
 
With the added workload associated with the additional substations and increasing regulatory 
requirements, the Company looks to increase productivity through efficient dispatch of crews. In 
particular, the Company seeks to maximize the dispatch of single employees to perform tasks that can 
be completed with just one person.   
 
The addition of four vehicles for our Substation Electricians will also enable the Company 
to respond more efficiently to adverse weather and system emergencies.  This will allow the Company 
to respond more expeditiously to a greater number of incidents during storm emergencies.     
 
Relationship to 5-Year and Long-Range Plans and Enterprise Risk Management Strategy 
The addition of four vehicles for our Substation Electricians will serve to better utilize qualified FTEs 
for recently negotiated ‘one-person tasks’. The Company will also see an increase in productivity due 
to a more efficient dispatch of personnel over its geographically disperse service territory.   
2. Supplemental Information 
 
Alternatives 
 
Alternative 1 description and reason for rejection 
Maintain vehicle inventory at the current level.   
• 
Orange and Rockland does not recommend this alternative because the Company has 
recently negotiated one-person tasks within the Substation Electrician job specifications. Having 
more than one qualified person in a vehicle does not maximize the utilization of the Company’s 
qualified employees’ time and thereby reduces productivity.   
 
Risk of No Action 
 
Risk 1 
The Company has recently negotiated one-person tasks within the Substation Electrician job 
specifications.  Having more than one qualified person in a vehicle does not maximize the utilization of 
the Company’s qualified employees’ time and thereby reduces productivity.   
 
Non-Financial Benefits 
• 
Increased reliability and customer satisfaction due to qualified employees’ reduced response time 
to system emergencies and storm conditions.  
• 
Increased efficiency due to an increase in productivity as individual qualified employees will be 
able to complete single-person tasks.  
• 
 
Summary of Financial Benefits and Costs (attach backup) 
1. Cost-benefit analysis (if required) 
 
2. Major financial benefits 
 
3. Total cost 
$86k per vehicle x 4 vehicles = Total $344k (Capital) 
$19k per year per vehicle for fuel and maintenance plus escalation - $317.9k (O&M) 
 
4. Basis for estimate 
The estimate for the vehicles cost is based on historical data.

Control Center and Substation Operations 
2023 
1. Project / Program Summary

Project/Program Title: Distribution System Operators Staffing in the Distribution Control Center

Work Description: 
 
The core responsibility of Orange and Rockland’s Distribution Control Center (DCC) is to monitor, 
control and operate the electric distribution system in a safe, reliable and compliant manner. Meeting 
this obligation requires that all maintenance, capital improvement, and emergency work that is 
performed on the electric distribution system, be planned and controlled through the DCC. It is 
imperative that the Distribution System Operators (DSOs), who are responsible for its operation, 
maintain a high level of situational awareness and focus on the core tasks of the DCC. The current 
primary DSOs activities include, but are not limited to, the following:  
• 
Providing lockout/tagout measures for the protection of the line worker and the public; 
• 
Monitoring and controlling all electric distribution system assets via Distribution Supervisory 
Control and Data Acquisition (DSCADA) and Supervisory Control and Data Acquisition 
(SCADA) systems; 
• 
Directing isolation of dangerous fault conditions on an emergent basis; 
• 
Reporting outages, injuries, vehicle accidents and other abnormal system conditions to 
regulatory entities; 
• 
Directing the fast restoration of outages; 
• 
Providing oversight for the DCC dispatchers; 
• 
Monitoring changing weather patterns and preparing for storms accordingly; 
• 
Conducting quality assurance checks of the DCC and Operations practices; 
• 
Conducting on-going procedural analysis; and 
• 
Completing development and training activities. 
 
The electric distribution system is becoming more complex and sophisticated with automated devices 
and the connection of an increasing number of Distributed Energy Resources (DERs). This has 
triggered an increasing workload and the need to reevaluate the staffing levels in the DCC. Presently, 


--- Page 413 ---
 
 
EXHIBIT ____ (EIOP-4) 
 
 
PAGE 17 OF 30 
17 
 
monitoring, controlling, and dispatching DERs are outside of the normal functions of the DSOs in the 
DCC. New skillsets and additional resources to monitor, manage and optimize the benefits provided 
by DERs on the electric distribution system, and to address the new risks to the reliability of the 
distribution system are immediate concerns that need to be addressed and must be strategically 
implemented for future expansion. Reverse power flow and voltage imbalances will become more 
prevalent on the system and coordination of these technologies with providers will be required. 
Additionally, in order to operate the distribution system more dynamically, the DSOs will need to 
analyze sensor inputs, coordinate load shifting, and monitor and control certain DERs that are having 
an impact upon the system.  DSOs will need to provide management and oversight to automated 
system-wide volt/var optimization.  
 
Justification Summary: 
 
The Company’s primary responsibility is to preserve distribution system safety, acceptable operating 
parameters, and reliability. With the increased integration of DERs and automation, the role of the 
DCC is even more critical in order to maximize the operational opportunities and mitigate risks.    
 
Since 2018, the Company has made significant strides installing automated field devices, which 
provide operators with real-time system information to recognize and remotely address proactively 
issues on the system, thereby reducing the reliance on customers to report incidents while improving 
public safety. Currently, 74 percent of the total circuits in Orange and Rockland’s service territory have 
distribution automation (DA) devices installed; approximately 40 percent of the total circuits are in 
auto-loop configurations thereby enhancing reliability. This equates to about 25 percent of the circuits 
being fully Smart Grid-ready (e.g., installation of motorized operated air break switches (MOABs), 
mid-point reclosers and auto-loops) with the current plan to complete an additional five percent of the 
system each year. The Company anticipates that the entire Orange and Rockland service territory will 
be Smart Grid ready by 2026 without the installation of Power Quality nodes and Smart Capacitors. 
 
Total Number of O&R Automated Devices*: 
 
* Data source is the Company’s DSIP Submission on June 30, 2020. Current line represents data as of that date.  
 
Orange and Rockland is continually reviewing opportunities to improve technology and operational 
efficiency. One initiative is the adoption of an Advanced Distribution Management System (ADMS) in 
2021, which will serve as the foundational platform to integrate multiple systems and provide real-
time data to enhance electric distribution system situational awareness, analysis, reliability, resiliency, 
and efficiency. An ADMS will expand the planning model of the Company’s electric distribution 
system into real-time operations. Coordinating through integrated systems and the external interfaces, 


--- Page 414 ---
 
 
EXHIBIT ____ (EIOP-4) 
 
 
PAGE 18 OF 30 
18 
 
ADMS will act in near real-time to adjust both Company and customer equipment, so as to achieve 
system states that maintain appropriate and efficient operating conditions. It will also provide the 
platform to realize volt/var optimization functionality that have the capability to improve system 
efficiency and reliability substantially through expansive implementation. ADMS will do this through 
its dynamic model of the electric delivery system and near real-time operations through SCADA 
feedback and control. It will have a near real-time reference to the current state electrical system, which 
will be the basis for analyzing and executing on appropriate future system states for switching plans 
and contingency situations. ADMS will be able to identify, monitor, and record data from abnormal 
system conditions resulting from planned and unplanned events that modify the design configuration 
of the electrical system.  
 
As the electric distribution system becomes more complex with the addition of automated devices and 
the growing DERs interconnections, the DCC needs to evolve and adapt to these changes such that the 
safety and integrity of the system is maintained. This is achievable with the addition of one new DSO 
in 2022. This DSO will review and establish operating procedures, service level agreements, 
troubleshoot and address any challenges with integrating ADMS in the DCC. In addition, this DSO 
will focus on monitoring, analyzing, and responding to the dynamic impact of DERs on the system.   
 
This DSO’s functions will include but are not limited to the following:  
• 
Real time monitoring, assessment, and response to the electric distribution system using 
ADMS; 
• 
Contingency analysis for planned and emergent work in response to reverse power flows, and 
voltage imbalances and produced by DERs on the system;  
• 
Generate contingency plans as needed; 
• 
Two to seven day look ahead analysis to determine optimal resource needs during normal and 
contingency operation, and operating reserves; and  
• 
Liaison between the Bulk Power Room and the DSOs on the desk to enhance system security.  
• 
Coordinate day ahead and real time commitments and dispatch of DERs on the electric 
distribution system 
 
Orange and Rockland’s FTE calculation is based on time study estimates to research, analyze, 
coordinate with distribution planning and mapping groups, and process switching orders. Currently, 
the DCC processes approximately 105 switch requests with DERs annually excluding daily protection 
requests for non-auto recloser switching. These requests vary in complexity depending on the 
sequence with transmission work, current equipment status, impact of DER on non-native circuits 
during contingency switching, and other outage restrictions. On average, it takes approximately six 
hours to process each request. There is a projected 134% increase in total solar photovoltaic (PV) 
connections between 2020 and 2026:

Relationship to 5-Year and Long-Range Plans and Enterprise Risk Management Strategy 
 
In 2014, New York State commenced its journey towards cleaner, more resilient and affordable energy 
delivery under the Reforming the Energy Vision (REV) initiative. In 2020, the State enacted the 
Accelerated Renewable Energy Growth and Community Benefit Act (Benefit Act) which establishes 
State organizations and processes to expedite the development of renewable energy in New York. The 
clean energy landscape in New York, while continuing to focus on widespread, small-scale DERs, is 
expanding to incorporate and prioritize advancing decarbonization through large scale resources such 
as offshore wind, utility scale solar, and utility scale energy storage systems. 
 
Orange and Rockland’s increase in staffing in the DCC supports the State’s REV initiative and the 
Company’s commitment to encourage and integrate DERs, smarter technologies and energy efficiency 
measures. The additional staffing in the DCC is paramount to facilitate reliability, optimization of new 
technology and increased operational efficiency.   
 
 
2. Supplemental Information 
 
Alternatives 
 
Alternative 1 description and reason for rejection 
The alternative is to maintain the current staffing level. In 2019, the DCC performed an estimated 
430,000 operations in the Oracle Network Management System (NMS), with approximately 22,000 of 
those being device operations. There has been an increase in operations primarily due to the adoption 
of new DA devices and this number is expected to increase. As of September 23, 2020, the DCC has 
performed 433,300 operations in NMS, with approximately 19,000 of those being device operations. 


--- Page 416 ---
 
 
EXHIBIT ____ (EIOP-4) 
 
 
PAGE 20 OF 30 
20 
 
The 2020 to date statistics include 3,843 device operations and 130,580 total operations that occurred 
the Company’s response to during Tropical Storm Isaias.

The DSOs must monitor, analyze, and respond to each of these switching operations. Another DSO is 
needed to respond to the increasing complexity of the electric distribution system and switching using 
ADMS during these routine and more frequent inclement weather conditions.  
 
Additionally, the Company has seen an increase in frequency of impactful storms from every four to 
five years to every two years and storm response is a critical function in the DCC. This additional DSO 
will be another resource available during the Company’s storm response efforts.  
 
Risk of No Action 
 
Risk 1 
The risk of compromised safety, prolonged outages, operating incidents, and missed regulatory 
reporting requirements are of concern with the current staffing levels and with the growing number of 
DER interconnections.  
 
Non-Financial Benefits 
 
By augmenting the DCC’s DSO staffing level, the DCC will seek to optimize customer satisfaction, 
meet existing compliance and evolving regulatory REV requirements, increase situational awareness 
(safety, and reliability), and maintain more efficient workflows and communication among 
departments. Operators are able to recognize and respond to system issues and begin to mitigate the 
problem well before customers or emergency workers can report the problem. This improves the safety 
of addressing dangerous situations, such as wires down, outages to be reduced to fewer customers 
quickly, and allows the restoration worker to arrive at the right location, repair the damage, and 
restore the system quicker.

Control Center and Substation Operations 
2021 
1. Project / Program Summary 
 
Type:  ☒ Project  ☐ Program  
Category:  ☒ Capital  ☐ O&M 
Work Plan Category:  ☐ Regulatory Mandated  ☒ Operationally Required  ☐ Strategic 
Project/Program Title: Ladentown Breaker Replacements 
Project/Program Manager: Ralph DeVitto 
Project/Program Number (Level 1): 
Status:  ☐ Initiation  ☐ Planning  ☒ Execution  ☐ On-going  ☐  ☐ Other: ___________ 
Estimated Start Date: 1/1/21 
Estimated Date In Service: 12/1/23 
I. 
Total Funding Request ($000)  
Capital: $1,665 
O&M:  
 
J. 
 
☐ 5-Year Gross Cost Savings ($000) 
☐ 5-Year Gross Cost Avoidance ($000)

K. 5-Year Ongoing Maintenance Expense ($000)

Capital: 
L. Investment Payback Period:   
(Years/months) (If applicable) 
Work Description: 
The Ladentown Substation presently has four 345 kV circuit breakers in service (1-56-2, 3-56-2, 4-56-2, 
and 6-56-2) which are Delle Alsthom, air, type PK-4C, rated 362 kV, 3000 amperes continuous current, 
and 63 kA short circuit interrupting current circuit breakers. The purpose of this project is to replace all 
four breakers with new ABB 345 kV SF6 breakers, with the following characteristics:  
a. Breaker Type ABB 362PMR63-30B SF6;  
b. Rated Voltage 362 kV (Nominal);  
c. BIL 1300 kV; 
d. Rated Current 3000 amperes; and  
e. Short Circuit Interrupting Rating 63 kA (Symmetrical). 
 
 
Justification Summary: 
Ladentown Substation is co-owned by Con Edison and Orange and Rockland, with Con Edison being 
the majority owner. All costs associated with this station are split according to the ownership 
percentages. Con Edison is responsible for engineering in this station and, therefore, has the lead in 
identifying and initiating this project. The cost of this project, as presented here, reflects solely Orange 
and Rockland’s portion of the entire cost for the replacement of these four breakers.  
 
The Con Edison High Voltage Circuit Breaker Capital Program is an on-going program that targets the 
replacement of 33 138 kV, and 345 kV circuit breakers with a history of poor performance, 
environmental concerns, in-service failures, and those which are uneconomical to maintain. 
Circuit breakers are identified for replacement when the health of a breaker is considered low as 
identified by the EPRI maintenance ranking program and the Con Edison Circuit Breaker Peer Team 
review, as directed by CE-ES-1000. 
 


--- Page 420 ---
 
 
EXHIBIT ____ (EIOP-4) 
 
 
PAGE 24 OF 30 
24 
 
The 345 kV Delle Alsthom type PK4C circuit breakers at Ladentown have an overhaul cost that is 
greater than replacing the breakers.  Also, the success rate is low for reducing leaks after an overhaul of 
PK4C type breakers. The Con Edison Circuit Breaker Peer Team identified the four breakers at the 
Ladentown 345 kV substation for replacement. These 345 kV breakers are 1-56-2, 3-56-2, 4-56-2, and 6-
56-2. These breakers are planned for replacement from 2021 to 2023. 
 
 
Relationship to 5-Year and Long-Range Plans and Enterprise Risk Management Strategy 
Delle Alsthom type PK4C circuit breakers are expensive to maintain and the overhaul cost is more 
expensive than the cost for replacement. 
 
The four circuit breakers were soon to be scheduled for major overhaul. The Con Edison Circuit 
Breaker Peer Team recommended replacement rather than overhaul because of its limited success in 
decreasing leaks and the large expense. Therefore, the replacement option is the proposed plan. The 
replacement breakers will be SF6 operated 345 kV, 3000 amp continuous current, 63 kA interrupting 
current, which is standard for the recently purchased fleet of 345 kV breakers on the Con Edison 
system. 
 
 
2. Supplemental Information 
 
Alternatives 
 
Alternative 1 description and reason for rejection 
Keep the circuit breakers in service until failure and then replace the equipment on an emergency 
basis. Orange and Rockland does not recommend this alternative because of reliability, system 
performance, environmental, safety, and cost concerns. 
 
Alternative 2 description and reason for rejection 
Overhaul the circuit breakers in an attempt to restore them to Original Equipment Manufacturer 
(OEM) condition. Orange and Rockland does not recommended this method because the overhaul cost 
is greater than replacing the breakers.  In addition, OEM parts are no longer available and replacement 
parts do not guarantee OEM specifications and reliability. 
 
Risk of No Action 
 
Risk 1 
System reliability, system performance, environmental and safety risks all exist with the current 
breakers in service.   
 
Risk 2 
Upon failure of the current breakers, OEM parts are not readily available and are costly. This will 
increase the risk to system reliability.  
 
Non-Financial Benefits 
• 
Increased safety and reliability.

Summary of Financial Benefits and Costs (attach backup)

2. Major financial benefits 
 
3. Total cost 
Total cost for O&R is $2.44M over three years.  
 
4. Basis for estimate 
 
The cost estimate is based on vendor quotes for materials and on historical timelines for similar 
breaker replacements for labor costs. 
 
5. Conclusion 
Replacement of the Circuit Breaker is recommended because it is the most cost efficient way to achieve 
the desired level of reliability and life cycle cost.

1. Project / Program Summary

Project/Program Title: Systems Specialist for O&R CIP Admin Team 
Project/Program Manager: Sarah Ambrose 
Project/Program Number (Level 1): 
Status:  ☒ Initiation  ☐ Planning  ☐ Execution  ☐ On-going  ☐  ☐ Other: ___________

N. Investment Payback Period:   
(Years/months) (If applicable) 
Work Description: 
 
The Systems Specialist position that Orange and Rockland is planning to hire would work in Orange 
and Rockland’s Critical Infrastructure Program (CIP) Admin Team and would be responsible for the 
following: 
• 
Monitor and maintain compliance with North American Electric Reliability Corporation 
(NERC) CIP Standards and other Cyber Security Standards applicable to Orange and 
Rockland; 
• 
Continue to develop and implement Orange and Rockland’s CIP Program in the Energy 
Control Center (ECC) and Substation Operations; and 
• 
Engage in activities and applications including patch management of Control Center 
equipment, anti-virus updates/upgrades, electronic access and physical security systems and 
protocols, intrusion detection, baseline monitoring, and other systems administration 
functions. 
 
Justification Summary: 
 
The Energy Sector has seen a large surge in cyber security risks and associated cyber security 
compliance measures over the last five years. This has resulted in an increasing number of NERC CIP 
Standards that the Company is required to comply with in the ECC and in Substation Operations. The 
following lists the on-going expansion of existing CIP standards and the adoption of two entirely new 
CIP Standards recently approved by the Federal Regulator Energy Commission (FERC).  
• 
Expansion of Existing Standards: 
o 
NERC CIP-010-3 R1.6, Configuration Change Management, effective 10/1/2020; 
o 
NERC CIP-005-6 R2.4, R2.5, Electronic Security Perimeters, effective 10/1/2020; 
and 


--- Page 424 ---
 
 
EXHIBIT ____ (EIOP-4) 
 
 
PAGE 28 OF 30 
28 
 
o 
NERC CIP-008-6 R4, Incident Reporting and Response Planning effective 
1/1/2021. 
• 
Adoption of New Standards:  
o 
NERC CIP-013-1, Supply Chain Risk Management, effective 10/1/2020; and 
o 
NERC CIP-012-1, Communications Between Control Centers, effective 7/1/2022. 
 
The new System Specialist would be responsible for the Company’s compliance with these new 
obligations. 
 
In addition to NERC CIP, there are multiple federal initiatives that are driving increasing activities in 
the cyber security and supply chain arena. This new System Specialist position will aid the Company 
in responding to labor intensive data requests and implementing new requirements that result from 
the executive order on this topic that was issued May 1, 2020. New Department of Energy (DOE) 
guidance is expected to be issued shortly. 
 
 
Relationship to 5-Year and Long-Range Plans and Enterprise Risk Management Strategy 
 
As identified in Risk 1 below, currently there are too many responsibilities assigned to each individual 
of the Orange and Rockland CIP Admin Team.  The addition of a Systems Specialist will allow the 
Orange and Rockland CIP Admin Team to allocate responsibility for NERC CIP and other Cyber 
Security Standard responsibilities more efficiently and thereby reduce the “single point of failure” risk, 
identified in Risk 1 below. 
 
The addition of a Systems Specialist to the Orange and Rockland CIP Admin Team will reduce the 
likelihood of both a cyber-security incident and the likelihood of a non-compliance situation and the 
associated risk of a financial penalty incurred by the Company. 
 
2. Supplemental Information 
 
Alternatives 
 
Alternative 1 description and reason for rejection 
Develop and maintain NERC CIP compliance with the current Orange and Rockland CIP Admin Team 
staffing level:  
• 
Orange and Rockland does not recommend this alternative because the NERC CIP 
requirements continue to expand. Orange and Rockland having to assigning these additional 
cyber security requirements to the existing Orange and Rockland CIP Admin Team members 
will increase the chance that a compliance requirement may be overlooked and not met, 
resulting in potential cyber security events and/or financial penalties to the Company. 
 
Risk of No Action 
 
Risk 1 
A risk of Orange and Rockland maintaining compliance with the continuous expansion of the NERC 
CIP requirements with the current staffing levels of the Orange and Rockland CIP Admin Team could 
result in a single point of failure and lead to cyber security events and/or compliance issues. This 
concern was identified as a ‘Recommendation’ by Northeast Power Coordinating Council (NPCC) 
during the 2016 NPCC Audit. The same concern was again identified and elevated to an ‘Area of 
Concern’ by NPCC during the 2019 NPCC Audit.

Work Description: 
 
This Vegetation Management Program enhancement is to expand the current Hazard Tree Removal 
Program.  Currently, there are two nearly full-time crews completing Hazard Tree Removals.  This 
program enhancement is to expand the crew complement completing this work to eight full-time 
crews (i.e., add six additional full-time crews).  The Hazard Tree Removal Program has been effective 
in culling hazardous trees in proximity to energized conductors, and the public and municipal 
reception to this initiative has been very positive. 
 
Justification Summary: 
 
The Hazard Tree Removal Program has been effective in culling hazardous trees in proximity to 
energized conductors, and the public and municipal reception to this initiative has been very positive. 
The proposed enhancement will allow a continuation of the Company’s storm hardening efforts, as 
well as provide additional support to address the impact of the Emerald Ash Borer on the ash tree 
population. 
 
 
Relationship to 5-Year and Long-Range Plans and Enterprise Risk Management Strategy

Non-Financial Benefits 
 
The Hazard Tree Removal Program has been effective in culling hazardous trees in proximity to 
energized conductors, and the public and municipal reception to this initiative has been very positive.  
The proposed enhancement will improve the safety and performance of the electric system, thereby 
improving the Company’s relationship with its customers.

5. Conclusion 
 
The Hazard Tree Removal Program is an existing program and is in need of further enhancement and 
support.   Although limited, the program has been effective in culling hazardous trees in proximity to 
energized conductors, and the public and municipal reception to this initiative has been very positive.  
The proposed enhancement will improve the safety and performance of the electric system, thereby 
improving the Company’s relationship with its customers.  It will also provide benefits during weather 
events by reducing the number of tree related outages on the distribution system.
